Data types & operators · 14
DS-U1-Q01MCQeasy

Compiled with a C99-or-later compiler on a machine where int is 32 bits, what does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { printf("%d %d\n", 7 / 2 * 2, -7 / 2); return 0; }

  1. 6 -3
  2. 7 -3
  3. 6 -4
  4. 7 -4
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In C99 and later, integer division truncates towards zero, so -7 / 2 is -3, not -4. Multiplication and division have equal precedence and associate left to right.

Step by step.

  1. 7 / 2 is integer division: both operands are int, so the result is 3.
  2. Then 3 * 2 gives 6 — the multiply happens after the division because of left-to-right associativity.
  3. -7 / 2 discards the fractional part towards zero: the exact quotient is -3.5, truncated to -3.

Final answer. 6 -3

Common trap. Python and many pocket calculators use floor division, where -7 / 2 is -4. C truncates towards zero, so the second value is -3. The first trap is reading 7 / 2 * 2 as 7 / (2 * 2) = 1 or as floating point 7.

DS-U1-Q02MCQeasy

On an implementation where sizeof(int) is 4, what is the value of sizeof('A') in C?

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 4
  4. It depends on the character encoding used by the compiler
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In C a character constant such as 'A' has type int, not char. Its size is therefore the size of an int.

Step by step.

  1. The literal 'A' is a character constant whose type is int (C11 §6.4.4.4).
  2. No conversion to char takes place inside sizeof, because the operand of sizeof is not evaluated or converted to a narrower type.
  3. Hence sizeof('A') == sizeof(int) == 4.

Final answer. 4

Common trap. This is the classic C-versus-C++ confusion: in C++ 'A' has type char and the answer would be 1. The character encoding never changes the size of an int.

DS-U1-Q03MCQeasy

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { int a = 6, b = 3; printf("%d\n", a ^ b | a & b); return 0; }

  1. 7
  2. 5
  3. 2
  4. 1
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Bitwise AND binds tighter than XOR, which binds tighter than OR, so the expression groups as (a ^ b) | (a & b).

Step by step.

  1. a & b with a = 6 (110₂) and b = 3 (011₂) gives 010₂ = 2.
  2. a ^ b gives 101₂ = 5.
  3. 5 | 2 gives 101₂ | 010₂ = 111₂ = 7.

Final answer. 7

Common trap. Reading the expression left to right and computing (6 ^ 3) | 6 first, or assuming all three bitwise operators share precedence. The precedence order is & above ^ above |. Option 5 comes from evaluating only a ^ b and stopping.

DS-U1-Q04MCQmedium

What does this program print, assuming CHAR_BIT == 8?

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { unsigned char c = 250; c = c + 10; printf("%u\n", c); return 0; }

  1. 260
  2. 4
  3. 0
  4. 10
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Unsigned arithmetic is performed modulo 2ⁿ for an n-bit type. Assignment to an unsigned type reduces the value modulo 2ⁿ; it never traps and never wraps into a negative number.

Step by step.

  1. In c + 10 the unsigned char is promoted to int, so the sum is the ordinary integer 260.
  2. The assignment back to unsigned char converts the value modulo 2⁸ = 256: 260 mod 256 = 4.
  3. So c holds 4 and %u prints 4.

Final answer. 4

Common trap. Answering 260 ignores the conversion on assignment; answering 0 assumes the value wraps to a signed negative and is then read as unsigned. Neither happens — the reduction is modulo 256 and the result is 4.

DS-U1-Q05MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the standard C integer types are TRUE?

  1. sizeof(short) <= sizeof(int) on every conforming implementation
  2. sizeof(char) is 1 on every conforming implementation
  3. Signed integer overflow is defined to wrap around, exactly as it does for unsigned types
  4. Plain char is signed on every conforming implementation
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The C standard fixes minimum ranges and a rank order for the integer types, but leaves the size of most of them to the implementation. It also leaves signed overflow undefined and the signedness of plain char implementation-defined.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: the standard requires sizeof(short) <= sizeof(int) <= sizeof(long), so a short can never be larger than an int.
  2. (B) is true: sizeof(char) == 1 by definition — a byte in C is exactly one char, whatever its bit width.
  3. (C) is false: unsigned arithmetic is defined modulo 2ⁿ, but signed overflow is undefined behaviour. An optimiser may assume it never happens.
  4. (D) is false: whether plain char behaves as signed char or unsigned char is implementation-defined; only signed char and unsigned char are distinct types with fixed signedness.

Final answer. Options (A) and (B)

Common trap. The tempting error is (C): students see unsigned wrap-around and assume signed types do the same. They do not — signed overflow is undefined, and a program that relies on it can behave differently at -O0 and -O2. Option (D) is the other classic: char is not guaranteed signed.

DS-U1-Q06MCQmedium

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { int x, y = 2; x = (y += 3, y * 2); printf("%d\n", x); return 0; }

  1. 5
  2. 10
  3. 8
  4. 2
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The comma operator evaluates its left operand, discards that value, then evaluates the right operand and yields its value. The parentheses make the whole comma expression the right-hand side of the assignment to x.

Step by step.

  1. The left operand y += 3 runs first: y becomes 2 + 3 = 5; its value 5 is discarded.
  2. The right operand y * 2 is then evaluated with the updated y: 5 * 2 = 10.
  3. The comma expression yields 10, which is assigned to x.

Final answer. 10

Common trap. Two traps. First, forgetting that the left operand has already changed y, so the second operand uses 5 rather than 2 — that gives the distractor 4 or 5. Second, reading the comma as a declaration separator; inside an expression it is the comma operator. Note also that int x, y = 2; initialises only y.

DS-U1-Q07NAThard

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { unsigned char c = 0xF0; int r = c << 4; printf("%d\n", r); return 0; }

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a shift expression the operands are promoted to int first (the usual arithmetic conversions), so the left shift happens on a 32-bit value, not on an 8-bit one. The result of c << 4 is therefore not reduced modulo 256.

Step by step.

  1. c holds 0xF0 = 240.
  2. Integer promotion converts c to the int value 240 before the shift.
  3. 240 << 4 = 240 × 16 = 3840, which is 0xF00 and still fits in a 32-bit int.
  4. The assignment to r is a plain int assignment, so no truncation occurs and %d prints 3840.

Final answer. 3840

Common trap. The classic error is to keep the value 8 bits wide and answer 0: 0xF0 << 4 truncated to 8 bits is 0x00. That would be true only if the shift were performed in unsigned char arithmetic, which C does not do. Remember that shifts do not truncate to the left operand's type.

DS-U1-Q26MCQeasy

On an implementation where int is 4 bytes and float is 4 bytes, what is the value of sizeof(1.0f + 1)?

  1. 1
  2. 4
  3. 8
  4. It cannot be determined without knowing the value of 1.0f + 1.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The type of an arithmetic expression is fixed by the usual arithmetic conversions, and sizeof reports the size of that type, not of a computed value.

Step by step.

  1. 1.0f has type float and 1 has type int.
  2. The usual arithmetic conversions convert the int operand to float, so the sum has type float.
  3. There is no double in the expression: a float stays float unless a double operand forces promotion.
  4. sizeof(float) is 4 on this implementation, so the value is 4.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Assuming that any floating-point expression is evaluated in double and answering 8. Only an unsuffixed literal such as 1.0 is a double; 1.0f is not. A second trap is thinking sizeof evaluates the operand — it does not, which is why the value of the sum is irrelevant.

DS-U1-Q27MCQeasy

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { int a = 017, b = 0x1F; printf("%d %d\n", a, b); return 0; }

  1. 15 31
  2. 17 31
  3. 15 1F
  4. 017 31
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The base of an integer literal is set by its prefix: a leading 0 means octal, 0x means hexadecimal, and no prefix means decimal.

Step by step.

  1. 017 is octal: 1·8 + 7 = 15.
  2. 0x1F is hexadecimal: 1·16 + 15 = 31.
  3. Both initialisers store the integer values 15 and 31; the base affects only how the source text is read.
  4. %d prints them in decimal, so the output is 15 31.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Reading 017 as the decimal seventeen. The second trap is expecting %d to echo the hexadecimal digits — to see 1f you must print with %x.

DS-U1-Q28MCQmedium

On a machine where int is 32 bits, what does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { int i = -1; unsigned int u = 1; printf("%d\n", i < u); return 0; }

  1. 1
  2. 0
  3. -1
  4. The behaviour is undefined
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. When an int meets an unsigned int of the same rank, the usual arithmetic conversions turn the int into unsigned int before the comparison.

Step by step.

  1. The conversion of a negative value to unsigned is defined as arithmetic modulo 2³², so -1 becomes 4294967295u.
  2. The comparison is therefore 4294967295u < 1u, which is false.
  3. A relational operator yields int 0 or 1, so the value passed to %d is 0.
  4. Hence the program prints 0.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Reasoning in signed arithmetic, where −1 < 1 is true, and answering 1. This is the mirror image of the classic -1 < sizeof(x) trap: an unsigned operand in a mixed expression silently reinterprets the signed value.

DS-U1-Q29MCQmedium

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { int x = 1; x <<= 3; x |= 1; x ^= 5; printf("%d\n", x); return 0; }

  1. 8
  2. 9
  3. 12
  4. 13
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Compound assignment operators apply one bitwise operation at a time, in the order written, and each one assigns the result back to the variable.

Step by step.

  1. x = 1, i.e. binary 0001.
  2. x <<= 3 shifts left by three: 1000, so x = 8.
  3. x |= 1 sets the low bit: 1001, so x = 9.
  4. x ^= 5 is 1001 XOR 0101 = 1100, so x = 12.

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Using | and ^ in the wrong order, or replacing the XOR by an OR, which gives 13. Bit 3 survives the OR and is cleared by the XOR because 5 has that bit set.

DS-U1-Q30MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about C integer and character constants are TRUE?

  1. The integer literal 10 has type int.
  2. The hexadecimal literal 0x1F has the decimal value 31.
  3. An integer literal that begins with 0 is octal, so 011 equals 9.
  4. A character constant such as 'a' has type char.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Literal type is determined by the literal's form and by the value it must hold; character constants are the one constant form whose type surprises most candidates.

Step by step.

  1. (A) An unsuffixed decimal literal whose value fits in int has type int. TRUE.
  2. (B) 0x1F = 1·16 + 15 = 31. TRUE.
  3. (C) A leading zero means octal, and 011 octal = 1·8 + 1 = 9. TRUE.
  4. (D) In C a character constant has type int, not char — which is why sizeof('a') equals sizeof(int). FALSE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Option (D) is the standard error, imported from C++, where character constants do have type char. In C the value of 'a' is an int holding the execution character set code.

DS-U1-Q31NATeasy

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { printf("%d\n", (5 > 3) + (2 == 2) + (1 != 1)); return 0; }

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Relational and equality operators yield the int values 1 and 0, so their results can be added like any other integers.

Step by step.

  1. 5 > 3 is true, giving 1.
  2. 2 == 2 is true, giving 1.
  3. 1 != 1 is false, giving 0.
  4. The sum is 1 + 1 + 0 = 2.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Believing comparison results cannot be used in arithmetic, or assuming they are of type _Bool and therefore print differently. In C the result of a comparison is a plain int.

DS-U1-Q32NAThard

On a machine where unsigned int is 32 bits, what does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { unsigned int a = 3000000000u; printf("%u\n", a * 2u); return 0; }

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Unsigned arithmetic is performed modulo 2ⁿ, so a result that overflows wraps around rather than invoking undefined behaviour.

Step by step.

  1. The exact product is 3000000000 × 2 = 6000000000.
  2. The modulus for 32-bit unsigned int is 2³² = 4294967296.
  3. 6000000000 − 4294967296 = 1705032704, which is still below the modulus, so one subtraction suffices.
  4. %u prints that wrapped value.

Answer: 1705032704

Common misconception. Answering 6000000000, or assuming the wrap-around is undefined behaviour. Wrapping is guaranteed for unsigned types only; the same computation on a signed int would indeed be undefined.

Control structures · 12
DS-U1-Q08MCQeasy

What does this program print? (It compiles; the compiler only warns.)

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { int x = 0; if (x = 0) printf("A\n"); else printf("B\n"); return 0; }

  1. A
  2. B
  3. Nothing is printed
  4. It does not compile
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The condition of an if is any scalar expression; here it is an assignment expression, whose value is the value stored. Storing 0 makes the condition false.

Step by step.

  1. x = 0 assigns 0 to x and yields the value 0.
  2. A zero value is false, so the else branch runs.
  3. The program prints B.

Final answer. B

Common trap. Reading x = 0 as the comparison x == 0. That comparison would be true and print A. This is why good compilers warn about an assignment used as a condition. With x = 1 the program would print A.

DS-U1-Q09MCQmedium

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { int i, j; for (i = 0, j = 10; i < j; i++, j--) ; printf("%d %d\n", i, j); return 0; }

  1. 4 6
  2. 5 5
  3. 6 4
  4. 10 0
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A for header has three parts separated by semicolons: initialisation, condition and update. The update here is a single comma expression, so both i++ and j-- run at the end of every iteration.

Step by step.

  1. Start: i = 0, j = 10; condition 0 < 10 true.
  2. After each iteration both counters move: (1, 9), (2, 8), (3, 7), (4, 6).
  3. At (5, 5) the condition 5 < 5 is false, so the loop stops with i = 5 and j = 5.

Final answer. 5 5

Common trap. Stopping at 4 6 — that is the state at the start of the last successful iteration, not the state after the failing test. The update clause always runs before the condition is re-tested, so the counters meet at 5.

DS-U1-Q10NATeasy

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { int c = 0; for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) for (int j = i; j < 5; j++) c++; printf("%d\n", c); return 0; }

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The inner loop starts at j = i, so it runs 5 - i times for each value of i. The total is the sum of an arithmetic series.

Step by step.

  1. For i = 0, 1, 2, 3, 4 the inner loop runs 5, 4, 3, 2, 1 times.
  2. Total: 5 + 4 + 3 + 2 + 1 = 15.
  3. Equivalently n(n + 1)/2 with n = 5, i.e. the number of pairs (i, j) with 0 ≤ i ≤ j < 5.

Final answer. 15

Common trap. Assuming both loops always run 5 times and answering 25, or counting only the strictly upper-triangular pairs (10) by starting the inner loop at j = i + 1. The condition is j < 5 with j starting at i, so the diagonal is included.

DS-U1-Q11MCQmedium

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { int x = 2; switch (x) { case 1: printf("one"); case 2: printf("two"); case 3: printf("three"); break; default: printf("other"); } return 0; }

  1. two
  2. twothree
  3. onetwothree
  4. twoother
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A switch transfers control to the matching case label and then simply falls through the following statements until a break or the end of the block. Labels do not act as implicit exits.

Step by step.

  1. The controlling value is 2, so control jumps to case 2:.
  2. The statement printf("two") runs.
  3. There is no break after it, so execution continues into case 3: and prints three.
  4. The break after case 3: ends the switch, so the default is never reached.

Final answer. twothree

Common trap. Assuming each case is exclusive, as in an if-else chain, and answering two. Fall-through is the default in C; a break is required at the end of every case you want to end. Note also that the default is skipped here only because a break intervened.

DS-U1-Q12MSQmedium

On a typical 32-bit implementation, which of these loops TERMINATE?

  1. for (unsigned int i = 10; i >= 0; i--) { }
  2. int i = 0; while (i < 5) i += 2;
  3. int i = 0; do { i++; } while (i < 0);
  4. for (int i = 0; i != 10; i += 3) { }
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A loop terminates when its condition eventually becomes false. For unsigned counters and for equality tests with a stride that does not divide the gap, the condition can be permanently true.

Step by step.

  1. (A) An unsigned int is never negative, so i >= 0 is always true. When i is 0 the decrement wraps to 4294967295 and the loop never ends.
  2. (B) i takes 0, 2, 4 and then 6, at which point 6 < 5 is false. It terminates.
  3. (C) The body runs once (i becomes 1) and then 1 < 0 is false. A do-while always executes its body at least once. It terminates.
  4. (D) i takes 0, 3, 6, 9, 12, … and never equals 10, so the test is never false. It does not terminate.

Final answer. Options (B) and (C)

Common trap. Option (A) is the trap: the loop looks exactly like a signed countdown that ends at 0, but on an unsigned type the final decrement wraps instead of going negative. Option (D) shows the second trap — != is safe only when the stride divides the target.

DS-U1-Q13MCQhard

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { int s = 0; for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) { switch (i) { case 0: continue; case 1: s += 1; case 2: s += 2; break; case 3: s += 4; default: s += 8; } s += 100; } printf("%d\n", s); return 0; }

  1. 425
  2. 325
  3. 417
  4. 405
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Inside a switch that is inside a loop, continue belongs to the loop: it abandons the rest of the switch and the rest of the loop body, and jumps to the loop's update step. Fall-through still applies between case labels.

Step by step.

  1. i = 0: case 0 executes continue, so neither the switch nor the trailing s += 100 runs. s = 0.
  2. i = 1: case 1 adds 1, falls into case 2 and adds 2, then break exits the switch. s += 100 runs: s = 3 + 100 = 103.
  3. i = 2: case 2 adds 2 and breaks, then s += 100: s = 105 + 100 = 205.
  4. i = 3: case 3 adds 4 and falls through into default, adding 8: s = 209 + 8 = 217; then s += 100 gives 317.
  5. i = 4: default adds 8: s = 325; then s += 100 gives 425.

Final answer. 425

Common trap. Two mistakes produce the distractors. Treating continue as break — that is, letting the loop carry on to s += 100 for i = 0 — gives 325. Forgetting the fall-through from case 1 into case 2, or from case 3 into default, gives 417 or 405. Note that in C, unlike Java, continue inside a switch acts on the enclosing loop.

DS-U1-Q33MCQeasy

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { int i = 0, s = 0; while (i < 6) { i++; if (i % 3 == 0) continue; s += i; } printf("%d\n", s); return 0; }

  1. 9
  2. 10
  3. 12
  4. 15
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. continue abandons the rest of the loop body and re-tests the condition; it does not skip the update that already happened.

Step by step.

  1. i is incremented before the divisibility test, so no iteration is lost.
  2. Values 1 and 2 are added: s = 3.
  3. 3 is a multiple of 3, so continue skips the addition.
  4. 4 and 5 are added: s = 12; 6 is skipped; the loop then ends.

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Putting the increment after the continue would have made this an infinite loop, so the increment must come first. A second trap is adding 3 and 6 as well, which gives 21, or omitting 5, which gives 10.

DS-U1-Q34MCQmedium

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { int i, j, c = 0; for (i = 0; i < 3; i++) for (j = 0; j < 3; j++) { if (j == i) break; c++; } printf("%d\n", c); return 0; }

  1. 2
  2. 3
  3. 6
  4. 9
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. break leaves only the innermost enclosing loop or switch, and the outer loop then continues with its next iteration.

Step by step.

  1. For i = 0 the very first inner test j == i breaks out, adding nothing.
  2. For i = 1: j = 0 counts, then j = 1 breaks — one addition.
  3. For i = 2: j = 0 and j = 1 count, then j = 2 breaks — two additions.
  4. Total 0 + 1 + 2 = 3.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Assuming break escapes both loops, which would give 0, or assuming it only skips one iteration, which would give 6. Note that the inner loop is re-entered with j reset to 0 each time.

DS-U1-Q35MCQmedium

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { int x = 3, r = 0; switch (x) { default: r += 1; case 1: r += 2; break; case 3: r += 4; } printf("%d\n", r); return 0; }

  1. 1
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 7
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A switch jumps to the matching case label only; default is selected solely when no case matches, and execution then falls through the labels below the entry point.

Step by step.

  1. x is 3, so control jumps to case 3; the default label above it is skipped.
  2. r += 4 makes r = 4.
  3. There is no statement after case 3, so the switch ends; the fall-through into case 1 never happens because labels are not re-entered backwards.
  4. The program prints 4.

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Two traps. First, believing default runs before the matching case because it is written first. Second, expecting the fall-through to wrap around to case 1, which would give 7. Fall-through is always downward.

DS-U1-Q36MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about C loop and switch control are TRUE?

  1. The body of a do-while loop executes at least once, even if its condition is false on entry.
  2. In a for statement all three expressions must be present.
  3. A break inside a switch terminates only that switch, not an enclosing loop.
  4. A continue inside a while loop transfers control to the loop condition test.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each loop form fixes where control resumes on continue, and break always binds to the nearest enclosing loop or switch.

Step by step.

  1. (A) A do-while tests its condition after the body, so the body runs once even when the condition is false. TRUE.
  2. (B) All three parts may be omitted — for (;;) is a legal infinite loop, and for (i = 0; i < n;) is legal too. FALSE.
  3. (C) break leaves the nearest enclosing loop or switch; inside a switch it does not touch an outer loop. TRUE.
  4. (D) In a while loop continue jumps to the condition test (in a for loop it jumps to the increment expression instead). TRUE.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Common misconception. Option (B) is the usual error, and it hides a real hazard: with a continue inside a for loop the increment still runs, but inside the equivalent while loop it does not, which can turn the loop infinite.

DS-U1-Q37NATeasy

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { int n = 10, c = 0; do { n -= 3; c++; } while (n > 0); printf("%d\n", c); return 0; }

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A do-while checks its condition after each iteration, so the body always runs at least once and the loop stops only when the test fails.

Step by step.

  1. After iteration 1, n = 7 and c = 1.
  2. After iteration 2, n = 4 and c = 2.
  3. After iteration 3, n = 1 and c = 3; 1 > 0, so the loop continues.
  4. After iteration 4, n = -2 and c = 4; the test fails and the loop ends.

Answer: 4

Common misconception. Stopping at 3 because n reached 1 and 'looks finished', or running a fifth iteration. The test happens after the subtraction, so the negative value is reached but not iterated again.

DS-U1-Q38NAThard

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int main(void) { int c = 0; for (int i = 1; i <= 10; i++) for (int j = i; j <= 10; j += 2) c++; printf("%d\n", c); return 0; }

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a nested loop whose inner index starts at the outer index and advances with a fixed stride, the inner trip count depends on the parity of i, not on a single fixed number.

Step by step.

  1. The inner loop visits j = i, i+2, i+4, … up to 10.
  2. For odd i (1, 3, 5, 7, 9) the trip counts are 5, 4, 3, 2, 1 — odd values reach 9 at most.
  3. For even i (2, 4, 6, 8, 10) the trip counts are 5, 4, 3, 2, 1 — even values reach 10.
  4. Sum: (5+4+3+2+1) + (5+4+3+2+1) = 15 + 15 = 30.

Answer: 30

Common misconception. Multiplying 10 by an average of 5 and answering 50, or assuming every outer iteration runs the inner loop five times. The stride of 2 makes the count fall by one every second value of i.

Functions & parameter passing · 12
DS-U1-Q14MCQeasy

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> void swap(int a, int b) { int t = a; a = b; b = t; } int main(void) { int x = 1, y = 2; swap(x, y); printf("%d %d\n", x, y); return 0; }

  1. 1 2
  2. 2 1
  3. 2 2
  4. 1 1
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. C has only call by value. The parameters a and b are fresh objects inside swap, initialised with copies of the arguments, so swapping them changes nothing in the caller.

Step by step.

  1. The call copies x into a and y into b.
  2. The body exchanges the copies: a = 2, b = 1.
  3. On return those copies are destroyed; x and y are untouched.
  4. The program prints 1 2.

Final answer. 1 2

Common trap. Expecting the swap to work, as it would in a language with call by reference. To make it work in C you must pass addresses — void swap(int *a, int *b) called as swap(&x, &y) — and dereference inside.

DS-U1-Q15NATeasy

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int sq(int x) { return x * x; } int main(void) { int s = 0; for (int i = 1; i <= 4; i++) s += sq(i); printf("%d\n", s); return 0; }

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The function is called once per iteration with the current value of i, and its return value is added to the accumulator.

Step by step.

  1. Calls: sq(1) = 1, sq(2) = 4, sq(3) = 9, sq(4) = 16.
  2. Sum: 1 + 4 + 9 + 16 = 30.
  3. Closed form: n(n + 1)(2n + 1)/6 = 4·5·9/6 = 30 for n = 4.

Final answer. 30

Common trap. Summing 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 = 10 by reading the call as sq(i) = i, or stopping the loop at i < 4 and losing the 16. The upper bound is i <= 4, so four calls are made.

DS-U1-Q16MCQmedium

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int f(void) { static int c = 0; c++; return c; } int main(void) { f(); f(); printf("%d\n", f()); return 0; }

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. 0
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A static local variable is created once, before the program starts, and lives for the whole execution. It keeps its value between calls; only its name is scoped to the function.

Step by step.

  1. The initialiser c = 0 is applied once, at program start — not on every call.
  2. First call: c becomes 1, returns 1. Second call: becomes 2, returns 2.
  3. The third call (the one inside printf) makes c = 3 and returns 3, which is printed.

Final answer. 3

Common trap. Assuming the local is re-initialised on every call and answering 1. That would be true for an automatic local such as int c = 0;. static changes storage duration, not scope: c is still invisible outside f.

DS-U1-Q17MSQmedium

An array declared as int a[10] is passed to a function declared as void g(int a[]) and called as g(a). Which statements are TRUE?

  1. Inside g the parameter is a pointer, so sizeof a gives the size of a pointer, not 40
  2. The call copies all ten elements into the activation record of g
  3. An assignment to a[i] inside g is visible to the caller
  4. The number of elements cannot be recovered from the parameter alone
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a parameter list, an array declarator is adjusted to a pointer type: int a[] and int a[10] both mean int *a. What is passed is the address of the first element, not a copy of the array.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: after adjustment the parameter has type int *, so sizeof a is the pointer size (4 or 8 bytes), never 40.
  2. (B) is false: C never copies an array on a call. Only the pointer value is copied.
  3. (C) is true: a[i] designates the caller's element through the pointer, so writes are visible after return.
  4. (D) is true: the pointer carries no length, which is why such functions take a separate count parameter.

Final answer. Options (A), (C) and (D)

Common trap. The natural but wrong reading is (B) — "arrays are passed by value like everything else". The value that is copied is the address, so element changes propagate while reassigning the pointer itself does not. And because the length is lost, sizeof inside the callee is the classic way to get a wrong byte count.

DS-U1-Q18MCQmedium

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> void inc(int *p) { *p = *p + 1; } void bump(int p) { p = p + 1; } int main(void) { int a = 5; inc(&a); bump(a); printf("%d\n", a); return 0; }

  1. 5
  2. 6
  3. 7
  4. 8
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Only the callee that receives an address can change the caller's object. inc dereferences its pointer and writes through it; bump increments its own local copy.

Step by step.

  1. inc(&a) passes the address of a; *p = *p + 1 stores 6 in a itself.
  2. bump(a) passes the value 6 into a new local p; p = p + 1 makes that local 7 and it is discarded on return.
  3. So a is 6 and the program prints 6.

Final answer. 6

Common trap. Adding both increments and answering 7, on the assumption that bump also changes the caller. A value parameter is a copy; only writes through a pointer reach the caller. A related trap is writing void inc(int p) and expecting the caller to change.

DS-U1-Q19NAThard

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int f(int n) { static int s = 0; s = s + n; return s; } int main(void) { int i, t = 0; for (i = 1; i <= 4; i++) t += f(i); printf("%d\n", t); return 0; }

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A static local keeps its value across calls, so f returns a running total rather than just n. The calls here are sequential, so the order of evaluation is fully determined.

Step by step.

  1. Call f(1): s = 0 + 1 = 1, returns 1. t = 1.
  2. Call f(2): s = 1 + 2 = 3, returns 3. t = 4.
  3. Call f(3): s = 3 + 3 = 6, returns 6. t = 10.
  4. Call f(4): s = 6 + 4 = 10, returns 10. t = 20.

Final answer. 20

Common trap. Two errors. Ignoring static and computing 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 = 10 gives the distractor 10. Summing the returned values but forgetting that s also accumulates them (answering 10 + 10 = 20 by a wrong route) is harmless here, but the reliable method is to track s call by call. Note the initialiser runs only once for the whole program.

DS-U1-Q39MCQeasy

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> void f(int x) { x = x * 2; } int main(void) { int a[3] = {1, 2, 3}; f(a[1]); printf("%d\n", a[1]); return 0; }

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 4
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. C passes every argument by value: the parameter is a fresh object initialised with a copy of the argument, so assigning to it cannot touch the caller.

Step by step.

  1. a[1] is an int lvalue holding 2; its value is copied into the parameter x.
  2. Inside f, x becomes 4, but that is the local copy.
  3. When f returns, the copy is destroyed and a[1] still holds 2.
  4. The program prints 2.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Assuming an array element is passed by reference because an array name decays to a pointer elsewhere. Only the array name decays; a single element is an ordinary value.

DS-U1-Q40MCQmedium

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int f(int n) { static int c = 0; c += n; return c; } int main(void) { int a = f(2); int b = f(3); printf("%d %d\n", a, b); return 0; }

  1. 2 3
  2. 2 5
  3. 5 5
  4. 0 0
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A static local has static storage duration: it is created once, initialised once, and keeps its value between calls.

Step by step.

  1. The initialiser static int c = 0; runs once, before main begins.
  2. First call: c = 0 + 2 = 2, returned and stored in a.
  3. Second call: c = 2 + 3 = 5, returned and stored in b.
  4. The two statements are sequenced, so the output is 2 5.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Answering 2 3 by assuming the static variable is re-initialised per call. The related hazard is writing the two calls inside one printf: the order in which arguments are evaluated is unspecified, so f(2) and f(3) could run in either order.

DS-U1-Q41MCQhard

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> void f(int *p) { int q = 100; p = &q; } int main(void) { int x = 5; f(&x); printf("%d\n", x); return 0; }

  1. 5
  2. 100
  3. 0
  4. The behaviour is undefined
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A pointer parameter is itself passed by value: the function receives a copy of the address, so re-pointing that copy cannot redirect the caller's pointer or modify the caller's variable.

Step by step.

  1. f(&x) copies the address of x into the parameter p.
  2. p = &q overwrites that local copy with the address of the local q; nothing is written through the pointer.
  3. No dereference assignment ever happens, so x keeps the value 5.
  4. The program prints 5.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Assuming a pointer parameter is 'passed by reference' and that *p = ... was performed. To change the caller's variable you must assign through the pointer (*p = 100), not re-point it. This is also why returning p would be wrong: q dies when f returns.

DS-U1-Q42MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about C function declarations and definitions are TRUE?

  1. A prototype such as int f(int, double); lets the compiler check the number and types of the arguments at each call.
  2. A C function can return an array by value.
  3. A function defined as void g(void) can be called as g().
  4. A declaration int h(); is a prototype that specifies that h takes no arguments.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A prototype carries parameter type information, which is exactly what separates a prototype from an old-style declaration with an empty parameter list.

Step by step.

  1. (A) A prototype declares the parameter types, so the compiler can convert arguments and diagnose mismatches. TRUE.
  2. (B) An array type cannot be returned by value; return a pointer to its first element, or wrap the array in a struct. FALSE.
  3. (C) void g(void) declares no parameters, and g() is the correct call. TRUE.
  4. (D) In C, empty parentheses in a declaration mean the parameters are unspecified, not that there are none — it is not a prototype. FALSE.

Answer: Options (A) and (C)

Common misconception. Option (D) is the classic confusion, and it has a real consequence: because int h(); suppresses argument checking, a wrong-argument call may compile silently. Use (void) to mean 'no arguments'.

DS-U1-Q43MCQeasy

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int g(int a, int b) { return a > b ? a : b; } int h(int a, int b, int c) { return g(g(a, b), c); } int main(void) { printf("%d\n", h(3, 9, 5)); return 0; }

  1. 3
  2. 9
  3. 5
  4. 15
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A function call used as an argument is evaluated first, and its returned value becomes the argument — here g is composed with itself to take a maximum of three values.

Step by step.

  1. The inner call g(3, 9) returns 9, since 3 > 9 is false and the conditional yields b.
  2. The outer call becomes g(9, 5), which returns 9.
  3. So h(3, 9, 5) returns 9 and the program prints 9.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Reading the conditional backwards and taking the minimum, which gives 3. Remember a > b ? a : b returns a when the test is true.

DS-U1-Q44MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about swapping two int variables through a function are TRUE?

  1. A function whose parameters are declared int a, int b cannot change the caller's variables.
  2. With void swap(int *a, int *b) { int t = *a; *a = *b; *b = t; }, the call swap(&x, &y) exchanges x and y.
  3. With void swap(int *a, int *b) { int *t = a; a = b; b = t; }, the call swap(&x, &y) exchanges x and y.
  4. For the call swap(&x, &y) to compile, x and y must be declared static.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Swapping through a function requires writing through pointers to the caller's objects; rearranging the pointer copies themselves has no effect on the pointees.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Value parameters are copies, so the caller's variables are untouched. TRUE.
  2. (B) The body dereferences: t saves the value of x, then *a = *b and *b = t write both pointees. TRUE.
  3. (C) This version copies the two pointers and swaps the copies; no dereference assignment happens, so x and y are unchanged. FALSE.
  4. (D) Any object with an address can have it taken — static is irrelevant to passing a pointer. FALSE.

Answer: Options (A) and (B)

Common misconception. Option (C) is the trap: it is symmetric with the correct version but swaps the wrong level of indirection. Students who remember 'swapping needs pointers' often write the pointer-swapping variant by mistake.

Recursion & stack execution · 12
DS-U1-Q20MCQeasy

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int f(int n) { if (n == 0) return 0; return n + f(n - 1); } int main(void) { printf("%d\n", f(4)); return 0; }

  1. 4
  2. 10
  3. 24
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The recursive call is inside an addition, so each frame must wait for the value returned by the frame below it before it can finish. The results are added on the way back up the stack.

Step by step.

  1. Calls descend: f(4) → f(3) → f(2) → f(1) → f(0).
  2. The base case returns 0. Unwinding: f(1) = 1 + 0 = 1, f(2) = 2 + 1 = 3, f(3) = 3 + 3 = 6, f(4) = 4 + 6 = 10.
  3. This is the sum 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 = 10.

Final answer. 10

Common trap. Answering 24 treats the function as a factorial (4! = 24); the multiplication is nowhere in the code. Answering 4 reads the return as just n. The addition of the returned value is what makes this a sum.

DS-U1-Q21MSQmedium

The program below prints a sequence of digits. Which statements about that output are TRUE?

#include <stdio.h> void f(int n) { if (n > 0) { printf("%d", n % 2); f(n / 2); } } int main(void) { f(13); return 0; }

  1. The digits are printed in the order 1, 0, 1, 1
  2. The first digit printed is the least significant bit of 13
  3. The program prints the binary numeral of 13 in the usual most-significant-first order
  4. The last digit printed is the most significant bit of 13
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each frame prints the least significant remaining bit and only then recurses on the quotient, so the bits emerge least significant first. Code placed before a recursive call runs on the way down the stack.

Step by step.

  1. f(13): 13 mod 2 = 1, print 1, recurse on 13 / 2 = 6.
  2. f(6): 6 mod 2 = 0, print 0, recurse on 3. f(3): print 1, recurse on 1. f(1): print 1, recurse on 0, which stops.
  3. Digits printed: 1, 0, 1, 1. The first is the low-order bit of 13; the last, printed by the deepest frame that prints anything, is the high-order bit. 13 is 1101₂, so the output is that numeral reversed.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (D)

Common trap. Option (C) is the trap: students assume a bit-printing recursion prints the numeral in reading order. Moving the printf after the recursive call would do exactly that and produce 1101. Option (D) is easy to get wrong by assuming the last frame is f(13) — it is the deepest one.

DS-U1-Q22MCQeasy

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> void f(int n) { if (n == 0) return; f(n - 1); printf("%d", n); } int main(void) { f(3); return 0; }

  1. 321
  2. 123
  3. 0123
  4. 3
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The printf is placed after the recursive call, so it runs while the stack unwinds — from the deepest frame back to the shallowest.

Step by step.

  1. Calls descend f(3) → f(2) → f(1) → f(0); f(0) returns immediately.
  2. Unwinding, f(1) prints 1, then f(2) prints 2, then f(3) prints 3.
  3. The output is 123.

Final answer. 123

Common trap. Answering 321 assumes the print happens before the recursion. The distinction is worth memorising: code before the recursive call runs on the way down (most significant first), code after it runs on the way up (least significant first). Nothing prints 0 because the base case returns before printing.

DS-U1-Q23MSQhard

Which statements about recursion and the run-time stack in C are TRUE?

  1. Any recursive function can be rewritten iteratively with an explicit stack that mimics the activation records
  2. A tail-recursive call can reuse the caller's activation record when the compiler performs tail-call optimisation
  3. The C standard guarantees that recursion depth is limited only by available memory
  4. Each active call has its own copy of the parameters and of the automatic local variables
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A call in C uses an activation record (frame) on the run-time stack, holding the return address, the saved state, the parameters and the automatic locals. Recursion is therefore just a sequence of frames, and it can always be simulated explicitly.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: a recursive call does nothing that a manually maintained stack of the same frames cannot reproduce.
  2. (B) is true: when the call is the last operation of the caller, the callee can reuse the frame; this is a permitted optimisation, and -foptimize-sibling-calls enables it in GCC.
  3. (C) is false: the standard sets no minimum depth and no guarantee. The stack is finite and typically a few megabytes, so deep recursion overflows it — a crash, not an out-of-memory error.
  4. (D) is true: that per-call copy is exactly why a value parameter cannot be changed in the caller and why automatic locals do not interfere across frames.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (D)

Common trap. Option (C) is the trap: students confuse "the stack grows as needed" with a standard guarantee. In practice the depth limit is small (tens of thousands of frames at most) and exceeding it is undefined behaviour. A second trap is assuming (B) happens automatically in C — it is an optimisation, not a language requirement.

DS-U1-Q24NATeasy

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int f(int n) { return (n < 2) ? n : f(n - 1) + f(n - 2); } int main(void) { printf("%d\n", f(6)); return 0; }

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. This is the Fibonacci recurrence with base values f(0) = 0 and f(1) = 1. Each value is the sum of the two before it.

Step by step.

  1. Compute upwards: f(2) = 1 + 0 = 1, f(3) = 1 + 1 = 2, f(4) = 2 + 1 = 3, f(5) = 3 + 2 = 5, f(6) = 5 + 3 = 8.
  2. The sequence is 0, 1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8 for n = 0…6.
  3. So the program prints 8.

Final answer. 8

Common trap. Shifting the index by one and answering 5 (the value of f(5)) or 13 (the value of f(7)). Note also that this naive recursion recomputes the same subproblems repeatedly — f(6) needs 25 calls — which is why the exponential cost of the naive version matters.

DS-U1-Q25MCQhard

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int f(int n, int *c) { (*c)++; if (n <= 1) return 1; return f(n - 1, c) + f(n - 2, c); } int main(void) { int c = 0; f(5, &c); printf("%d\n", c); return 0; }

  1. 9
  2. 15
  3. 25
  4. 8
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The counter is incremented once per call, in every frame, through a pointer shared by all of them. The answer is the total number of calls made by the recursion tree, not the value returned.

Step by step.

  1. Let C(n) be the number of calls made by f(n). One call happens at the top, plus the calls made by its two children.
  2. Base cases: C(0) = C(1) = 1. For n ≥ 2: C(n) = 1 + C(n − 1) + C(n − 2).
  3. Compute: C(2) = 1 + 1 + 1 = 3, C(3) = 1 + 3 + 1 = 5, C(4) = 1 + 5 + 3 = 9, C(5) = 1 + 9 + 5 = 15.
  4. So c ends at 15.

Final answer. 15

Common trap. Answering 8, the value f returns, instead of the count. Answering 9 is C(4), the count one level down. The recurrence C(n) = C(n−1) + C(n−2) + 1 is worth remembering: for n = 5 it gives 15, and in general it grows like the Fibonacci numbers — the reason naive recursion here is exponential.

DS-U1-Q45MCQeasy

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int c = 0; int f(int n) { c++; if (n == 0) return 0; return f(n - 1); } int main(void) { f(4); printf("%d\n", c); return 0; }

  1. 4
  2. 5
  3. 6
  4. 1
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The call count of a linear recursion is the number of distinct arguments, and the base-case call is a call like any other.

Step by step.

  1. f is invoked with 4, then 3, 2, 1 and finally 0.
  2. Each invocation increments the global c before testing the base case.
  3. The call with n = 0 also increments c before returning.
  4. So c = 5 when control returns to main.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Answering 4 by counting only the non-base calls. If the increment had been placed after the base-case test, the answer would indeed be 4 — the position of the statement decides it.

DS-U1-Q46MCQeasy

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> void f(int n) { if (n == 0) return; printf("%d", n % 10); f(n / 10); } int main(void) { f(407); printf("\n"); return 0; }

  1. 407
  2. 704
  3. 74
  4. 47
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Work done before the recursive call happens on the way down the stack, so the digits appear in the reverse of the order in which they are peeled off.

Step by step.

  1. f(407) prints 407 % 10 = 7, then calls f(40).
  2. f(40) prints 0, then calls f(4).
  3. f(4) prints 4, then calls f(0), which returns immediately.
  4. The digits appear as 7, 0, 4 — the output is 704.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Answering 407 by assuming the digits are printed most-significant first. To get 407 the recursive call must come before the printf, so that printing happens while the stack unwinds.

DS-U1-Q47MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about recursion and the run-time stack in C are TRUE?

  1. Each active call of a function has its own copy of its automatic local variables.
  2. A static local variable inside a recursive function is re-initialised on every call.
  3. The C standard fixes a maximum recursion depth.
  4. A recursive function with no base case still compiles and links.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Automatic storage is per activation, static storage is per program, and the depth limit is an implementation property of the stack rather than a language rule.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Each activation record holds fresh automatic locals, which is why one call cannot see another's n. TRUE.
  2. (B) A static local is initialised once, before the program starts, and shared by all activations. FALSE.
  3. (C) No limit is specified; exceeding the available stack causes a crash, but the number is not fixed by the standard. FALSE.
  4. (D) A missing base case is not a compile error — the function compiles, links and then fails to terminate. TRUE.

Answer: Options (A) and (D)

Common misconception. Option (C) is often marked true on the assumption that 'the standard must limit recursion somewhere'. It does not; that is why the same program can run on one machine and overflow on another with a smaller stack.

DS-U1-Q48MCQhard

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int f(int n) { if (n == 0) return 0; if (n % 2) return n + f(n - 1); return f(n - 1); } int main(void) { printf("%d\n", f(7)); return 0; }

  1. 9
  2. 12
  3. 16
  4. 28
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The recurrence has two branches: an odd argument contributes its own value plus the result on n − 1, an even argument contributes only the result on n − 1.

Step by step.

  1. f(7) = 7 + f(6), and f(6) = f(5) because 6 is even.
  2. So f(5) = 5 + f(4) with f(4) = f(3), and f(3) = 3 + f(2) with f(2) = f(1).
  3. f(1) = 1 + f(0) = 1, giving f(2) = 1, f(3) = 4, f(5) = 9, f(7) = 16.
  4. In effect the sum is 7 + 5 + 3 + 1 = 16.

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Answering 28 by summing 7 + 6 + … + 1, which ignores the even branch, or 12 by dropping one term. The even case adds nothing at all, so only the odd arguments survive.

DS-U1-Q49MCQeasy

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int f(int n) { if (n == 0) return 0; return (n % 2) + f(n / 2); } int main(void) { printf("%d\n", f(100)); return 0; }

  1. 2
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 7
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Dividing by 2 strips one binary digit per call, and n % 2 is the digit removed, so the recursion counts the 1-bits.

Step by step.

  1. 100 in binary is 1100100, which has three 1-bits.
  2. The first call adds 100 % 2 = 0 and recurses on 50.
  3. The chain continues 100 → 50 → 25 → 12 → 6 → 3 → 1 → 0, adding the low bit at each step.
  4. The bits added are 0, 0, 1, 0, 0, 1, 1 — a total of 3.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Answering 7 by counting the number of bits of 100 instead of the number of 1-bits, or 2 by miscounting the binary expansion. Writing 100 = 64 + 32 + 4 makes the three set bits visible immediately.

DS-U1-Q50NAThard

During the execution of this program, what is the maximum number of activation records of f that are alive at the same time?

#include <stdio.h> int f(int n) { if (n <= 1) return 1; return f(n / 2) + f(n / 2); } int main(void) { f(16); return 0; }

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The number of live frames is the depth of the recursion, not the number of calls: the second recursive call starts only after the first has returned and its frame has been destroyed.

Step by step.

  1. The chain of nested calls is f(16) → f(8) → f(4) → f(2) → f(1).
  2. Each of those five calls is still on the stack when f(1) is entered, so the depth is 5.
  3. The second f(n / 2) in each frame runs after the first has returned, reusing that depth rather than adding to it.
  4. The total number of calls is 31, but the maximum number of live frames is 5.

Answer: 5

Common misconception. Answering 31 by counting calls instead of depth, or 4 by forgetting that the base-case call f(1) is itself a live frame. Depth is the longest root-to-leaf chain, not the size of the tree.

Pointer arithmetic · 14
DS-U2-Q01MCQeasy

Compiled with a C99 compiler on a machine where int is 32 bits, what does this program print?

int a[5] = {10, 20, 30, 40, 50}; int *p = a + 2; printf("%d", *(p - 1) + p[1]);

  1. 50
  2. 60
  3. 70
  4. 40
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Pointer arithmetic is scaled by the size of the pointed-to type, and the subscript p[k] is defined as *(p + k). Both forms index relative to p, not to the start of the array.

Step by step.

  1. p = a + 2 points at a[2], whose value is 30.
  2. *(p - 1) = *(a + 1) = a[1] = 20.
  3. p[1] = *(p + 1) = *(a + 3) = a[3] = 40.
  4. 20 + 40 = 60.

Answer: 60 (option B).

Common misconception. Reading p[1] as a[1]. A subscript applied to a pointer is always relative to where that pointer currently points, so p[1] is the element after *p.

Takeaway. p[k] ≡ *(p + k) ≡ k[p]; the base of the offset is always p.

DS-U2-Q02NATeasy

On a target where sizeof(int) == 4 and sizeof(struct node) == 8 for struct node { int key; char tag; };, consider the fragment below. What integer does it print?

struct node t[10]; struct node *p = &t[2]; printf("%ld", (char *)(p + 3) - (char *)p);

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. p + 3 advances by three elements of type struct node, so the byte distance is 3 × sizeof(struct node). Casting both pointers to char * before subtracting makes the subtraction count bytes, because sizeof(char) == 1.

Step by step.

  1. p = &t[2], so p + 3 = &t[5].
  2. The element difference is 3; the byte difference is 3 × 8 = 24.
  3. (char *)(p + 3) - (char *)p = 24.
  4. Without the casts the same expression would print 3, because pointer subtraction on typed pointers divides by the element size.

Answer: 24

Common misconception. Answering 3 (the element count) or 12 (assuming a 4-byte struct from the int member alone and forgetting the char member and padding).

Takeaway. Typed pointer subtraction counts elements; cast to char * when the byte count is what the question asks for.

DS-U2-Q03MCQeasy

For int a[10]; and an index i with 0 ≤ i < 10, which of the following expressions is NOT equivalent to a[i]?

  1. *(a + i)
  2. *(i + a)
  3. i[a]
  4. *(a + i * sizeof(int))
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The C standard defines a[i] as *(a + i). Pointer addition is commutative, so *(i + a) and, by the same definition, i[a] are the identical expression.

Step by step.

  1. a decays to int *, so a + i advances i elements.
  2. a + i * sizeof(int) advances i * 4 elements, since the scale factor 4 is applied again inside pointer arithmetic.
  3. For i = 1 that expression is a[4], not a[1].
  4. Options A, B and C all evaluate to the address of the same element as a[i].

Answer: *(a + i * sizeof(int)) (option D).

Common misconception. Carrying byte arithmetic from assembly or from char * code into typed pointer arithmetic, where the compiler already multiplies by the element size.

Takeaway. In C, pointer + integer is already measured in elements.

DS-U2-Q04MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about pointer arithmetic in C are TRUE?

  1. Adding 1 to a pointer of type int * advances it by sizeof(int) bytes.
  2. Subtracting two pointers that point into two different arrays is defined by the C standard.
  3. For an array a of n elements, the value a + n may be formed and compared, but must not be dereferenced.
  4. If p and q point into the same array, p - q has type int.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Pointer arithmetic is defined only within one array object (plus its one-past-the-end address); the scale factor is the size of the pointed-to type, and the type of a pointer difference is ptrdiff_t.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. p + 1 is the next object of the pointed-to type, which is sizeof(int) bytes further on.
  2. (B) FALSE. Subtracting pointers that refer to different array objects is undefined behaviour; only pointers into the same array (or one past its end) may be subtracted.
  3. (C) TRUE. One-past-the-end is a valid pointer value that may be formed, compared and subtracted, but dereferencing it is undefined behaviour.
  4. (D) FALSE. The result type is ptrdiff_t, a signed integer type that may be wider than int; it is not int.

Answer: (A) and (C).

Common misconception. Treating p - q as an int because it "looks like" an integer; and assuming any two pointers may be subtracted, which is only guaranteed inside one array.

Takeaway. Pointer arithmetic lives inside one array object; one past the end is addressable but not readable.

DS-U2-Q05MCQeasy

For a two-dimensional array declared as int a[3][4];, what is the type of the expression a + 1?

  1. int *
  2. int **
  3. int (*)[4]
  4. int [4]
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An array name decays one level only: int[3][4] decays to a pointer to its first element, and that element is a row of type int[4]. Hence the decayed type is "pointer to array of 4 int", written int (*)[4].

Step by step.

  1. The element type of a is int[4].
  2. So a decays to int (*)[4] and a + 1 has that same type.
  3. Adding 1 moves by one whole row: with 4-byte int, sizeof(*(a + 1)) evaluates to 16, which confirms the pointed-to type is int[4].

Answer: int (*)[4] (option C).

Common misconception. Assuming the decay goes all the way to int **. That only happens for an array of pointers, such as int *a[3], never for a true two-dimensional array.

Takeaway. An n-dimensional array decays to a pointer to an (n−1)-dimensional array, and only one level is lost.

DS-U2-Q06MCQhard

What does this program print?

int a[4] = {1, 2, 3, 4}; int *p = a; int **pp = &p; *pp += 2; *(*pp + 1) += 10; (*pp)--; printf("%d %d %d", *p, a[2], a[3]);

  1. 1 3 14
  2. 2 3 14
  3. 2 14 14
  4. 3 3 14
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. pp is a pointer to the pointer variable p. Writing *pp designates the object p itself, so *pp += 2 moves the pointer, whereas **pp += 2 would have changed a[0].

Step by step.

  1. *pp += 2 sets p = a + 2; the array is untouched.
  2. *pp + 1 is p + 1 = &a[3], so *(*pp + 1) += 10 makes a[3] = 4 + 10 = 14.
  3. (*pp)-- decrements the pointer: p = a + 1, so *p = a[1] = 2.
  4. a[2] was never written, so it is still 3. Output: 2 3 14.

Answer: 2 3 14 (option B).

Common misconception. Reading *pp += 2 as an increment of the pointed-to value. The left operand of += here is the pointer object p reached through pp, not the element it addresses.

Takeaway. Count the stars: one star reaches the pointer, two stars reach the element.

DS-U2-Q07MCQmedium

What is printed by the following fragment?

int a[6] = {0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5}; int *p = a + 5; while (p > a) printf("%d", *--p);

  1. 54321
  2. 4321
  3. 43210
  4. 543210
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. *--p is a pre-decrement: p is moved first and the new target is then dereferenced. *p-- would dereference first and move afterwards.

Step by step.

  1. Initially p = a + 5, pointing at 5; the test p > a is true.
  2. First iteration: p becomes a + 4 and 4 is printed.
  3. The loop repeats for 3, 2, 1 and 0.
  4. When p reaches a the test fails, so 5 is never printed and the last character printed is 0. Output: 43210.

Answer: 43210 (option C).

Common misconception. Assuming the starting element is printed, which is what *p-- would do, giving 54321.

Takeaway. The loop bound p > a guards against reading a[-1] when the decrement form is used.

DS-U2B-Q01MCQeasy

For an array declared as int a[10];, what is the type of the expression &a?

  1. int *
  2. int **
  3. int (*)[10]
  4. int [10]
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Unary & applies to the array object itself, not to the pointer it decays to, so the result points to a whole array type.

Step by step.

  1. In almost every expression the name a decays to int *, pointing at a[0].
  2. That decay is suppressed when the name is the operand of sizeof, of unary &, or the initialiser of a character array. Here it is the operand of &.
  3. So &a is a pointer to the object of type int[10]: its type is int (*)[10].
  4. The two types are visible in the arithmetic: &a + 1 jumps 40 bytes (the whole array), while a + 1 jumps 4 bytes.

Answer: int (*)[10] (option C).

Common trap. Concluding that &a is int * because a behaves like one. The two pointers hold the same address but have different types, so they scale differently and are not interchangeable.

DS-U2B-Q02MCQeasy

What does this program print?

int a[5] = {2, 4, 6, 8, 10}; int *p = a; p += 2; printf("%d %d", *p, p[-1]);

  1. 6 4
  2. 6 2
  3. 4 6
  4. 8 6
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A subscript on a pointer is relative to where the pointer currently points: p[k] means *(p + k), and a negative index is legal as long as the resulting address is inside the same array.

Step by step.

  1. p = a, then p += 2 moves it two elements on, so p points at a[2], whose value is 6.
  2. *p is therefore 6.
  3. p[-1] is *(p - 1), one element back from a[2]: that is a[1], whose value is 4.
  4. The output is 6 4.

Answer: 6 4 (option A).

Common trap. Reading p[-1] as a[-1] and calling it out of bounds. The index is measured from p, not from the start of the array, so it lands on a perfectly valid element.

DS-U2B-Q03NATeasy

On a machine where int is 4 bytes, what integer does this program print?

int a[4][3]; printf("%d", (int)((char *)(a + 1) - (char *)a));

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Pointer arithmetic is scaled by the size of the pointed-to type. Here a has type int[4][3] and decays to a pointer to its first row, of type int (*)[3].

Step by step.

  1. One step of a + 1 advances by sizeof(int[3]), the size of a whole row.
  2. sizeof(int[3]) = 3 × 4 = 12 bytes.
  3. Casting both addresses to char * makes the subtraction a plain byte count, which is 12.

Answer: 12

Common trap. Reporting 4, the size of one element, or 3, the number of elements in a row. A row pointer steps by the size of the row in bytes, not by the size of an int and not by the element count.

DS-U2B-Q04MCQmedium

What does this program print?

int a[3] = {7, 8, 9}; int *p = a + 1; int **q = &p; (*q)[1] += 10; printf("%d %d", a[1], a[2]);

  1. 8 9
  2. 18 9
  3. 18 19
  4. 8 19
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. q points at the pointer variable p, so *q is p itself and the subscript applies to p, not to a.

Step by step.

  1. p = a + 1, so p points at a[1].
  2. *q is p; therefore (*q)[1] is p[1], which is *(p + 1) = a[2] = 9.
  3. 9 + 10 = 19 is stored into a[2].
  4. a[1] was never touched and stays 8, so the output is 8 19.

Answer: 8 19 (option D).

Common trap. Treating (*q)[1] as a[1]. The dereference yields the pointer p, so index 1 means one element past p's target, which is a[2].

DS-U2B-Q05MSQmedium

For int a[4][5]; and int (*r)[5] = a;, on a machine where int is 4 bytes, which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. r + 1 advances the address by 20 bytes.
  2. r[2][3] denotes the same element as a[2][3].
  3. sizeof(*r) equals 20.
  4. r[0] + 5 may be dereferenced to read a[1][0].
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. r is a pointer to a row, so every step and every dereference is scaled by the size of one row, and each row is an array object in its own right.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. r + 1 advances by sizeof(int[5]) = 20 bytes.
  2. (B) TRUE. r[2] is *(r + 2), the row a[2]; subscripting it again gives a[2][3].
  3. (C) TRUE. *r has type int[5], and sizeof does not decay it, so sizeof(*r) = 20.
  4. (D) FALSE. r[0] is the row a[0], an array of 5 ints. r[0] + 5 is exactly its one-past-the-end address; forming it is allowed, but dereferencing it is undefined behaviour, because pointer arithmetic is bounded by the array object it started in.

Answer: options (A), (B) and (C).

Common trap. Assuming that because the rows sit contiguously, r[0] + 5 must reach a[1][0]. The standard tracks the array object, not the address, so the dereference is invalid even though the byte is physically there.

DS-U2B-Q06MCQhard

On a machine where pointers are 8 bytes and int is 4 bytes, what does this program print?

int a[3][4] = {{0}}; int *b[3]; int (*c)[4] = a; printf("%d %d", (int)sizeof(b), (int)sizeof(*c));

  1. 24 16
  2. 24 48
  3. 48 16
  4. 12 16
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Postfix [] binds tighter than *, so int *b[3] is an array of three int pointers, whereas int (*c)[4] is one pointer to an array of four ints.

Step by step.

  1. b is an array of 3 pointers; sizeof(b) = 3 × 8 = 24 bytes.
  2. c points to a row of four ints, so *c has type int[4].
  3. sizeof(*c) = 4 × 4 = 16 bytes. Only c itself is a pointer, and it is not what sizeof was applied to.
  4. The output is 24 16.

Answer: 24 16 (option A).

Common trap. Reading sizeof(b) as the size of three ints (12) or sizeof(*c) as the size of a pointer (8). An array of pointers stores pointers, and dereferencing a row pointer yields a row, not an int.

DS-U2B-Q07NAThard

What integer does this program print?

int a[7] = {3, 1, 4, 1, 5, 9, 2}; int *p = a, *q = a + 6; int c = 0; while (p < q) { if (*p++ < *q--) c++; } printf("%d", c);

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Post-increment in a condition reads the current element and then advances the pointer, and both increments happen on every iteration whether or not the test succeeds.

Step by step.

  1. Iteration 1: p reads 3, q reads 2. 3 < 2 is false, c stays 0; both pointers advance.
  2. Iteration 2: p reads 1, q reads 9. 1 < 9 is true, so c becomes 1.
  3. Iteration 3: p reads 4, q reads 5. 4 < 5 is true, so c becomes 2.
  4. Now both pointers point at a[3], the test p < q fails, and the loop ends with c = 2.

Answer: 2

Common trap. Counting the third comparison, or assuming the loop runs for all seven elements. The two pointers close in from both ends and stop when they meet, so only three comparisons are made.

Pointers to functions · 12
DS-U2-Q08MCQeasy

Which of the following declarations declares f as a pointer to a function that takes two int arguments and returns int?

  1. int (*f)(int, int);
  2. int *f(int, int);
  3. int *(*f)(int, int);
  4. int (*f[2])(int, int);
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The postfix function-call operator binds tighter than the prefix *. Parentheses around *f are therefore what make f a pointer; without them f is a function.

Step by step.

  1. (A) int (*f)(int, int): f is a pointer to a function taking two int and returning int. This is the required type.
  2. (B) int *f(int, int): a function taking two int and returning int *.
  3. (C) int *(*f)(int, int): a pointer to a function returning int *.
  4. (D) int (*f[2])(int, int): an array of two function pointers.

Answer: int (*f)(int, int); (option A).

Common misconception. Dropping the parentheses, which silently turns a pointer into a function declaration returning a pointer.

Takeaway. Read declarations inside-out, starting from the identifier: (*f) first, then the parameter list, then the return type.

DS-U2-Q09MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about pointers to functions in C are TRUE?

  1. A function name used in an expression, other than as the operand of sizeof or of unary &, converts to a pointer to that function.
  2. Two function pointers may be compared with == and !=, but the relational operators < and > are not defined for them.
  3. A function pointer must be dereferenced with * before a call: (*fp)() is valid but fp() is a constraint violation.
  4. A function pointer can be converted to void * and back without loss on every conforming implementation.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Function designators decay to pointers to the function; a function pointer supports assignment, equality comparison and a call, but the standard does not place function pointers in the same representation class as object pointers.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. Apart from sizeof and unary &, a function designator converts to "pointer to function returning ..." — this is why g(f) passes the address of f.
  2. (B) TRUE. Equality and inequality are defined for pointers to functions; ordering comparisons of function pointers are undefined behaviour.
  3. (C) FALSE. The & and * operators are optional around a function pointer, so fp(), (*fp)() and (**fp)() all call the same function.
  4. (D) FALSE. Converting a function pointer to void * is not guaranteed by the standard; some architectures keep code and data in separate address spaces.

Answer: (A) and (B).

Common misconception. Believing that the explicit * is required for a call, or that void * is a universal pointer type. Neither is true in standard C.

Takeaway. Function pointers compare for equality only, and call through either form of the expression.

DS-U2-Q10NATmedium

What integer does the following program print?

int add(int a, int b) { return a + b; } int sub(int a, int b) { return a - b; } int mul(int a, int b) { return a * b; } int main(void) { int (*ops[3])(int, int) = {add, sub, mul}; int r = 0; for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) r += ops[i](7, 3); printf("%d", r); return 0; }

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An array of function pointers is indexed and then called exactly like an array of objects: ops[i] is a function pointer and ops[i](7, 3) is a call through it.

Step by step.

  1. ops[0] = add → 7 + 3 = 10.
  2. ops[1] = sub → 7 − 3 = 4.
  3. ops[2] = mul → 7 × 3 = 21.
  4. r = 10 + 4 + 21 = 35.

Answer: 35

Common misconception. Writing ops[i](7, 3) as *ops[i](7, 3) or assuming the subscript selects the argument rather than the callee.

Takeaway. A dispatch table turns a chain of switch cases into one indexed call, which is the standard way to implement callbacks.

DS-U2-Q11MCQhard

What does this program print?

typedef int (*Op)(int, int); int add(int a, int b) { return a + b; } int sub(int a, int b) { return a - b; } Op table[2] = {add, sub}; int apply(Op f, int a, int b) { return f(a, b); } int main(void) { int r = 0; for (int i = 0; i < 2; i++) r += apply(table[i], table[i](10, 4), 2); printf("%d", r); return 0; }

  1. 20
  2. 18
  3. 22
  4. 16
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. apply receives a function pointer and calls it on the two values it is given. Its first argument is evaluated by a call through the same table entry, so the table entry is used twice per iteration.

Step by step.

  1. i = 0: table[0](10, 4) = add(10, 4) = 14. Then apply(add, 14, 2) = 14 + 2 = 16.
  2. i = 1: table[1](10, 4) = sub(10, 4) = 6. Then apply(sub, 6, 2) = 6 − 2 = 4.
  3. r = 16 + 4 = 20.

Answer: 20 (option A).

Common misconception. Applying the second operand 2 as a third argument, or using table[i](10, 4) as the second argument of the same call, which reverses the nesting and gives 18.

Takeaway. A typedef for a function pointer type makes higher-order signatures readable and keeps argument order obvious.

DS-U2-Q12MCQeasy

A function int f(int); and a function void g(int (*fn)(int)); are both in scope. Which of the following calls correctly passes f to g?

  1. g(f());
  2. g(&f());
  3. g(*f());
  4. g(f);
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A function designator converts to a pointer to the function, so the bare name f already has the required type int (*)(int).

Step by step.

  1. f() calls f and yields an int, which is not a function pointer; the argument type is wrong.
  2. &f() takes the address of the int returned by the call. That value is not an lvalue, so the & operator is not applicable.
  3. *f() dereferences that int, which is also invalid.
  4. g(f) passes the converted function pointer. Writing g(&f) would be equally correct.

Answer: g(f); (option D).

Common misconception. Adding a call’s parentheses out of habit, which turns the function pointer into the function’s return value.

Takeaway. Name the function without parentheses to take its address; add parentheses only to call it.

DS-U2-Q13MCQmedium

Which of the following declarations makes F an alias for a pointer to a function that takes one int and returns a char?

  1. typedef char *F(int);
  2. typedef char **F(int);
  3. typedef char (*F[2])(int);
  4. typedef char (*F)(int);
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a typedef the declarator is read the same way as in an object declaration: the parenthesised (*F) is what makes F a pointer, and the return type is whatever precedes the declarator.

Step by step.

  1. (A) typedef char *F(int) names a function type taking int and returning char *, so F is not a pointer.
  2. (B) same shape, returning char **.
  3. (C) F is an array of two pointers to functions returning char.
  4. (D) typedef char (*F)(int) names the type "pointer to function taking int returning char", so F t = some_function; is a valid initialisation.

Answer: typedef char (*F)(int); (option D).

Common misconception. Reading char *F(int) as "pointer to function". In a typedef the * belongs to the return type unless it is parenthesised with the name.

Takeaway. The typedef name sits exactly where a variable name would; write the declaration for a variable first, then replace the name.

DS-U2B-Q08MCQeasy

Which of the following declarations declares a as an array of three pointers to functions that each take one int and return void?

  1. void *a[3](int);
  2. void (*a[3])(int);
  3. void (*a)(int)[3];
  4. void *(*a[3])(int);
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A declarator is read from the identifier outwards, with postfix [] and () binding tighter than prefix * unless parentheses override the order.

Step by step.

  1. In void (*a[3])(int), the parentheses first bind * to a, but inside them the postfix [3] still applies first: a is an array of 3.
  2. Each element is then (*…), a pointer, and the trailing (int) says it points to a function taking an int.
  3. The leading void is that function's return type.
  4. Option (A) is an array of functions returning void *, (C) is a syntax error, and (D) is an array of pointers to functions returning void *.

Answer: void (*a[3])(int); (option B).

Common trap. Dropping the parentheses and writing void *a[3](int), which declares an array of functions, not of pointers to functions. Without the parentheses the * attaches to the return type instead of to a.

DS-U2B-Q09NATeasy

What integer does this program print?

int inc(int x) { return x + 1; } int dbl(int x) { return x * 2; } int main(void) { int (*t[2])(int) = {inc, dbl}; int v = 3, s = 0; for (int i = 0; i < 2; i++) { v = t[i](v); s += v; } printf("%d", s); return 0; }

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An array of function pointers lets a loop apply a chain of different functions to a running value; the result of one call is the argument of the next.

Step by step.

  1. v = 3, s = 0.
  2. i = 0: t[0] is inc, so v = 4 and s = 0 + 4 = 4.
  3. i = 1: t[1] is dbl, so v = 8 and s = 4 + 8 = 12.
  4. The loop ends and 12 is printed.

Answer: 12

Common trap. Applying both functions to the original 3 and summing 4 + 6 = 10. Each call receives the value produced by the previous one, so the chain is 3 → 4 → 8.

DS-U2B-Q10MCQmedium

What does this program print?

int add(int a, int b) { return a + b; } int mul(int a, int b) { return a * b; } int apply(int (*f)(int, int), int a, int b) { return f(a, b); } int main(void) { int (*p)(int, int) = &mul; printf("%d", apply(p, 2 + 3, 4)); return 0; }

  1. 9
  2. 14
  3. 20
  4. 24
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A function pointer is an ordinary value: it can be stored, passed as an argument and called through, and unary & on a function name is optional.

Step by step.

  1. p = &mul, and &mul is the same value as mul.
  2. 2 + 3 is evaluated before the call, giving the argument 5.
  3. apply(p, 5, 4) calls p(5, 4), which is mul(5, 4) = 20.
  4. The output is 20.

Answer: 20 (option C).

Common trap. Answering 14 by using add, or 24 by reading the call as mul(2 + 3 * 4). The arguments are two separate values, 5 and 4, and the function called is the one the pointer holds.

DS-U2B-Q11MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about pointers to functions in C are TRUE?

  1. int (*f)(int); declares f as a pointer to a function taking one int and returning int.
  2. A function designator, except as the operand of sizeof or of unary &, is converted to a pointer to that function.
  3. Two function pointers may be compared with < to decide which function has the lower address.
  4. A function pointer may be stored in a void * and converted back without loss on every conforming implementation.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A function designator decays to a pointer to the function, and only the equality operators are defined on those pointers.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. That is exactly what the declarator says: f is a pointer, and the function it points to takes an int and returns an int.
  2. (B) TRUE. This is the conversion rule for function designators, which is why qsort(v, n, sz, cmp) needs no &.
  3. (C) FALSE. Only == and != are defined between function pointers; relational comparison has no defined meaning.
  4. (D) FALSE. Conversion to void * is guaranteed only for pointers to objects. Storing a function pointer in a void * is a common extension, not a portable guarantee.

Answer: options (A) and (B).

Common trap. Assuming all pointers are interchangeable. Object pointers and function pointers live in different conversion rules, and ordering them is not part of the language at all.

DS-U2B-Q12MCQhard

What does this program print?

int add(int a, int b) { return a + b; } int mul(int a, int b) { return a * b; } typedef int (*Op)(int, int); Op pick(char c) { return c == '+' ? add : mul; } int main(void) { Op a = pick('+'), b = pick('*'); printf("%d", a(b(2, 3), 4)); return 0; }

  1. 20
  2. 14
  3. 24
  4. 10
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A function can return a function pointer, and a call written as a(b(2, 3), 4) evaluates the inner call first because it is an argument of the outer one.

Step by step.

  1. pick('+') returns add, so a is add.
  2. pick('*') returns mul, so b is mul.
  3. The inner call b(2, 3) is mul(2, 3) = 6.
  4. The outer call a(6, 4) is add(6, 4) = 10.

Answer: 10 (option D).

Common trap. Answering 20 by applying mul last, or 14 by applying add twice. The innermost call is the argument of the outer one, so mul runs first.

DS-U2B-Q13MCQmedium

What does this program print?

int f(int x) { return x + 1; } int g(int x) { return x * 2; } int main(void) { int (*p)(int) = f, (*q)(int) = g, (*r)(int) = p; p = q; q = r; printf("%d %d", p(3), q(3)); return 0; }

  1. 4 6
  2. 6 4
  3. 6 6
  4. 4 4
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A function pointer is a variable holding an address; assigning one pointer to another copies the address, it does not link the two variables.

Step by step.

  1. Initially p = f, q = g, and r = p copies the value f into r.
  2. p = q copies g into p. Now p is g; q is still g.
  3. q = r copies f into q. Now q is f.
  4. p(3) = g(3) = 6 and q(3) = f(3) = 4, so the output is 6 4.

Answer: 6 4 (option B).

Common trap. Treating r = p as a reference, so that changing p would change r. It is a value copy taken at the moment of assignment, which is why r still holds f.

Dynamic memory allocation · 12
DS-U2-Q14MCQeasy

Which of the following statements about the C dynamic allocation functions is TRUE?

  1. malloc zero-fills the block it returns, while calloc leaves the bytes indeterminate.
  2. calloc takes two arguments, multiplies them, and zero-fills the block; malloc takes one size in bytes and leaves the contents indeterminate.
  3. realloc never moves the block, so the returned pointer always equals the pointer passed in.
  4. free(p) sets the pointer variable p itself to NULL.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. malloc(n) requests n bytes with indeterminate contents; calloc(n, m) requests n * m bytes and zero-fills them; realloc may move the block; free takes the pointer by value.

Step by step.
