  1. (A) FALSE. The behaviours are the other way round: malloc does not initialise, calloc does.
  2. (B) TRUE. calloc(nmemb, size) computes nmemb * size and zeroes the result.
  3. (C) FALSE. realloc commonly moves the block when the new size does not fit in place, which is why its return value must be used.
  4. (D) FALSE. The argument is passed by value, so the caller's variable keeps its old value; that stale value is a dangling pointer.

Answer: (B).

Common misconception. Expecting malloc to hand back zeroed memory because it often does on a freshly started process.

Takeaway. Use calloc when zeroes are needed; never assume malloc gives them.

DS-U2-Q15NATeasy

On a target where sizeof(struct rec) == 16 for struct rec { int id; double w; };, a program executes the statement below. How many bytes does the call request in total?

struct rec *r = calloc(6, sizeof(struct rec));

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. calloc(nmemb, size) allocates space for an array of nmemb objects, each of size bytes, and zero-fills the whole region.

Step by step.

  1. The object size is given as 16 bytes.
  2. The element count is 6.
  3. Total = 6 × 16 = 96 bytes.
  4. calloc returns a pointer to 96 zero bytes, or NULL on failure.

Answer: 96

Common misconception. Passing the element size as the count, as in calloc(sizeof(struct rec), 6). The product is the same, but the intent and the type of each argument are reversed, which hides bugs when the values differ.

Takeaway. calloc multiplies its two arguments; the byte count is never written out by hand.

DS-U2-Q16MSQhard

Which of the following statements about dynamic storage management in C are TRUE?

  1. Calling free(p) where p was not returned by an allocation function is undefined behaviour.
  2. After free(p) the value of p is indeterminate and using it is undefined behaviour.
  3. In C99, realloc(p, 0) is guaranteed to free the block and return NULL.
  4. malloc(0) may return either NULL or a unique pointer that must not be dereferenced but may be passed to free.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The allocation functions own the heap blocks; a pointer that has been freed or never allocated carries no defined behaviour when used, and the zero-size cases are left implementation-defined by the standard.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. free requires an argument that is a null pointer or a pointer returned by malloc, calloc or realloc; anything else is undefined behaviour.
  2. (B) TRUE. The pointer object still exists, but its value is indeterminate after the free, so any use is undefined behaviour — this is the dangling-pointer case.
  3. (C) FALSE. C99 makes realloc(p, 0) implementation-defined: the call either returns NULL without freeing, or behaves as realloc(p, 1). It is not guaranteed to free, which is why free(p); p = NULL; is preferred.
  4. (D) TRUE. For a zero size the call may return NULL or a unique pointer to no object; if a pointer is returned it must be freed and must not be dereferenced.

Answer: (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Believing that realloc(p, 0) is a portable way to free memory. It is not; call free.

Takeaway. Free exactly what was allocated, exactly once, and set the handle to NULL afterwards.

DS-U2-Q17MCQmedium

A buffer allocated with malloc is grown by the statement below. If the reallocation fails, what is the defect?

p = realloc(p, 100 * sizeof *p);

  1. realloc returns NULL and p is overwritten, so the original block is leaked and its address is lost.
  2. There is none: realloc leaves p unchanged when it fails, so the old block is still reachable.
  3. It is a constraint violation that a conforming compiler must diagnose.
  4. It leaks memory even when the reallocation succeeds.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. realloc may move the block, so it returns the new address. On failure it returns NULL and leaves the original block allocated and unchanged. Assigning the result straight back to p therefore destroys the only handle to that block.

Step by step.

  1. Success: the new address is returned and the old block is released. Assigning to p is then correct.
  2. Failure: NULL is returned, and the old block is still allocated.
  3. Writing p = realloc(p, n) stores NULL into p, so the old address is lost and the block can never be freed — a leak.
  4. The safe pattern keeps the result in a temporary: q = realloc(p, n); if (q) p = q;

Answer: (A).

Common misconception. Believing that a failed realloc frees the old block. It does not; the caller still owns it.

Takeaway. Never overwrite the only pointer to a block with a value that may be NULL.

DS-U2-Q18MCQmedium

Consider the function below. Which statement correctly describes the pointer it returns?

int *make(void) { int x = 42; return &x; }

  1. It is valid: the lifetime of x extends to the end of the program because the pointer escapes the function.
  2. It is a compile-time error to return the address of an automatic variable, so the program cannot be built.
  3. It is undefined behaviour: the pointer refers to an object whose lifetime ended when make returned.
  4. It is valid, but the value read through the pointer is merely unspecified.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. x has automatic storage duration: its lifetime begins on entry to the block and ends when the block terminates. A pointer to it after that point is dangling.

Step by step.

  1. The object x ceases to exist when make returns.
  2. The returned address therefore refers to no object; the C standard makes any use of it undefined behaviour.
  3. Compilers usually diagnose it (-Wreturn-local-addr), but it is not a constraint violation, so a conforming implementation may accept the program.
  4. Undefined behaviour is stronger than "unspecified value": the program has no defined meaning at all, and the value may even appear to be 42 on some builds.

Answer: (C).

Common misconception. Calling the result "unspecified" or "garbage but harmless". Reading through a dangling pointer is undefined behaviour, not merely an unpredictable value.

Takeaway. Return a pointer to a static object, to a caller-supplied buffer, or to a heap block — never to an automatic local.

DS-U2-Q19NATmedium

How many bytes are leaked when the following function returns?

void f(void) { char *p = malloc(20); char *q = malloc(30); p = q; free(p); }

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A block is reachable only through the pointers that hold its address. Overwriting the last such pointer loses the block permanently, and that is what "leaked" means.

Step by step.

  1. malloc(20) returns block A and stores its address in p.
  2. malloc(30) returns block B and stores its address in q.
  3. p = q overwrites the only handle to block A. Block A is still allocated but no longer reachable.
  4. free(p) frees block B, which is correct. Block A is never freed, so 20 bytes leak.

Answer: 20

Common misconception. Reporting 30, the size of the block that was freed, or 50, the total requested. A leak is measured by the block that became unreachable.

Takeaway. Free in the reverse order of allocation, and null a handle as soon as the block behind it is released.

DS-U2B-Q14MCQeasy

On a target where sizeof(double) == 8, what does the call calloc(5, sizeof(double)) request?

  1. 40 bytes, with every bit set to zero.
  2. 40 bytes, with indeterminate contents.
  3. 8 bytes, with every bit set to zero.
  4. 13 bytes, with every bit set to zero.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. calloc(n, size) allocates space for an array of n objects of size bytes each and initialises all of it to zero bits.

Step by step.

  1. The two arguments are multiplied: 5 × 8 = 40 bytes.
  2. calloc then sets every bit of those 40 bytes to 0, which is why it is preferred when a zero-initialised array is wanted.
  3. malloc would request the same 40 bytes but leave the contents indeterminate.

Answer: 40 bytes, zero-filled (option A).

Common trap. Swapping the two functions and choosing "indeterminate". Also note that zero bits is not the same as a floating-point 0.0 on every implementation, though for IEEE-754 it happens to coincide.

DS-U2B-Q15NATeasy

On a target where int is 4 bytes, a program executes the statement below. How many bytes does the call request in total?

struct pt { int x, y; }; struct pt *a = calloc(12, sizeof(struct pt));

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. calloc multiplies its two arguments, so the byte count is the element count times the element size.

Step by step.

  1. struct pt holds two int members, so sizeof(struct pt) = 8 bytes; no padding is added because both members are 4-byte aligned and the total is already a multiple of 4.
  2. The call requests 12 × 8 = 96 bytes, all zero-filled.
  3. The result is a pointer to the first of 12 struct pt objects.

Answer: 96

Common trap. Adding the arguments (12 + 8 = 20) or using the size of a member (12 × 4 = 48). The second argument is the size of the whole element, and the two arguments multiply.

DS-U2B-Q16MCQmedium

Consider int *q = realloc(p, 8 * sizeof *p);. If the call succeeds by moving the block to a new address, which statement is correct?

  1. realloc returns NULL on failure and frees the original block.
  2. p and q always refer to the same block.
  3. The old block has been released by realloc, so p must not be used again.
  4. realloc always returns the same address it was given.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. realloc may extend the block in place or allocate a new one and copy; on success the old block is no longer owned by the program.

Step by step.

  1. On success, realloc returns a pointer to the block of the new size and releases the old one if it had to move.
  2. So after a moving reallocation, p is a dangling pointer: any use of it is undefined behaviour, and q is the only valid handle.
  3. On failure, realloc returns NULL and leaves the original block allocated and untouched — which is why the safe idiom stores the result in a temporary before overwriting p.
  4. Options (A), (B) and (D) each contradict one of those rules.

Answer: option (C).

Common trap. Believing that a failed realloc destroys the original block, or that a successful one keeps the same address. Both beliefs cause leaks and use-after-free bugs respectively.

DS-U2B-Q17MSQhard

Which of the following statements about dynamic storage management in C are TRUE?

  1. free(NULL) has no effect and is well defined.
  2. Freeing the same block twice is undefined behaviour.
  3. If realloc(p, n) fails, the original block is still allocated and must still be freed.
  4. malloc returns storage that is guaranteed to be filled with zero bytes.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The allocation functions have narrow, precisely stated guarantees: only calloc zero-fills, only a live block may be freed, and a failed realloc changes nothing.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. The standard says that if the argument is a null pointer, no action occurs. It is therefore safe and idiomatic.
  2. (B) TRUE. A second free on the same block is a double free, which is undefined behaviour.
  3. (C) TRUE. Failure means the old block was left as it was, so the program still owns it and must free it.
  4. (D) FALSE. malloc leaves the contents indeterminate; only calloc guarantees zero bits.

Answer: options (A), (B) and (C).

Common trap. Assuming a failed realloc has already freed the old block, and so skipping the free and leaking it. Failure is a no-op on the existing allocation.

DS-U2B-Q18MCQmedium

Consider this fragment. Which statement about q after the free is correct?

int *p = malloc(3 * sizeof *p); for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) p[i] = i + 1; int *q = p + 1; free(p);

  1. q still points to valid storage, because only p was freed.
  2. free(p) also resets q to NULL.
  3. Reading *q still prints 2, because the value has not been overwritten yet.
  4. q is a dangling pointer; reading *q is undefined behaviour.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. free releases the whole allocated block, and every pointer into that block becomes invalid at once, not just the pointer that was passed.

Step by step.

  1. p owns a block of 3 ints; q = p + 1 points at the second element, inside the same block.
  2. free(p) ends the lifetime of the entire block.
  3. q is not modified by free, so it still holds the old address, but that address no longer denotes a live object: it is a dangling pointer.
  4. Reading or writing *q is therefore undefined behaviour, even though the bytes may still hold 2.

Answer: option (D).

Common trap. Thinking that only the exact pointer passed to free becomes invalid, or that the value surviving in memory makes the read legal. A pointer's validity depends on the object's lifetime, not on what the bytes happen to contain.

DS-U2B-Q19MCQmedium

On a machine where pointers are 8 bytes and int is 4 bytes, how many allocation calls does this fragment make, and how many bytes does it request in total?

int **m = malloc(3 * sizeof *m); for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) m[i] = malloc(4 * sizeof **m);

  1. 1 call, 72 bytes
  2. 4 calls, 72 bytes
  3. 4 calls, 60 bytes
  4. 3 calls, 48 bytes
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A dynamically allocated two-dimensional array is built from one allocation for the row-pointer array plus one allocation per row; the pointers live in the outer block.

Step by step.

  1. Call 1: 3 × sizeof(int *) = 3 × 8 = 24 bytes for the array of row pointers.
  2. Calls 2–4: three calls of 4 × sizeof(int) = 16 bytes each, that is 48 bytes of row storage.
  3. Total: 4 calls and 24 + 48 = 72 bytes.
  4. The rows need not be contiguous with each other or with the pointer array, which is exactly why the two-level layout costs one allocation per row.

Answer: 4 calls, 72 bytes (option B).

Common trap. Counting only the three row allocations (3 calls), or pricing the pointers at 4 bytes each, which would give 60 bytes. A row pointer is a pointer, so it costs 8 bytes here, not the 4 bytes of an int.

Macro expansion, scope & lifetime · 12
DS-U2-Q20MCQeasy

What does the following program print?

#define SQR(x) x*x #include <stdio.h> int main(void) { printf("%d", SQR(2 + 3)); return 0; }

  1. 11
  2. 25
  3. 13
  4. 10
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The preprocessor performs textual substitution. SQR(x) x*x replaces the parameter by the argument tokens exactly as written, with no parentheses added.

Step by step.

  1. SQR(2 + 3) expands to 2 + 3*2 + 3.
  2. Multiplication binds tighter than addition, so the expression is 2 + 6 + 3.
  3. The value is 11, and it is printed as 11.
  4. Defining #define SQR(x) ((x)*(x)) would give ((2+3)*(2+3)) = 25.

Answer: 11 (option A).

Common misconception. Treating the macro as a function call and answering 25. A macro has no parameter objects; it has tokens.

Takeaway. Parenthesise every parameter and the whole body: #define SQR(x) ((x)*(x)).

DS-U2-Q21MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the C preprocessor are TRUE?

  1. A macro name is replaced by the preprocessor before compilation, so the arguments of a function-like macro are not type-checked.
  2. #define MAX(a,b) ((a)>(b)?(a):(b)) evaluates each of its arguments exactly once.
  3. A macro may be redefined without error provided the new definition is identical to the previous one.
  4. #undef removes a macro definition, after which the identifier may be redefined.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Macro expansion happens in translation phase 4, before any type information exists, and it is pure token substitution.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. The preprocessor knows nothing about types; the expanded text is type-checked only afterwards, as ordinary code.
  2. (B) FALSE. MAX(i++, j) expands to ((i++)>(j)?(i++):(j)), so the chosen argument is evaluated twice. This is the classic reason to prefer an inline function.
  3. (C) TRUE. An identical redefinition is permitted; a different one must be diagnosed.
  4. (D) TRUE. #undef ends the definition, and the name may then be defined again, possibly with a different body.

Answer: (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming a macro behaves like a function with arguments evaluated once, which silently breaks expressions with side effects.

Takeaway. Never pass an expression with a side effect to a macro parameter.

DS-U2-Q22MCQeasy

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> void f(void) { static int n; n++; printf("%d ", n); } int main(void) { f(); f(); f(); return 0; }

  1. 1 1 1
  2. 1 2 3
  3. 0 1 2
  4. 3 3 3
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. static on a block-scope variable changes its storage duration, not its scope. The object has static storage duration: it is created and zero-initialised once, before the program starts, and lives for the whole run.

Step by step.

  1. n is zero-initialised a single time.
  2. First call: n becomes 1, prints 1.
  3. Second call: the same object is incremented to 2.
  4. Third call: 3. Output 1 2 3 .

Answer: 1 2 3 (option B).

Common misconception. Expecting 0 1 2 by treating the initialiser as running on every call, or 1 1 1 by forgetting that static gives the object a lifetime beyond the call.

Takeaway. static at block scope = one object, one initialisation, program lifetime, still invisible outside the block.

DS-U2-Q23NAThard

What integer does the following program print?

#define SQ(x) x * x #define CUBE(x) SQ(x) * x #include <stdio.h> int main(void) { printf("%d", CUBE(1 + 2)); return 0; }

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Macro arguments are substituted as raw token sequences, and the result is then rescanned for further macros. With no parentheses anywhere, the surrounding operators re-associate the whole expression.

Step by step.

  1. CUBE(1 + 2) → SQ(1 + 2) * 1 + 2.
  2. The rescan expands SQ(1 + 2) → 1 + 2 * 1 + 2.
  3. The full expression is 1 + 2 * 1 + 2 * 1 + 2.
  4. Multiplication first: 1 + 2 + 2 + 2 = 7.

Answer: 7

Common misconception. Answering 27, the mathematical cube of 3, or 9 by expanding only the inner macro. Both assume the macros were parenthesised, and neither was.

Takeaway. Write #define SQ(x) ((x)*(x)) and #define CUBE(x) (SQ(x)*(x)); a macro is safe only when every argument and the whole body are parenthesised.

DS-U2-Q24MCQhard

What does this program print?

#include <stdio.h> int x = 10; int main(void) { int x = 20; { extern int x; printf("%d ", x); } printf("%d", x); return 0; }

  1. 10 20
  2. 20 20
  3. 10 10
  4. 20 10
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An extern declaration at block scope declares an identifier with external linkage; it does not introduce a new object, it names the one already defined at file scope.

Step by step.

  1. At file scope x is defined and initialised to 10, with external linkage.
  2. In main, the automatic int x = 20; hides the file-scope object inside the body of main.
  3. Inside the inner block, extern int x; declares x again with external linkage, so the name refers to the file-scope object: 10 is printed.
  4. After the block ends, the inner declaration goes out of scope and the automatic x = 20 is visible again. Output: 10 20.

Answer: 10 20 (option A).

Common misconception. Expecting 20 20, on the assumption that extern simply re-declares the nearest visible x. Linkage, not visibility, decides which object is named.

Takeaway. extern inside a block reaches outside the function; it is the one declaration that ignores shadowing.

DS-U2-Q25MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about scope, linkage and lifetime in C are TRUE?

  1. A block-scope variable declared static keeps its value between calls and is initialised only once.
  2. An object declared const has static storage duration by default.
  3. A file-scope variable declared static has internal linkage and is not visible from another translation unit.
  4. The lifetime of an automatic variable ends when its block terminates, and the storage it used may then be reused.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. C separates three ideas: scope (where a name is visible), linkage (whether another translation unit can refer to it) and storage duration (how long the object exists). static and const touch different ones.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. Block scope plus static storage duration: one object, initialised once, value preserved across calls.
  2. (B) FALSE. const qualifies the type, not the storage duration. A block-scope const object has automatic storage duration unless it is also declared static.
  3. (C) TRUE. At file scope, static gives internal linkage, so the name is private to that translation unit.
  4. (D) TRUE. Automatic storage duration ends at the closing brace; the memory may be reused, which is why a pointer to it becomes dangling.

Answer: (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Conflating const with static. const says the object will not be modified; it says nothing about when the object exists.

Takeaway. Ask three separate questions of any declaration: where is the name visible, who can link to it, and how long does the object live.

DS-U2B-Q20MCQeasy

What does this program print?

#define DOUBLE(x) (x) + (x) printf("%d", 3 * DOUBLE(4));

  1. 24
  2. 16
  3. 12
  4. 8
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A function-like macro is a textual substitution, so the expansion is spliced into the surrounding expression and the operators around it take part in the evaluation.

Step by step.

  1. DOUBLE(4) expands to (4) + (4).
  2. The whole expression becomes 3 * (4) + (4).
  3. Multiplication binds tighter than addition, so it is 12 + 4 = 16.
  4. Had the macro been written #define DOUBLE(x) ((x) + (x)) the answer would be 24, because the whole expansion would be one primary expression.

Answer: 16 (option B).

Common trap. Treating the macro as a function and answering 24. Parenthesising each parameter is not enough; the entire body needs its own pair of parentheses.

DS-U2B-Q21MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about scope, linkage and lifetime in C are TRUE?

  1. A block-scope static object is initialised once, before the first entry to its block, and keeps its value between calls.
  2. An extern declaration at block scope allocates storage for the object it names.
  3. Two functions may each declare a local variable of the same name; each declaration introduces a distinct object.
  4. A file-scope object declared static has internal linkage, so other translation units cannot refer to it by that name.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Scope is where a name is visible, linkage is whether other translation units can see it, and lifetime is when the object exists. The storage class specifiers move these three properties independently.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. A block-scope static object has static storage duration: it is initialised once and survives until the program ends, so a counter inside a function keeps counting.
  2. (B) FALSE. An extern declaration is a declaration, not a definition; it tells the compiler the object exists elsewhere and allocates nothing.
  3. (C) TRUE. Block scope is per block: two locals with the same spelling in different functions are unrelated objects, and neither can see the other.
  4. (D) TRUE. static at file scope gives internal linkage, so the name is private to that translation unit.

Answer: options (A), (C) and (D).

Common trap. Reading extern as "make it external, so allocate it here". It is the opposite: the keyword asserts that the storage is defined somewhere else.

DS-U2B-Q22MCQeasy

Why is a multi-statement macro conventionally wrapped in do { … } while (0)?

  1. It makes the expansion a single statement, so a trailing semicolon and an unbraced if/else parse correctly.
  2. It makes the macro return a value to the caller.
  3. It guarantees that the body executes exactly once even inside a loop.
  4. It forces each macro argument to be evaluated exactly once.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A macro expansion is a token sequence inserted where the name appears, and it must behave like a single statement when it is used as one.

Step by step.

  1. If the body were bare statements in braces, if (c) MACRO; else … would expand to if (c) { … } ; else …, and the stray semicolon would detach the else — a compile error.
  2. do { … } while (0) plus the semicolon the caller writes forms exactly one statement, so it nests correctly under any control structure.
  3. The loop runs exactly once because the condition is the constant 0, which the compiler removes entirely.
  4. It does not affect how many times arguments are evaluated: a parameter that appears twice in the body is still evaluated twice.

Answer: option (A).

Common trap. Believing the wrapper protects against multiple evaluation of arguments. Only a real function, or a macro that mentions each parameter exactly once, does that.

DS-U2B-Q23NAThard

What integer does this program print?

#define A 3 #define B A + 2 printf("%d", B * 2);

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An object-like macro is replaced by its token sequence exactly as written. If the definition is not parenthesised, the expansion inherits the precedence of the operators around it.

Step by step.

  1. B is replaced by A + 2, and A in turn by 3.
  2. The expression becomes 3 + 2 * 2.
  3. Multiplication is evaluated first: 3 + 4 = 7.
  4. With #define B (A + 2) the result would be 5 * 2 = 10, which is what most readers expect.

Answer: 7

Common trap. Evaluating B as the value 5 first and answering 10. Macro expansion is substitution, not evaluation, so the surrounding * 2 reaches only the last term.

DS-U2B-Q24MCQeasy

What is the result of compiling and running this program?

#define SIZE 5 int main(void) { int SIZE = 3; printf("%d", SIZE); return 0; }

  1. 3
  2. 5
  3. A compile-time error: the macro replaces the identifier, so the declaration reads int 5 = 3;.
  4. Undefined behaviour, because the macro and the variable conflict at run time.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The preprocessor runs before the compiler parses the program and has no notion of scope, so a macro name is replaced wherever it appears as a token, including in declarations.

Step by step.

  1. SIZE is defined as 5, so every occurrence of that token is replaced by 5.
  2. The declaration int SIZE = 3; therefore becomes int 5 = 3;.
  3. 5 is not a valid identifier, so the compiler reports a syntax error and no program is produced.
  4. A local declaration cannot shadow a macro: shadowing is a compile-time scope rule, and the substitution has already happened by then.

Answer: a compile-time error (option C).

Common trap. Expecting the local variable to win and printing 3. Macros are not variables and cannot be shadowed; the convention of upper-case macro names exists precisely to avoid this collision.

DS-U2B-Q25MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the C preprocessor are TRUE?

  1. #define performs textual substitution before compilation, so the preprocessor has no knowledge of C types.
  2. A macro definition may span several lines provided every line but the last ends with a backslash.
  3. #ifdef … #endif can remove code from the translation unit entirely, so the removed text need not even be valid C.
  4. sizeof applied to an expression containing a macro is evaluated by the preprocessor.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The preprocessor is a separate translation phase that works on tokens and lines; it knows nothing about types, values or sizes.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. It substitutes tokens, which is why a macro has no argument type checking and why #define MAX(a,b) can be passed anything at all.
  2. (B) TRUE. A backslash immediately before the newline splices the physical lines, so a long definition can be written over several lines.
  3. (C) TRUE. Conditional compilation happens before parsing, so the excluded text is never compiled and may be incomplete or ungrammatical.
  4. (D) FALSE. sizeof is an operator of the compiled language. The preprocessor expands the macro text and hands it on; the compiler then evaluates the size.

Answer: options (A), (B) and (C).

Common trap. Confusing #if with a run-time condition, or expecting the preprocessor to compute sizes. Anything that needs a type, a value or a size belongs to the compiler, not the preprocessor.

Review the 50 solved questions from Unit 3 — Linear Data Structures
Arrays & multidimensional arrays · 13
DS-U3-Q01MCQeasy

An array is declared as int A[5][8]; and stored in row-major order starting at byte address 1000. On a machine where int occupies 4 bytes, what is the byte address of A[3][2]?

  1. 1056
  2. 1104
  3. 1096
  4. 1108
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In row-major order the rows are laid out one after another, so the element A[i][j] of an m × n array sits at element offset i × n + j from the base, and the byte offset is that number times the element size.

Step by step.

  1. Here m = 5 and n = 8, so a row holds 8 integers.
  2. The offset in elements is 3 × 8 + 2 = 26.
  3. At 4 bytes each, that is 26 × 4 = 104 bytes.
  4. Address = 1000 + 104 = 1104.

Final answer. 1104 (option B)

Common trap. Using the row count (5) instead of the column count (8) gives 3 × 5 + 2 = 17 elements, or 68 bytes, and 1068 — a number that looks plausible but corresponds to a 5-column layout.

DS-U3-Q02MCQeasy

The array int A[3][4]; is stored in column-major order starting at byte address 2000, and int occupies 4 bytes. What is the address of A[2][1]?

  1. 2036
  2. 2012
  3. 2020
  4. 2044
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In column-major order the columns are stored one after another, so for an m × n array the element offset is j × m + i — the roles of the two indices swap relative to row-major order.

Step by step.

  1. m = 3 rows, so each column holds 3 integers.
  2. Offset in elements = 1 × 3 + 2 = 5.
  3. Byte offset = 5 × 4 = 20.
  4. Address = 2000 + 20 = 2020.

Final answer. 2020 (option C)

Common trap. Applying the row-major formula 2 × 4 + 1 = 9 gives 2036, which is offered as option (A). The layout convention has to be read from the question, not assumed.

DS-U3-Q03NATeasy

int A[5][6]; is stored in row-major order and int occupies 4 bytes. How many bytes apart are the starting addresses of A[2][0] and A[0][5]?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two elements of the same array differ in address by the difference of their element offsets, multiplied by the element size. The order of the two addresses does not matter, only the gap.

Step by step.

  1. Offset of A[2][0] = 2 × 6 + 0 = 12 elements.
  2. Offset of A[0][5] = 0 × 6 + 5 = 5 elements.
  3. Difference = 12 − 5 = 7 elements.
  4. 7 × 4 = 28 bytes.

Final answer. 28

Common trap. Subtracting the row and column indices separately, or using the number of rows (5) as the stride, gives 4 or 20. Only the flat element offset is meaningful.

DS-U3-Q04MCQmedium

The lower triangle, including the diagonal, of a 10 × 10 matrix is stored row by row in a one-dimensional array T beginning at index 0: row 0 first, then row 1, and so on. What is the index in T of the element A[7][3]?

  1. 32
  2. 28
  3. 34
  4. 31
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Row i of the lower triangle holds i + 1 entries, so the first element of row i sits at index 1 + 2 + … + i = i(i + 1)/2.

Step by step.

  1. Rows 0 to 6 together hold 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + 5 + 6 + 7 = 28 entries, occupying indices 0 to 27.
  2. Row 7 therefore begins at index 28.
  3. Within row 7 the entry with column index 3 is the fourth element, at offset 3.
  4. Index = 28 + 3 = 31.

Final answer. 31 (option D)

Common trap. Using 7 × 8 / 2 = 28 and stopping there treats the row start as the answer, giving 28 — option (B). The column offset inside the row still has to be added.

DS-U3-Q05MCQmedium

The array int A[2][3][4]; begins at byte address 0 and is stored in row-major order with int occupying 4 bytes. What is the byte address of A[1][2][3]?

  1. 92
  2. 88
  3. 96
  4. 80
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. For a three-dimensional array the row-major offset is built from the inside out: ((i × d₂ + j) × d₃ + k), where d₂ and d₃ are the second and third dimensions.

Step by step.

  1. d₂ = 3 and d₃ = 4.
  2. Inner index: 1 × 3 + 2 = 5 — the plane number.
  3. Element offset: 5 × 4 + 3 = 23.
  4. Byte address: 23 × 4 = 92.

Final answer. 92 (option A)

Common trap. Flattening the indices as 1 + 2 + 3 = 6, or as 1 × 3 × 4 + 2 × 4 + 3 = 23 — the second is right, the first gives 24 and 96, which is option (C).

DS-U3-Q06MSQmedium

For int A[3][4]; compiled as C, which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. In an expression such as A + 1, the name A is converted to a pointer of type int (*)[4].
  2. A[i][j] denotes the same object as *(*(A + i) + j).
  3. A and A[0] have the same type after the usual array-to-pointer conversion.
  4. sizeof(A[0]) is equal to sizeof(int).
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A two-dimensional array is an array of rows. The array name decays to a pointer to its first element, and its first element is a whole row, not a single int.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. A is an int[3][4], so its first element is an int[4] and the decayed type is int (*)[4]. Stepping A + 1 therefore moves by a whole row.
  2. (B) TRUE. That is the definition of subscripting: A[i] is *(A + i), a row, and a second subscript picks an element out of it.
  3. (C) FALSE. A decays to int (*)[4] while A[0] decays to int *. They hold the same address but have different types.
  4. (D) FALSE. A[0] is an int[4], so sizeof(A[0]) is 16 when an int is 4 bytes.

Final answer. options (A) and (B)

Common trap. Assuming that because A and A[0] print the same address they must have the same type. Equal addresses, different pointer types, different step sizes.

DS-U3-Q07MSQhard

For int A[2][3][4]; on a machine where int occupies 4 bytes, which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. A[1] has type int [3][4], and in an expression it decays to int (*)[4].
  2. A[0][1] decays to int (*)[4] in an expression.
  3. A + 1 differs from A by 48 bytes.
  4. sizeof(A[0]) is 8, the size of a pointer.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each subscript peels one dimension off the type: int[2][3][4] → int[3][4] → int[4] → int. The pointer produced by decay always points at one element of the peeled type.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. A[1] is a plane of type int[3][4]; its first element is an int[4], so the decayed pointer is int (*)[4].
  2. (B) FALSE. A[0][1] is already an int[4], so it decays to int *, not to a pointer to an array.
  3. (C) TRUE. A decays to int (*)[3][4], whose element is one plane: 3 × 4 × 4 = 48 bytes.
  4. (D) FALSE. A[0] is an int[3][4] object, and sizeof does not decay it: 3 × 4 × 4 = 48.

Final answer. options (A) and (C)

Common trap. Applying the decay rule inside sizeof. sizeof is one of the three contexts that suppress decay, so it always reports the whole array object.

DS-U3-Q26MCQeasy

An array is declared as int A[1..10][1..8]; — both subscripts are 1-based — and is stored in row-major order starting at byte address 100. Each int occupies 2 bytes. What is the byte address of A[5][3]?

  1. 152
  2. 160
  3. 168
  4. 176
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. In row-major order the row index moves in strides of one whole row, so the flat element offset of A[i][j] is (i − lo1) × n + (j − lo2), where lo1 and lo2 are the lower bounds and n is the number of columns. The lower bound must be subtracted; the count of columns, not the count of rows, is the stride.

Step by step.

  1. The row stride is the number of columns, 8, not the number of rows, 10.
  2. Element offset = (5 − 1) × 8 + (3 − 1) = 32 + 2 = 34.
  3. Byte offset = 34 × 2 = 68.
  4. Address = 100 + 68 = 168.

Common trap. Using the subscripts directly, 5 × 8 + 3 = 43, which silently treats the array as 0-based; or using 10 as the stride because 10 is the first bound written.

Takeaway. Write the offset formula with the lower bounds before touching numbers, then multiply by the element size once.

DS-U3-Q27MCQeasy

int A[4][5]; is stored in column-major order from byte address 500, and int occupies 4 bytes. What is the byte address of A[2][3]?

  1. 532
  2. 548
  3. 556
  4. 564
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Column-major order stores one whole column before the next, so the column index moves in strides of the number of rows: element offset = j × m + i for an m × n array. Row-major would use i × n + j.

Step by step.

  1. The stride here is the number of rows, 4.
  2. Element offset = 3 × 4 + 2 = 14.
  3. Byte offset = 14 × 4 = 56.
  4. Address = 500 + 56 = 556.

Common trap. Applying the row-major formula 2 × 5 + 3 = 13, which gives 552 — the wrong stride is the whole error.

Takeaway. Fix the layout first, then the stride: rows in the stride means column-major, columns in the stride means row-major.

DS-U3-Q28MCQmedium

On a 64-bit machine where int occupies 4 bytes and a pointer occupies 8 bytes, the declaration int A[4][5]; is compiled. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. sizeof(A) is 20
  2. sizeof(A[2]) is 20
  3. sizeof(&A[0]) is 20
  4. sizeof(A[0][0]) is 8
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. A is an array of 4 arrays of 5 int; A[2] is one of those inner arrays, of type int[5]; &A[0] is a pointer of type int (*)[5]. The sizeof operator does not let an array decay to a pointer, so it reports the whole object, while a pointer is reported by pointer width.

Step by step.

  1. sizeof(A) = 4 × 5 × 4 = 80 bytes, so (A) is false.
  2. sizeof(A[2]) = 5 × 4 = 20 bytes, so (B) is true.
  3. sizeof(&A[0]) is the size of a pointer, 8 bytes, so (C) is false.
  4. sizeof(A[0][0]) is the size of one int, 4 bytes, so (D) is false.

Common trap. Believing that A[2] has already decayed to a pointer inside sizeof. Decay happens when the array is used as a value in an expression, not as the operand of sizeof.

Takeaway. Read the type of the operand first: int[5] is 20 bytes, int * is 8.

DS-U3-Q29MCQhard

A 4 × 5 array of 4-byte elements begins at the same base address in two separate programs; one stores it in row-major order and the other in column-major order. Apart from A[0][0], which element occupies the same byte offset from the base in both layouts?

  1. A[1][4]
  2. A[2][2]
  3. A[3][4]
  4. A[4][3]
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Row-major offset is i × n + j and column-major offset is j × m + i. Equality of the two offsets is an equation in the indices, and it must be solved inside the legal index ranges, which are 0 ≤ i ≤ m − 1 and 0 ≤ j ≤ n − 1.

Step by step.

  1. With m = 4 and n = 5, set 5i + j = 4j + i.
  2. Rearrange: 4i = 3j.
  3. The solutions with i ≤ 3 and j ≤ 4 are (0, 0) and (3, 4).
  4. Excluding the base element, the answer is A[3][4].

Common trap. Choosing A[4][3] because it looks like the mirror of the right answer. The first index is a row and runs only to 3, so A[4][3] does not exist; it would be the answer only if the array were 5 × 4.

Takeaway. When an index equation has a symmetric-looking solution, check it against the declared bounds before believing it.

DS-U3-Q41MSQmedium

The lower triangle, diagonal included, of an n × n symmetric matrix is stored row by row in a one-dimensional array T with 1-based indexing, T[1] holding the element (1, 1). Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. For n = 10 the array needs 55 locations.
  2. For i ≥ j the element (i, j) is stored at T[i(i − 1)/2 + j].
  3. For n = 100 the array needs 5050 locations.
  4. This packing halves the storage requirement only when n is even.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B), (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A symmetric matrix satisfies A[i][j] = A[j][i], so the lower triangle carries all the information. Row i of the triangle holds i elements, so the total is 1 + 2 + … + n = n(n + 1)/2, and the element (i, j) with i ≥ j is preceded by all complete rows above it plus j − 1 elements of its own row.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. 10 × 11 / 2 = 55.
  2. (B) TRUE. Rows 1 to i − 1 hold i(i − 1)/2 elements, and (i, j) is the j-th in row i, so the 1-based position is i(i − 1)/2 + j. Check: (3, 2) gives 3 + 2 = 5, and the row layout is 1 | 2 3 | 4 5 6, so the fifth entry is indeed (3, 2).
  3. (C) TRUE. 100 × 101 / 2 = 5050.
  4. (D) FALSE. The saving is n2 − n(n + 1)/2 = n(n − 1)/2 entries for every n, and the ratio tends to one half as n grows. Parity plays no part.

Common trap. Checking only the closed-form count and forgetting to verify the index formula on an actual element, or assuming the ratio is exactly one half when it is only asymptotically one half.

Takeaway. For a packed triangular array, verify the index formula on a small element in the middle of the triangle.

DS-U3-Q46NATeasy

How many elements does the array int A[-2..5][3..7][1..4]; hold?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 160

Detailed solution

Concept. The number of values along one dimension is upper − lower + 1, and the total is the product of the three counts. Negative lower bounds are allowed and change only the count, not the address formula.

Step by step.

  1. First dimension: 5 − (−2) + 1 = 8.
  2. Second dimension: 7 − 3 + 1 = 5.
  3. Third dimension: 4 − 1 + 1 = 4.
  4. Total = 8 × 5 × 4 = 160.

Common trap. Using the upper bounds themselves as counts, 5 × 7 × 4 = 140, or 7 × 4 × 3 = 84 by dropping the negative sign.

Takeaway. Count per dimension with the +1, then multiply.

Singly, doubly & circular linked lists · 12
DS-U3-Q08MCQeasy

A singly linked list is maintained with a pointer to its first node only, and there is no tail pointer. Which of the following operations runs in Θ(1) time in the worst case?

  1. Inserting a new node at the end of the list.
  2. Deleting the last node of the list.
  3. Inserting a new node at the front of the list.
  4. Searching for a node that holds a given value.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A singly linked list gives constant-time access only to what a pointer already points at. Reaching any other position requires walking the next chain.

Step by step.

  1. (A) To append, the last node must be found, which costs Θ(n).
  2. (B) Deleting the last node needs its predecessor, so the walk is Θ(n). A tail pointer alone does not help: it points at the node to remove, not at the one before it.
  3. (C) Prepending rewrites the new node's next and the head pointer. Two assignments, Θ(1).
  4. (D) An unsorted list must be scanned, which is Θ(n).

Final answer. option (C)

Common trap. Believing that a tail pointer makes deletion at the tail Θ(1). It makes insertion at the tail Θ(1) but leaves deletion at Θ(n) unless the list is doubly linked or the node before the tail is tracked.

DS-U3-Q09MCQeasy

A circular singly linked list contains 9 nodes. Starting at any node and following the next pointer repeatedly, how many times must next be followed before the traversal first returns to the starting node?

  1. 9
  2. 8
  3. 10
  4. 1
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a circular list no next pointer is null; the last node points back to the first, so the chain is one cycle through every node.

Step by step.

  1. Every node has exactly one outgoing next, so the cycle has exactly 9 distinct nodes.
  2. Leaving the start node and stepping 9 times visits the other 8 nodes and lands back on the start.
  3. A count of 8 would return to the start only in a list that had a null terminator and 9 nodes, which is not circular.

Final answer. 9 (option A)

Common trap. Answering 8 by analogy with a linear list of 9 nodes, where following next 8 times reaches the last node. In a circular list the ninth step closes the loop.

DS-U3-Q10MCQmedium

The loop below reverses a singly linked list in place. For a list of 6 nodes, how many times is the assignment q->next = p; executed?

node *p = NULL, *q = head, *r; while (q != NULL) { r = q->next; q->next = p; p = q; q = r; }

  1. 5
  2. 7
  3. 6
  4. 12
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The loop body runs once per node: each iteration redirects exactly one node's next backwards. Reversal therefore performs one pointer reassignment per node.

Step by step.

  1. Iteration 1: q is the first node; its next becomes NULL.
  2. Iterations 2 to 6: each redirects one further node to its predecessor.
  3. After the sixth iteration q is NULL and the loop test fails, so there is no seventh execution.
  4. Total: 6 reassignments, and 6 is also the number of next fields in the list.

Final answer. 6 (option C)

Common trap. Answering 5 by analogy with the number of "gaps" between 6 nodes. Every node's next is rewritten, including the first, which becomes NULL.

DS-U3-Q11MCQmedium

A singly linked list contains the nodes 1 → 2 → 3 → 4 → 5 → 6 → 7, and the last node points back to node 4. A slow pointer advances one node per step and a fast pointer advances two nodes per step; both start at node 1. After how many steps do they first meet, and at which node?

  1. 3 steps, at node 4
  2. 4 steps, at node 5
  3. 5 steps, at node 6
  4. They never meet
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Once the fast pointer is inside the cycle it gains exactly one node per step on the slow pointer, so the two must meet. The meeting point is found by simulating the two walks on the actual cycle.

Step by step.

  1. Step 0: slow at 1, fast at 1.
  2. Step 1: slow 1→2, fast 1→3.
  3. Step 2: slow 2→3, fast 3→5.
  4. Step 3: slow 3→4, fast 5→7.
  5. Step 4: slow 4→5, fast 7→4→5. Both are now at node 5.

Final answer. 4 steps, at node 5 (option B)

Common trap. Counting the steps of the slow pointer only up to the entry node (3 steps) and declaring a meeting at node 4. The pointers meet after the slow pointer has entered the cycle, not at its entry.

DS-U3-Q12MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about linked lists are TRUE?

  1. Reversing a singly linked list in place takes Θ(n) time and Θ(1) extra space.
  2. In a doubly linked list, deleting a node given only a pointer to that node takes Θ(1) time.
  3. A circular doubly linked list with n nodes has 2n link fields.
  4. With both a head and a tail pointer, deleting the last node of a singly linked list takes Θ(1) time.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The cost of a linked-list operation is decided by which nodes it must reach, and whether the structure stores a pointer to them.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. The three-pointer loop touches each node once and uses a fixed number of variables, so time is Θ(n) and space is Θ(1).
  2. (B) TRUE. The node's own prev and next give both neighbours directly; four assignments unlink it.
  3. (C) TRUE. Each of the n nodes carries a prev and a next, so there are 2n links, and in a circular list none of them is null.
  4. (D) FALSE. The tail pointer identifies the node to delete but not its predecessor, so the list must still be walked to find the node before the tail: Θ(n).

Final answer. options (A), (B) and (C)

Common trap. Concluding that a tail pointer upgrades every tail operation to constant time. It only helps insertion, because insertion needs the old tail, whereas deletion needs the node before it.

DS-U3-Q13NAThard

Seven people numbered 1 to 7 stand in a circle and are stored in a circular singly linked list in that order. Counting begins at person 1 and proceeds 1, 2, 1, 2, …; every second person is eliminated, and counting restarts at the next surviving person. What is the number of the last survivor?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. This is the Josephus problem with k = 2 on a circular list. Because elimination always skips one surviving node and removes the next, the list shrinks by one node per round and the circle closes up behind the removed node.

Step by step.

  1. Circle 1 2 3 4 5 6 7, counting from 1: 1 is counted first, 2 is second and is eliminated. The circle is now 1 3 4 5 6 7 and counting resumes at 3.
  2. From 3: 3 counted first, 4 second, so 4 is eliminated. Circle 1 3 5 6 7, resume at 5.
  3. From 5: 5 first, 6 second, so 6 is eliminated. Circle 1 3 5 7, resume at 7.
  4. From 7: 7 first, 1 second, so 1 is eliminated. Circle 3 5 7, resume at 3.
  5. From 3: 3 first, 5 second, so 5 is eliminated. Circle 3 7, resume at 7.
  6. From 7: 7 first, 3 second, so 3 is eliminated. Only 7 remains.

Final answer. 7

Common trap. Restarting the count at the node after the eliminated one instead of the node after the one that was just counted. In this variant the survivor after two eliminations is 7, whereas the more common "eliminate the first, restart at the next" convention gives 4 for seven people.

DS-U3-Q30MCQeasy

In a doubly linked list, p points to a node that is neither the first nor the last. That node must be deleted, and p is the only pointer available — there is no head pointer and no size field. How many link fields must be rewritten?

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. 4
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. A doubly linked node is held from both sides, so removing it requires telling both neighbours about each other: p->prev->next = p->next and p->next->prev = p->prev.

Step by step.

  1. Set p->prev->next = p->next — the predecessor skips over p.
  2. Set p->next->prev = p->prev — the successor skips back over p.
  3. Two fields are rewritten; the fields inside p become unreachable and need no repair before the node is freed.

Common trap. Answering 1 by analogy with a singly linked list, where deleting a node with only a pointer to its predecessor costs one field. Here p itself is the handle, and both sides must be fixed.

Takeaway. Count the neighbours a node is referenced from, not the number of pointers you hold.

DS-U3-Q31MCQeasy

A singly linked list is maintained with a head pointer only — there is no tail pointer and no size field. What is the worst-case time to append a new node at the end of a list of n nodes?

  1. Θ(1)
  2. Θ(log n)
  3. Θ(n)
  4. Θ(n log n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Insertion into a linked list is constant time only when the predecessor is already in hand. With a head pointer alone the last node is not addressable, so it must be found by walking.

Step by step.

  1. Start at the head and follow next until the node whose next is NULL.
  2. That walk touches all n nodes.
  3. The link assignment itself is one operation, but the search dominates: Θ(n).
  4. Keeping a tail pointer makes the same append Θ(1), because the old last node is then directly available.

Common trap. "Insertion in a linked list is O(1)" is a statement about insertion at a known position, usually the head. Without a tail pointer the position is not known.

Takeaway. A linked list buys O(1) insertion at the ends only if you keep pointers to the ends.

DS-U3-Q32MCQmedium

Binary search is run on a sorted singly linked list of n nodes. Compared with the same algorithm on a sorted array, its running time is:

  1. Θ(log n)
  2. Θ(√n)
  3. Θ(n)
  4. Θ(n log n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Each probe of binary search needs random access to the middle of the current range. An array gives that in Θ(1); a linked list gives it only after a walk from the start of the range, which costs time proportional to the range length.

Step by step.

  1. The first probe walks about n/2 nodes, the next about n/4, and so on.
  2. The recurrence is T(n) = T(n/2) + Θ(n), whose solution is Θ(n).
  3. The number of comparisons is still Θ(log n); the traversal cost is what changes the bound.
  4. So the list version is Θ(n), no better than one linear scan.

Common trap. Counting only comparisons and concluding Θ(log n). The bound must cover every operation the algorithm performs, including the ones that reach a position.

Takeaway. A good asymptotic bound counts the cost of reaching data, not just the cost of comparing it.

DS-U3-Q33MCQhard

The function below is called with the head of a singly linked list that holds 10 nodes and contains no cycle. What does it return?

int count(struct node *h) { struct node *p = h, *q = h; int c = 0; while (p != NULL && p->next != NULL) { p = p->next->next; q = q->next; c++; } return c; }

  1. 4
  2. 5
  3. 6
  4. 10
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The loop condition protects two dereferences: p must exist and p->next must exist. So the loop body runs while p is one of the first n − 1 nodes, and each iteration advances p by two nodes.

Step by step.

  1. Iteration 1: p moves from node 1 to node 3, c = 1.
  2. Iteration 2: p 3 → 5, c = 2.
  3. Iteration 3: p 5 → 7, c = 3.
  4. Iteration 4: p 7 → 9, c = 4.
  5. Iteration 5: node 9 still has a successor, so the body runs again and p becomes NULL, c = 5. The condition now fails on p != NULL.
  6. The function returns 5, which is n/2 for n = 10.

Common trap. Stopping at c = 4 by assuming the loop ends when p reaches the last node. It ends one iteration later, when p steps past it, because node 9 does have a successor.

Takeaway. Trace the guard, not the intent: p->next != NULL allows one more jump from the last node.

DS-U3-Q42MSQmedium

A circular doubly linked list of n nodes is maintained with a head pointer that is never NULL. Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. The last node's next field points to the head node.
  2. head->prev points to the last node.
  3. Walking forward from the head with next pointers returns to the head after exactly n steps.
  4. Inserting a new node immediately before the head requires rewriting exactly two link fields.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B), (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. In a circular list the ring is closed in both directions: the last node's next is the head and the head's prev is the last node. In a doubly linked structure every node is referenced from two sides, so an insertion or a deletion touches both neighbours plus the new node's own two fields.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. That link is what makes the list circular.
  2. (B) TRUE. The backward ring mirrors the forward one.
  3. (C) TRUE. Each step visits one new node, and after n steps the walk has returned to the starting node.
  4. (D) FALSE. Four fields change: new->next, new->prev, head->prev->next and head->prev.

Common trap. Counting only the two writes into the new node and forgetting the two neighbours, or thinking (C) needs n + 1 steps because the walk returns to the head "after" the last node.

Takeaway. Count fields by counting references: a doubly linked insertion touches four.

DS-U3-Q47NATeasy

A circular singly linked list has 12 nodes, and each node stores only data and next. You are given a pointer p to one node, but not the head. What is the minimum number of next-pointer steps needed to reach the node whose next field points to p?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 11

Detailed solution

Concept. In a circular list every node is reachable from every other node by following next. The predecessor of p is the last distinct node on the way round, so it is reached after n − 1 steps, and no shorter walk exists because a singly linked list cannot go backwards.

Step by step.

  1. Start at p, which is step 0.
  2. Following next visits the 11 other nodes in turn.
  3. The eleventh distinct node visited has next == p, so it is the predecessor.
  4. Answer: 11 steps.

Common trap. Answering 12 by counting the return to p itself, which is one step beyond the predecessor.

Takeaway. On a ring of n nodes the predecessor of a node is n − 1 steps ahead, and the node itself is n steps ahead.

Stacks: infix, postfix & applications · 13
DS-U3-Q14MCQeasy

What is the postfix form of the infix expression A + B * C?

  1. A B + C *
  2. A B C + *
  3. A B C * +
  4. A + B C *
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Postfix notation places each operator immediately after its two operands, so the higher-precedence operation is completed first and no parentheses are needed.

Step by step.

  1. Multiplication binds tighter than addition, so the expression groups as A + (B * C).
  2. Convert the inner product: B C *.
  3. The product is now the right operand of +: A (B C *) +.
  4. Removing the brackets gives A B C * +.

Final answer. A B C * + (option C)

Common trap. Reading the expression strictly left to right gives A B + C *, which computes (A + B) * C — a different value.

DS-U3-Q15MCQeasy

The postfix expression 6 2 / 1 + 3 * is evaluated with a stack. What is the result?

  1. 9
  2. 15
  3. 3
  4. 12
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Postfix evaluation pushes operands and, on each operator, pops the right operand first and then the left one, applies the operator, and pushes the result.

Step by step.

  1. Push 6, push 2. Read /: pop 2 (right) and 6 (left), compute 6 / 2 = 3, push 3. Stack: [3].
  2. Push 1. Read +: pop 1 and 3, compute 3 + 1 = 4, push 4. Stack: [4].
  3. Push 3. Read *: pop 3 and 4, compute 4 × 3 = 12. Stack: [12].
  4. The expression is exhausted, so the single remaining value is the answer.

Final answer. 12 (option D)

Common trap. Popping in the wrong order on the division and computing 2 / 6. The right operand is on top of the stack, so it must be popped first.

DS-U3-Q16NATeasy

Evaluate the postfix expression 5 3 + 8 2 - *. Give the integer result.

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each binary operator consumes the two most recent values on the stack, so the operands of an operator are the two subexpressions immediately to its left.

Step by step.

  1. Push 5, push 3. Read +: 5 + 3 = 8. Stack: [8].
  2. Push 8, push 2. Read -: 8 − 2 = 6. Stack: [8, 6].
  3. Read *: 8 × 6 = 48. Stack: [48].

Final answer. 48

Common trap. Subtracting in the order the numbers appear, 2 − 8 = −6, which yields −48. Subtraction is not commutative and the top of the stack is the right operand.

DS-U3-Q17MCQmedium

What is the postfix form of the infix expression (A + B) * (C - D) + E?

  1. A B + C D - * E +
  2. A B + C D * - E +
  3. A B + C D - E + *
  4. A B C + D - * E +
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Converting with a stack: operands are emitted at once; an operator is emitted only after every operator of higher or equal precedence above it has been popped; a closing bracket pops up to its matching opening bracket.

Step by step.

  1. (A + B) becomes A B +; the bracket disappears once it closes.
  2. (C - D) becomes C D -.
  3. The * between the two brackets combines them: A B + C D - *.
  4. The final + E has lower precedence, so it is appended last: A B + C D - * E +.

Final answer. A B + C D - * E + (option A)

Common trap. Option (C) moves the * past the E +, which would compute (A + B) * ((C - D) + E) instead of adding E to the product.

DS-U3-Q18MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about stacks and their applications are TRUE?

  1. In an array-based stack, a push is rejected when the stack already holds as many elements as the array can store, and that test is made before the write.
  2. While converting an infix expression to postfix, the operator stack can never hold more than two operators.
  3. A closing bracket that arrives when the bracket stack is empty proves the expression is not balanced.
  4. Two stacks stored in one array of size n, growing towards each other from the two ends, can hold at most n − 1 items in total.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A stack is a last-in-first-out store with a single access point, so every implementation has to guard its boundary conditions: overflow at the top of the capacity and underflow at the bottom.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. The capacity test must precede the write; writing first would overrun the array.
  2. (B) FALSE. Nesting depth, not operator count, bounds the stack: A + (B + (C + (D + E))) leaves four operators pending at once.
  3. (C) TRUE. A closing bracket with nothing to match is exactly the unbalanced case the stack detects.
  4. (D) FALSE. When the two top indices become adjacent the array is full, so all n cells can be used.

Final answer. options (A) and (C)

Common trap. Thinking a shared array must waste one cell, as a circular queue does. The two stacks meet, and they can meet at the last free cell, so nothing is wasted.

DS-U3-Q19MCQhard

The integers 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 are pushed onto a stack in that order, each exactly once, and may be popped at any time. Which of the following output sequences cannot be produced?

  1. 2 3 1 5 4
  2. 3 1 2 5 4
  3. 4 5 3 2 1
  4. 5 4 3 2 1
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Whatever is pushed last must be popped first. So if a larger number is still sitting on the stack when a smaller one is popped, the larger one must come out before any number that was pushed before it.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Push 1, 2; pop 2. Push 3; pop 3. Pop 1. Push 4, 5; pop 5, pop 4. Gives 2 3 1 5 4.
  2. (B) To print 3 first, push 1, 2, 3 and pop 3. The next output must be 1, but 2 sits on top of 1 and cannot be passed. Printing 1 would require popping 2 first, which contradicts the sequence. Impossible.
  3. (C) Push 1, 2, 3, 4; pop 4. Push 5; pop 5. Pop 3, 2, 1. Gives 4 5 3 2 1.
  4. (D) Push all five, then pop all five.

Final answer. 3 1 2 5 4 (option B)

Common trap. Checking only that the first element is reachable. The obstruction in (B) appears at the second element, where the stack top blocks access to the value required next.

DS-U3-Q34MCQeasy

Which one of the following is not an application of a stack?

  1. Converting an infix expression to postfix
  2. Checking whether the parentheses in an expression are balanced
  3. Round-robin CPU scheduling
  4. Evaluating a postfix expression
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A stack is last-in-first-out and is used wherever the most recent unfinished item must be resumed first — nested brackets, nested calls, the rightmost pending operator. Round-robin scheduling keeps processes in arrival order and resumes the oldest one, which is first-in-first-out.

Step by step.

  1. Infix to postfix: the pending operators are held on a stack and the most recent one is popped first.
  2. Bracket matching: each closing bracket must match the most recently opened one, a LIFO match.
  3. Postfix evaluation: the two most recently seen operands are consumed by each operator.
  4. Round-robin scheduling: processes leave in the order they arrived, which is a queue.

Common trap. Treating every "data structure" answer as interchangeable. The test is the order of resumption: LIFO is a stack, FIFO is a queue.

Takeaway. Ask which item must be served first; the answer names the structure.

DS-U3-Q35MCQmedium

The postfix expression A B C + * D - is equivalent to which one of the following infix expressions?

  1. A * B + C - D
  2. (A * (B + C)) - D
  3. A * (B + (C - D))
  4. ((A * B) + C) - D
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Postfix is read left to right; operands are pushed and an operator pops the two most recent operands, taking the second popped as its left operand. The parenthesisation is therefore fixed by the order of the operators, not by any convention about precedence.

Step by step.

  1. Push A, B, C.
  2. The first operator is +: it pops C then B, giving the subexpression (B + C).
  3. The next operator is *: it pops (B + C) then A, giving (A * (B + C)).
  4. The last operator is -: it pops D then (A * (B + C)), giving (A * (B + C)) - D.

Common trap. Reading the trailing D - as D subtracted from the whole, or as (C − D) — the second operand popped is the left operand, and D is the second operand here.

Takeaway. In postfix conversion the operator always takes the last two things on the stack, in the order they were pushed.

DS-U3-Q36MCQmedium

Two stacks share a single array of 20 slots, indexed 0 to 19. Stack 1 grows upward from index 0 and its top is currently at index 8; stack 2 grows downward from index 19 and its top is currently at index 10. How many more pushes can be accepted before an overflow?

  1. 0
  2. 1
  3. 2
  4. 11
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The two stacks collide when the top of the upward stack and the top of the downward stack become adjacent. Every index strictly between the two tops is free, so the free count is top2 − top1 − 1, and the overflow test is top1 + 1 == top2.

Step by step.

  1. Stack 1 occupies indices 0 to 8; stack 2 occupies indices 10 to 19.
  2. Index 9 is the only slot that belongs to neither stack.
  3. Free slots = 10 − 8 − 1 = 1.
  4. One more push fills index 9 and the next one overflows.

Common trap. Computing 19 − 8 = 11 and forgetting that the eleven indices above stack 1 are mostly occupied by stack 2. Only the gap between the two tops is available.

Takeaway. In a shared array the capacity is the gap between the tops, not the distance from one top to the end of the array.

DS-U3-Q37MCQhard

The postfix expression 3 4 2 * 1 5 - 2 3 ^ ^ / + is evaluated with a stack. What is the maximum number of elements held on the stack at any instant?

  1. 3
  2. 4
  3. 5
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. While scanning postfix, a stack element is added by every operand and removed twice and replaced once by every binary operator. The peak depth therefore depends on where the operands cluster, and must be read off a trace rather than guessed from the number of operands.

Step by step.

  1. 3 → depth 1; 4 → 2; 2 → 3.
  2. * pops two and pushes one → depth 2 (stack 3, 8).
  3. 1 → 3; 5 → 4; - → 3 (stack 3, 8, −4).
  4. 2 → 4; 3 → 5 — this is the peak.
  5. The remaining operators only reduce the depth: ^ → 4, ^ → 3, / → 2, + → 1.

Common trap. Reporting 4 by forgetting that 2 and 3 are both pushed before the second ^ is applied, or counting the final single result.

Takeaway. For a peak-depth question, write the depth after every token; never estimate from the number of operators.

DS-U3-Q43MSQmedium

A stack is implemented with a singly linked list in which every push inserts at the head and every pop removes the head. Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. push runs in Θ(1) worst-case time.
  2. pop runs in Θ(1) worst-case time.
  3. Pushing at the tail would also run in Θ(1) without a tail pointer.
  4. The stack can overflow only when memory is exhausted.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B), (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The head of a singly linked list is the only position that is directly addressable, which is exactly what a stack needs: the top must be reachable in constant time. A linked implementation has no fixed capacity, so the classical "stack overflow" of an array-backed stack becomes allocation failure.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. Inserting at the head rewrites one pointer.
  2. (B) TRUE. Removing the head rewrites one pointer and frees one node.
  3. (C) FALSE. Without a tail pointer, reaching the last node costs a walk of Θ(n).
  4. (D) TRUE. The size is limited by available memory, not by a declared capacity.

Common trap. Carrying the array's overflow notion over to a linked stack, or assuming the tail is as cheap as the head.

Takeaway. A linked stack is the head of a list; that single fact answers cost, capacity and correctness together.

DS-U3-Q48NATmedium

The infix expression A - B + C * (D - E) + F is converted to postfix with the standard operator-stack algorithm. Counting only operators that are popped from the stack — the parenthesis that is discarded on ) is not counted — how many pops occur in total, including the flush of the stack at the end?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 5

Detailed solution

Concept. The algorithm pops while the operator on top of the stack has precedence greater than or equal to the incoming operator, and pops everything down to the matching ( when a ) arrives. Equal precedence pops too, because subtraction and addition are left-associative.

Step by step.

  1. - is pushed; on + the top - has equal precedence, so it is popped (1).
  2. * is pushed above +; ( is pushed; - is pushed inside the bracket.
  3. On ) the inner - is popped (2); the ( is then discarded and is not an operator.
  4. On the second +: * is popped (3) and then + is popped (4).
  5. At the end the remaining + is popped (5). Total 5.

Common trap. Forgetting the flush at the end (giving 4), or forgetting that equal precedence pops, so that the first + leaves the - on the stack (giving 3).

Takeaway. Trace the stack token by token, and remember that left-associative operators pop their equals.

DS-U3-Q49NAThard

The integers 1, 2, 3, 4 are pushed onto an initially empty stack in that order, each exactly once, and elements may be popped at any time, including between pushes. How many different output sequences can be produced?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 14

Detailed solution

Concept. The number of distinct permutations of 1 … n obtainable from a single stack with a fixed input order is the n-th Catalan number Cn = (1/(n+1)) × binom(2n, n). Each push/pop schedule corresponds to a balanced parenthesis string of length 2n.

Step by step.

  1. For n = 4: binom(8, 4) = 70.
  2. Divide by n + 1 = 5: 70 / 5 = 14.
  3. Sanity check by enumeration: starting from 1 and repeatedly either pushing the next integer or popping, exactly 14 distinct output orders arise; for example 4, 3, 2, 1 is possible and 4, 1, 2, 3 is not.

Common trap. Answering 4! = 24 by assuming every permutation is reachable, or 23 = 8 by counting the choice points instead of the outcomes. Permutations such as 3, 1, 2, 4 are impossible.

Takeaway. Stack-ordered permutations are counted by Catalan numbers, not by factorials.

Queues, circular queues & deques · 12
DS-U3-Q20MCQeasy

A circular queue uses an array of size 8 with indices 0 to 7. Both front and rear start at 0, the queue is empty exactly when front == rear, and every enqueue executes rear = (rear + 1) % 8. After 5 enqueues and no dequeue, what is the value of rear?

  1. 5
  2. 4
  3. 6
  4. 0
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a circular queue the indices wrap modulo the array size, and rear always designates the next cell to be written, not the last one written.

Step by step.

  1. Start: rear = 0.
  2. Five enqueues increment it five times: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5.
  3. No value reaches 8, so the modulo never wraps.
  4. rear = 5, and the five stored elements occupy cells 0 to 4.

Final answer. 5 (option A)

Common trap. Reporting 4 by treating rear as the index of the last element stored. Here it is the next free cell, which is why front == rear can mean empty.

DS-U3-Q21MCQmedium

Using the same circular queue (array size 8, front = rear = 0, rear = (rear + 1) % 8 on enqueue, front = (front + 1) % 8 on dequeue): after 5 enqueues, then 3 dequeues, then 4 enqueues, what is the value of rear?

  1. 0
  2. 2
  3. 5
  4. 1
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Dequeues move only front; rear is advanced by enqueues alone, modulo the array size. Tracking the two indices separately is what keeps the circular buffer correct.

Step by step.

  1. After 5 enqueues: front = 0, rear = 5, five elements in cells 0 to 4.
  2. After 3 dequeues: front = 3, rear = 5, two elements in cells 3 and 4.
  3. Four enqueues advance rear: 5 → 6 → 7 → 0 → 1, wrapping once at the end of the array.
  4. Final state: front = 3, rear = 1, holding 6 elements — within the capacity of 7.

Final answer. 1 (option D)

Common trap. Answering 0 by assuming the queue restarts from index 0 after the dequeue, or 5 by forgetting that the last enqueue wraps. The modulo applies to every single increment.

DS-U3-Q22NATeasy

A circular queue is implemented in an array of 10 slots, and one slot is deliberately left unused so that front == rear can stand for the empty state. What is the largest number of elements the queue can hold?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. With only the two indices, the states "empty" and "full" would both show front == rear. Sacrificing one slot keeps the two conditions distinguishable without a counter.

Step by step.

  1. Empty is front == rear.
  2. Full is declared one step short: (rear + 1) % 10 == front.
  3. So rear may never catch up with front, and at most 9 of the 10 cells can be occupied.

Final answer. 9

Common trap. Answering 10 by counting the array cells. Allowing all 10 makes full indistinguishable from empty, and the queue would report an empty buffer while holding 10 elements.

DS-U3-Q23NAThard

A queue is built from two stacks S1 and S2. Enqueue pushes onto S1. Dequeue pops from S2; if S2 is empty, every element of S1 is first popped and pushed onto S2, and then one element is popped from S2. Starting from empty stacks, the operations enqueue(1), enqueue(2), enqueue(3), dequeue, enqueue(4), dequeue, dequeue, dequeue are performed. Counting pushes onto S1 and onto S2 together, how many push operations are executed in total?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each element is pushed onto S1 once when it arrives, and is pushed onto S2 at most once, when the transfer is triggered. So the total is bounded by 2 per element, and the exact count follows the transfer points.

Step by step.

  1. enqueue(1), enqueue(2), enqueue(3): three pushes onto S1. Running total 3. S1 holds [1, 2, 3] bottom to top; S2 empty.
  2. dequeue: S2 is empty, so three elements move from S1 to S2 — three more pushes. Total 6. S2 holds [3, 2, 1] with 1 on top; pop returns 1.
  3. enqueue(4): one push onto S1. Total 7.
  4. dequeue: S2 is not empty, so no transfer; pop returns 2. Total stays 7.
  5. dequeue: pop returns 3. Total stays 7.
  6. dequeue: S2 is now empty, so the single element of S1 moves across — one push. Total 8. Pop returns 4.

Final answer. 8

Common trap. Charging a transfer on every dequeue, which would give 6 pushes to S2 instead of 4. The transfer happens only when S2 is empty, which is why the amortised cost of a dequeue stays constant.

DS-U3-Q24MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about queues and deques are TRUE?

  1. In a circular queue of array size n that leaves one slot unused, the number of stored elements is (rear − front + n) % n.
  2. A deque can provide both stack behaviour and queue behaviour without any additional structure.
  3. A circular queue that does not leave a slot unused can still distinguish a full queue from an empty one without a counter or a flag.
  4. A queue implemented as a singly linked list with head and tail pointers supports enqueue and dequeue in Θ(1) time.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The number of elements in a circular buffer is the forward distance from front to rear, taken modulo the array size; and a deque exposes both ends, which subsumes both restricted containers.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. The modulo keeps the count right when rear has wrapped past front. For front = 3, rear = 1, n = 8 it gives (1 − 3 + 8) % 8 = 6.
  2. (B) TRUE. Push and pop at the same end behave as a stack; push at one end and pop at the other behave as a queue.
  3. (C) FALSE. Without a spare slot, a full buffer and an empty one both satisfy front == rear, so a counter or flag is required.
  4. (D) TRUE. Enqueue links the new node after the tail; dequeue unlinks the head. Both touch a constant number of pointers.

Final answer. options (A), (B) and (D)

Common trap. Believing a deque is only a queue with extra features. Restricting the operations turns it into either a stack or a queue, which is why deques are used as the general building block.

DS-U3-Q25MCQhard

The integers 1 to 6 are placed in a queue in that order. The following is repeated until the queue is empty: dequeue an element and print it, then dequeue the next element and enqueue it at the rear. What is the fourth number printed?

  1. 4
  2. 2
  3. 6
  4. 8
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each round removes two elements from the front and puts the second one back at the rear, so the queue loses exactly one element per round while the survivors rotate.

Step by step.

  1. Round 1: print 1, move 2 to the rear. Queue: 3 4 5 6 2.
  2. Round 2: print 3, move 4 to the rear. Queue: 5 6 2 4.
  3. Round 3: print 5, move 6 to the rear. Queue: 2 4 6.
  4. Round 4: print 2, move 4 to the rear. The fourth number printed is 2.
  5. For completeness the run ends with print 6 (move 4), then print 4: the order is 1 3 5 2 6 4.

Final answer. 2 (option B)

Common trap. Printing the odd positions 1, 3, 5 and then assuming the evens follow as 2, 4, 6 in order. The moved element goes behind the untouched ones, so 2 is printed before 6 and 4.

DS-U3-Q38MCQeasy

Which of the following supports insertion and deletion at both ends in Θ(1) worst-case time?

  1. A singly linked list with a head pointer only
  2. An array with a single front index
  3. A doubly linked list with head and tail pointers
  4. A binary min-heap
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A deque needs a handle on both ends, and every node on the end being changed must have a pointer to its inward neighbour. A doubly linked list supplies that from either side; a singly linked list does not, because a node has no way back.

Step by step.

  1. Singly linked list with head only: removal at the tail needs a full walk, Θ(n).
  2. Array with one front index: removing at the front forces a shift, Θ(n).
  3. Doubly linked list with head and tail: each end operation rewrites a constant number of pointers, Θ(1).
  4. A binary heap supports the minimum only, and its other end is not a deque end.

Common trap. Assuming a singly linked list with a tail pointer would do. A tail pointer finds the last node but cannot reach its predecessor, so tail deletion still costs a walk.

Takeaway. A double-ended structure needs backward links, not just end pointers.

DS-U3-Q39MCQmedium

A circular array of 8 slots (indices 0 to 7) implements a queue in which one slot is always left unused. front is the index of the first element, count is the number of elements, and rear is the index of the next free slot. With front = 3 and count = 5, what is rear?

  1. 0
  2. 1
  3. 7
  4. 8
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. In a circular array every index is reduced modulo the array size. Since count elements occupy the slots front, front + 1, …, front + count − 1, the next free slot is (front + count) mod 8.

Step by step.

  1. front + count = 3 + 5 = 8.
  2. 8 mod 8 = 0.
  3. Check the occupancy: the five elements sit in slots 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, so the next free slot is 0 — the queue has wrapped exactly once.

Common trap. Answering 8, which is not an index of an array of 8 slots. The wrap is the whole point of the circular representation.

Takeaway. Reduce modulo the size before interpreting an index, never after.

DS-U3-Q40MCQeasy

A deque is initially empty. The operations push_back(1), push_back(2), push_back(3), push_back(4), push_back(5), pop_front(), pop_front(), push_front(6), pop_back(), pop_back() are performed in that order. What remains, from front to back?

  1. 3, 4
  2. 6, 3
  3. 6, 4
  4. 4, 5
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution
