Concept. A deque has four distinct ends: push_front and push_back add at opposite ends, and pop_front and pop_back remove from opposite ends. Keeping the front and the back apart is the only thing the question tests.

Step by step.

  1. After the five push_back calls: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5.
  2. Two pop_front calls remove 1 and 2: 3, 4, 5.
  3. push_front(6) adds at the front: 6, 3, 4, 5.
  4. Two pop_back calls remove 5 and 4: 6, 3.

Common trap. Treating push_front(6) as an append, which leaves 3, 4, 5, 6 and, after two pops at the back, 3, 4.

Takeaway. Write the deque out after each operation; four ends are easy to confuse in the head.

DS-U3-Q44MSQhard

The maximum of every window of size k in an array of n values is computed with a deque that stores indices. Before each insertion, the indices at the back whose values are less than or equal to the incoming value are removed; the index at the front is removed once it has left the window. Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. The deque holds at most k indices at any time.
  2. The values at the indices in the deque increase from front to back.
  3. Each index enters the deque once and leaves it at most once, so the total work is Θ(n).
  4. The front of the deque always holds the index of the maximum of the current window.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (C), (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The deque keeps only the indices that can still become a window maximum: a value that is smaller and older than a later value can never be the answer again, so it is discarded at the back. What survives is a decreasing sequence of values whose indices increase — a monotone deque.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. Only indices inside the current window are retained, and the window holds k positions.
  2. (B) FALSE. The values decrease from front to back: the front is the largest. Any value greater than its predecessor would have evicted that predecessor at the back.
  3. (C) TRUE. Every index is pushed once, and each index is removed at most once, so the amortised cost per step is constant and the total is Θ(n).
  4. (D) TRUE. Discarding smaller values at the back and expired indices at the front leaves exactly the largest value of the window at the front.

Common trap. Reading the monotonicity in the wrong direction, or thinking the deque costs Θ(nk) because the removals look like extra work.

Takeaway. In an amortised structure, count how often each element can enter and leave, not how many operations one step may perform.

DS-U3-Q45MSQeasy

Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. A queue returns elements in the reverse of the order in which they were inserted.
  2. A deque allows insertion and deletion at both ends.
  3. In a circular queue, no element has to be shifted when an element is removed from the front.
  4. A queue built on a singly linked list with both head and tail pointers enqueues in Θ(n) time.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (B), (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A queue is first-in-first-out: the element inserted earliest leaves first. A circular representation removes the shifting that a plain array would need, and a tail pointer makes the enqueue a constant-time operation.

Step by step.

  1. (A) FALSE. Reversal is the behaviour of a stack, not a queue.
  2. (B) TRUE. That is the definition of a double-ended queue.
  3. (C) TRUE. The front index simply advances modulo the array size.
  4. (D) FALSE. With a tail pointer the insertion is Θ(1).

Common trap. Confusing FIFO with LIFO in the first statement, or assuming that any linked-list operation is linear.

Takeaway. Queue means FIFO; the data structure chosen to implement it should not change that.

DS-U3-Q50NATmedium

A queue is built from two stacks S1 and S2. Enqueue pushes onto S1. Dequeue pops from S2, and only when S2 is empty is every element of S1 popped and pushed onto S2 before the pop. Starting from empty, the operations are: enqueue 1, enqueue 2, enqueue 3, dequeue, enqueue 4, dequeue, dequeue. How many elements remain in the queue afterwards?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 1

Detailed solution

Concept. The transfer of S1 into S2 reverses the order twice — once going in and once coming out — which is exactly the FIFO behaviour a queue needs. The elements are split between the two stacks at any moment, so the queue's size is |S1| + |S2|.

Step by step.

  1. Enqueue 1, 2, 3: S1 = [1, 2, 3] with 3 on top, S2 empty.
  2. Dequeue: transfer gives S2 = [3, 2, 1] with 1 on top, then pop returns 1. S2 = [3, 2].
  3. Enqueue 4: S1 = [4].
  4. Dequeue returns 2 from S2; dequeue returns 3. S2 is now empty.
  5. Remaining: S1 = [4], so 1 element.

Common trap. Forgetting that enqueue goes to S1 while S2 still holds elements, which leads to counting 2, or assuming the transfer happens on every dequeue.

Takeaway. Track both stacks separately; the queue is their union, and the transfer happens only when S2 is empty.

Review the 50 solved questions from Unit 4 — Binary Trees & Tree Traversals
Binary tree properties · 14
DS-U4-Q01MCQeasy

In a binary tree the height of a node is the number of edges on the longest downward path from that node to a leaf, and the height of the tree is the height of the root. What are the minimum and the maximum number of nodes in a binary tree of height 5?

  1. 6 and 63
  2. 5 and 63
  3. 6 and 32
  4. 7 and 64
Show answer & solution

Concept. A binary tree of height h spans levels 0 to h, that is h + 1 levels. The fewest nodes come from a single chain, the most from a perfect tree in which every level is completely filled.

Step by step.

  1. Minimum: one node per level, so h + 1 = 6 nodes.
  2. Maximum: level i holds at most 2^i nodes, so the total is 2^0 + 2^1 + … + 2^5 = 2^6 − 1 = 63.
  3. The pair is therefore 6 and 63.

Answer: Option (A) — 6 and 63

Common trap. Using 2^h − 1 = 31 or 2^h = 32 for the maximum, which counts levels 1 to h and drops level 0, or giving 5 as the minimum by treating the height as the number of levels.

Takeaway. Height counted in edges means h + 1 levels, so the maximum is always 2^(h+1) − 1.

DS-U4-Q02MCQeasy

A complete binary tree has 15 nodes. The root is at level 0 and the height is the number of edges on the longest root-to-leaf path. What is the height of this tree?

  1. 2
  2. 4
  3. 7
  4. 3
Show answer & solution

Concept. In a complete binary tree every level except possibly the last is full, and the last level is filled from the left. So the node count of a complete tree of height h lies between 2^h and 2^(h+1) − 1.

Step by step.

  1. Levels 0 to 2 can hold at most 1 + 2 + 4 = 7 nodes, which is fewer than 15.
  2. Levels 0 to 3 can hold at most 1 + 2 + 4 + 8 = 15 nodes, exactly 15.
  3. So the tree is the perfect tree with four complete levels and its height is 3.

Answer: Option (D) — 3

Common trap. Answering 4 by counting the levels 0, 1, 2, 3 instead of the edges, or answering 7 by taking log2 15 rounded up as the height.

Takeaway. n nodes in a complete tree give height ⌊log2 n⌋ = 3 when the count is exactly 2^(h+1) − 1.

DS-U4-Q03NATeasy

A full binary tree is one in which every node has either 0 or 2 children. If such a tree has 41 leaves, how many nodes does it have in total?

Show answer & solution

Concept. In a full (proper) binary tree the number of leaves and the number of internal nodes differ by exactly one: L = I + 1.

Step by step.

  1. With L = 41, the internal nodes are I = L − 1 = 40.
  2. Total nodes n = L + I = 41 + 40 = 81.
  3. Equivalently n = 2L − 1 = 81.

Answer: 81

Common trap. Reporting 82 by using 2L, or 40 by answering with the number of internal nodes instead of the total.

Takeaway. Full tree: L = I + 1 and n = 2L − 1. Both follow from counting edges two ways.

DS-U4-Q04MCQmedium

A complete binary tree with 100 nodes is stored in a 1-indexed array A[1..100]: the root is A[1], and the children of the node at index i are at 2i and 2i + 1. How many of the 100 array cells hold leaves?

  1. 50
  2. 49
  3. 51
  4. 99
Show answer & solution

Concept. In the array form of a complete binary tree the internal nodes occupy the first ⌊n/2⌋ cells, because a node has a child exactly when 2i ≤ n.

Step by step.

  1. Internal nodes: ⌊100/2⌋ = 50, occupying A[1] to A[50].
  2. Leaves: 100 − 50 = 50, occupying A[51] to A[100].
  3. Check: A[50] has children A[100] and A[101], and only the first exists, so the boundary is right.

Answer: Option (A) — 50

Common trap. Reporting 49 by assuming a node needs both children to count as internal, or 51 by rounding the half upwards.

Takeaway. Leaves = ⌈n/2⌉ and internal nodes = ⌊n/2⌋ for any complete binary tree.

DS-U4-Q05MSQmedium

For a non-empty binary tree let n be the number of nodes, n0 the number of leaves, n1 the number of nodes with exactly one child, n2 the number with two children, and h the height in edges. Which of the following are always TRUE?

  1. n0 = n2 + 1
  2. n = n0 + n1 + n2
  3. h ≥ ⌈log2(n + 1)⌉ − 1
  4. Every level i (root at level 0) holds exactly 2^i nodes.
Show answer & solution

Concept. The first two identities come from counting the nodes and then the edges of the same tree two different ways; the height bound comes from the largest tree a given height can hold.

Step by step.

  1. (B) Every node falls in exactly one class, so n = n0 + n1 + n2.
  2. A tree with n nodes has n − 1 edges. Counting edges by parent type gives n1 + 2n2, hence n − 1 = n1 + 2n2.
  3. Substituting (B) into that equation gives n0 + n1 + n2 − 1 = n1 + 2n2, so n0 = n2 + 1: (A) holds.
  4. A tree of height h holds at most 2^(h+1) − 1 nodes, so n ≤ 2^(h+1) − 1, giving h ≥ log2(n + 1) − 1 and, since h is an integer, h ≥ ⌈log2(n + 1)⌉ − 1: (C) holds.
  5. (D) fails for a chain: it has one node per level, not 2^i.

Answer: Options (A), (B), (C)

Common trap. Accepting (D) because it is true for a perfect tree and forgetting that a general binary tree may leave levels half empty.

Takeaway. n0 = n2 + 1 holds for every binary tree, not only for full ones.

DS-U4-Q06MCQmedium

How many structurally different binary trees with exactly 5 nodes are there, where a left child and a right child are treated as different?

  1. 14
  2. 42
  3. 120
  4. 132
Show answer & solution

Concept. Ordered binary tree shapes with n nodes are counted by the Catalan numbers C_n = C(2n, n)/(n + 1).

Step by step.

  1. The first Catalan numbers are C_0 = 1, C_1 = 1, C_2 = 2, C_3 = 5, C_4 = 14, C_5 = 42.
  2. With n = 5: C(10, 5) = 252 and 252 / 6 = 42.

Answer: Option (B) — 42

Common trap. Giving 14, which is the count for 4 nodes, or 120 = 5!, which counts label orders rather than shapes.

Takeaway. Shapes with n nodes: Catalan C_n; labelled trees are a different, larger count.

DS-U4-Q07MCQhard

Each node of a binary tree is stored in a record with three pointer fields: left, right and parent. For a tree with n ≥ 1 nodes, how many of the 3n pointer fields are NULL?

  1. n + 1
  2. 2n + 2
  3. n − 1
  4. n + 2
Show answer & solution

Concept. Count the NULLs field by field. Every node except the root is somebody's child, so the child pointers that are set number exactly n − 1 out of 2n. The parent pointer is set for every node except the root.

Step by step.

  1. Left and right fields together: 2n fields, of which n − 1 point at a child (each edge is stored once), so 2n − (n − 1) = n + 1 are NULL.
  2. Parent fields: n fields, of which n − 1 are set, so exactly 1 (the root's) is NULL.
  3. Total NULLs (n + 1) + 1 = n + 2.
  4. Check on a single node: n = 1 gives 3 NULLs, and 1 + 2 = 3 agrees.

Answer: Option (D) — n + 2

Common trap. Answering n + 1 by quoting the familiar result for the two child fields and forgetting that the parent field of the root is also NULL.

Takeaway. n + 1 NULLs is a statement about child links only; add one more per extra upward link.

DS-U4-S2-Q01MCQeasy

A binary tree has 20 nodes. Exactly 6 of them have two children and exactly 4 have exactly one child. How many leaves does the tree have?

  1. 10
  2. 9
  3. 11
  4. 8
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Every node of a binary tree has 0, 1 or 2 children, so the three classes partition the nodes: n = n0 + n1 + n2, where n0 counts leaves.

Step by step.

  1. The two known classes hold 4 + 6 = 10 nodes.
  2. So n0 = 20 − 10 = 10.
  3. Check: n0 = n2 + 1 = 7 is not required here, because that identity only holds when n1 = 0; the tree does have one-child nodes, and the partition identity already fixes n0.

Answer: 10 leaves

Common trap. Reaching for n0 = n2 + 1 and answering 7. That identity is true for every binary tree, but it relates n0 to n2 only; when n1 ≠ 0 you must also use n = n0 + n1 + n2, and here the partition alone is enough.

Takeaway. Start every counting question by writing n = n0 + n1 + n2; add n0 = n2 + 1 only as a second equation.

DS-U4-S2-Q02NATeasy

A binary tree with 30 nodes is stored in records that carry two child pointers, left and right. How many of the 60 pointer fields hold NULL?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A tree on n nodes has exactly n − 1 edges, and each edge is stored in exactly one child pointer, so exactly n − 1 of the 2n child pointers are non-NULL.

Step by step.

  1. Non-NULL child pointers = 30 − 1 = 29, one per non-root node.
  2. NULL child pointers = 60 − 29 = 31, which is n + 1.
  3. Equivalently, each node except the root consumes one pointer, so 2n − (n − 1) = n + 1.

Answer: 31 NULL fields

Common trap. Answering 30, one NULL per node. The count is per pointer field, not per node, and it is one more than the number of nodes, not one less.

Takeaway. Memorise the pair: a binary tree with n nodes has n + 1 NULL pointers and n − 1 edges.

DS-U4-S2-Q03MCQeasy

In a strictly binary tree every node has either 0 or 2 children. Such a tree has 21 nodes with two children. How many nodes does the tree have in total?

  1. 42
  2. 44
  3. 43
  4. 41
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A strictly binary tree is a full binary tree, so n0 = n2 + 1 and the total is n = 2n2 + 1.

Step by step.

  1. Leaves: n0 = 21 + 1 = 22.
  2. Total: 21 + 22 = 43, that is 2 × 21 + 1 = 43.
  3. Sanity check with edges: 43 nodes have 42 edges, and each internal node contributes exactly 2 of them.

Answer: 43 nodes

Common trap. Answering 42 from 2 × 21, which forgets that the leaves are counted on top of the internal nodes.

Takeaway. For a full binary tree: n = 2n2 + 1 = 2n0 − 1.

DS-U4-S2-Q04MCQmedium

A binary tree has 100 nodes, exactly 30 of which are leaves. How many of its nodes have exactly one child?

  1. 40
  2. 41
  3. 39
  4. 42
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Three equations link the node classes: n = n0 + n1 + n2, n0 = n2 + 1, and the edge count n − 1 = n1 + 2n2.

Step by step.

  1. From n0 = 30: n2 = 30 − 1 = 29.
  2. Then n1 = 100 − 30 − 29 = 41.
  3. Check against the edge count: n1 + 2n2 = 41 + 58 = 99 = n − 1. Consistent.

Answer: 41 nodes with exactly one child

Common trap. Setting n2 = n0 and getting 40. The correct relation is n2 = n0 − 1, which shifts the answer by one.

Takeaway. With n0 and n given, the answer is n − 2n0 + 1; verify it against n − 1 = n1 + 2n2.

DS-U4-S2-Q05NATmedium

A complete binary tree has 1000 nodes. The root sits at level 0, and nodes are numbered level by level from left to right. How many nodes lie on the deepest level?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A complete binary tree fills levels 0 to h − 1 completely and packs level h from the left, so the first h levels hold 2^h − 1 nodes.

Step by step.

  1. Find the deepest level: 2^9 − 1 = 511 ≤ 1000 < 1023 = 2^10 − 1, so h = 9.
  2. Levels 0 to 8 hold 511 nodes, all of them full.
  3. Deepest level: 1000 − 511 = 489 nodes, out of a possible 512.

Answer: 489 nodes on level 9

Common trap. Subtracting 512 instead of 511, which double-counts the root and gives 488. The fully filled prefix is 2^h − 1, not 2^h.

Takeaway. For a complete tree with n nodes, the last level holds n − (2^h − 1) nodes where h = ⌊log₂ n⌋.

DS-U4-S2-Q06MSQmedium

For a binary tree with n ≥ 1 nodes and height h measured in edges, which of the following are TRUE?

  1. The number of leaves is always exactly one more than the number of nodes with two children.
  2. A complete binary tree with n nodes has height ⌊log₂ n⌋.
  3. Every full binary tree is also a complete binary tree.
  4. Every binary tree with at least two nodes has a node with exactly one child.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two different structural facts are being tested: the leaf/internal identity, which holds for every binary tree, and the relationship between completeness, fullness and height.

Step by step.

  1. A is TRUE. Counting edges from both ends gives n − 1 = n1 + 2n2 and n = n0 + n1 + n2, so n0 = n2 + 1 for every binary tree, full or not.
  2. B is TRUE. Levels 0 to h − 1 are full, so 2^h − 1 < n ≤ 2^(h+1) − 1, hence h = ⌊log₂ n⌋. For n = 1000 this gives 9.
  3. C is FALSE. A full tree only requires every node to have 0 or 2 children. The tree with root 1, left child 2 and right child 3, where 2 has children 4 and 5 and 3 has none, is full but not complete, because level 2 is not packed from the left.
  4. D is FALSE. A perfect tree of 3 or 7 nodes has n1 = 0, so it has no one-child node at all.

Answer: Options (A) and (B)

Common trap. Treating full and complete as synonyms. Full constrains child counts, complete constrains level filling; neither implies the other.

Takeaway. Keep the three families apart: full (0 or 2 children), complete (all levels but the last full, last packed left), perfect (all levels full).

DS-U4-S2-Q07MCQhard

How many structurally different binary trees have exactly 6 nodes and height exactly 5, where the height is the number of edges on the longest path from the root to a leaf and left and right children are distinguished?

  1. 64
  2. 16
  3. 12
  4. 32
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Height 5 with only 6 nodes forces the shape: the longest root-to-leaf path uses all 5 edges, so every one of the 6 nodes lies on that single path and each node has exactly one child except the last.

Step by step.

  1. A tree of 6 nodes has height at most 5, and height 5 is reached only by a chain.
  2. A chain of 6 nodes has 5 edges, and each edge independently goes to a left child or to a right child.
  3. So the count is 2^5 = 32. They are all distinct because the sequence of left/right choices differs.
  4. Cross-check: the number of 6-node binary trees of height at most 5 is the Catalan number C_6 = 132, and only the chains are subtracted from it, which is consistent with 32 < 132.

Answer: 32 trees

Common trap. Answering 2^6 = 64 by counting one choice per node. The choices are per edge, and a chain of 6 nodes has 5 edges.

Takeaway. A chain on n nodes has 2^(n−1) distinct orientations; this is the standard witness that preorder plus postorder does not determine a binary tree.

Inorder, preorder & postorder · 12
DS-U4-Q08MCQeasy

A binary tree with distinct keys has preorder A B D E C F and inorder D B E A C F. What is its postorder traversal?

  1. D E B F C A
  2. D E B C F A
  3. E D B F C A
  4. D B E F C A
Show answer & solution

Concept. The first key of the preorder is the root; its position in the inorder splits the remaining keys into the left and the right subtree.

Step by step.

  1. Root is A. Inorder D B E | A | C F gives left {D, B, E} and right {C, F}.
  2. Left subtree: preorder B D E makes B the root; inorder D B E puts D on the left and E on the right.
  3. Right subtree: preorder C F makes C the root; inorder C F puts F on the right, so C has no left child.
  4. Postorder = left, right, root: D E B, then F C, then A.

Answer: Option (A) — D E B F C A

Common trap. Option (B) reverses the last two keys by placing C before F, which happens if the right subtree is read as a left chain.

Takeaway. Rebuild the shape from preorder plus inorder first, then read off the postorder; never try to transform the strings directly.

DS-U4-Q09MCQeasy

A binary tree with 7 nodes has level-order traversal A B C D E F G and inorder traversal D B E A F C G. What is its preorder traversal?

  1. A B C D E F G
  2. A D B E C F G
  3. A B D E C F G
  4. A B D E F C G
Show answer & solution

Concept. Level order fixes the parent-child relation level by level, and the inorder then fixes which child sits on which side.

Step by step.

  1. Level order starts with A, so A is the root, and the next level is B and C, its two children.
  2. Next level D E F G: D, E are the children of B and F, G the children of C.
  3. Inorder D B E | A | F C G confirms the sides: B has D left and E right; C has F left and G right.
  4. Preorder = root, left, right: A B D E C F G.

Answer: Option (C) — A B D E C F G

Common trap. Option (A) simply repeats the level order, and option (D) drops C behind E by treating the right subtree as part of the left one.

Takeaway. Level order plus inorder is enough; the level order gives the shape, the inorder the orientation.

DS-U4-Q10NATmedium

A binary tree with the distinct keys A, B, C has preorder A B C and postorder C B A. Left and right children are distinguished. How many different binary trees satisfy both traversals together?

Show answer & solution

Concept. Preorder and postorder together do not determine a binary tree whenever some node has a single child: the two traversals say which node is whose child, but not on which side.

Step by step.

  1. Preorder A B C forces A to be the root, B a child of A and C a child of B.
  2. Any such chain has postorder C B A whatever the sides, because each node is visited after its only child.
  3. B may be a left or a right child of A: 2 choices. C may be a left or a right child of B: 2 choices.
  4. Total 2 × 2 = 4 trees: A→B→C as left-left, left-right, right-left and right-right chains.

Answer: 4

Common trap. Answering 1, assuming two traversals always pin the tree down; that is true only when no node has exactly one child.

Takeaway. The pair (preorder, postorder) is unique exactly when every internal node has two children.

DS-U4-Q11MCQeasy

A binary search tree holds distinct integer keys. Which traversal lists the keys in increasing order?

  1. preorder
  2. postorder
  3. level order
  4. inorder
Show answer & solution

Concept. In a BST every key in the left subtree is smaller than the node and every key in the right subtree is larger. Inorder visits left subtree, then node, then right subtree.

Step by step.

  1. Inorder emits the whole left subtree first, and by induction it is ascending and every key in it is below the node.
  2. Then the node, then the right subtree, which is ascending and entirely above the node.
  3. The concatenation is therefore ascending. Preorder and postorder visit the node before or between the subtrees, so they interleave large and small keys.

Answer: Option (D) — inorder

Common trap. Choosing preorder because it starts at the root and looks ordered; the root of a BST is only the middle key, not the smallest.

Takeaway. Inorder of a BST is sorted, which is why "is this tree a BST?" is answered by one inorder scan.

DS-U4-Q12MSQmedium

For a binary tree whose keys are all distinct, which of these pairs of traversals determine the tree uniquely?

  1. preorder and inorder
  2. postorder and inorder
  3. preorder and postorder
  4. level order and inorder
Show answer & solution

Concept. A reconstruction needs one traversal that identifies the root of each subtree and one that says how the remaining keys split into left and right.

Step by step.

  1. Inorder always supplies the split, because the root's position separates left keys from right keys.
  2. Preorder gives the root first, postorder gives it last, and level order gives it first within each subtree. Each of them therefore pairs with inorder: (A), (B) and (D) are unique.
  3. (C) fails: with preorder A B C and postorder C B A both the left chain and the right chain fit, so the side of a single child is unknown.

Answer: Options (A), (B), (D)

Common trap. Believing preorder plus postorder is enough because together they name every node in two orders; they still leave single-child nodes unoriented.

Takeaway. Any traversal that names the root, plus inorder, is sufficient; two root-naming traversals without inorder are not.

DS-U4-Q13MCQhard

A binary tree with 9 nodes has preorder A B D G H C E F I and inorder G D H B A E C I F. How many nodes of this tree have exactly one child?

  1. 0
  2. 1
  3. 2
  4. 3
Show answer & solution

Concept. Rebuild the tree recursively: the preorder supplies each subtree root, and the inorder says how many keys fall to its left and to its right.

Step by step.

  1. Root A; inorder G D H B | A | E C I F gives 4 keys left and 4 right.
  2. Left: preorder B D G H makes B the root; inorder G D H B puts all three keys on its left, so B has no right child.
  3. Inside that left part, preorder D G H makes D the root with G left and H right.
  4. Right: preorder C E F I makes C the root; inorder E C I F gives E left and I F right, so C has two children.
  5. Preorder F I with inorder I F makes F the root with I on its left only.
  6. Single-child nodes: B (left only) and F (left only). All others have 0 or 2.

Answer: Option (C) — 2

Common trap. Counting the leaves G, H, E, I as single-child nodes, or stopping after the first level and answering 0.

Takeaway. Draw the tree first; every later question about it (child counts, height, mirror, diameter) then becomes a lookup.

DS-U4-S2-Q08MCQeasy

A binary tree with distinct keys has inorder A B C D E F and postorder B A D F E C. What is its preorder traversal?

  1. C A B E D F
  2. C A B E F D
  3. C B A E D F
  4. A B C D E F
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Inorder fixes which keys fall left and right of a root; postorder names the root last. Together they rebuild the tree, and the preorder is then read off the rebuilt tree.

Step by step.

  1. Postorder ends in C, so the root is C. Inorder splits as A B | C | D E F.
  2. Left subtree: inorder A B, postorder B A, so the root is A with B as its right child.
  3. Right subtree: inorder D E F, postorder D F E, so the root is E with left child D and right child F.
  4. Preorder (root, left, right): C, then A B, then E D F → C A B E D F.

Answer: C A B E D F

Common trap. Reading postorder left to right and assuming the first symbol is a root. The root is always the last symbol of postorder.

Takeaway. Rebuild first, then read the requested traversal; never guess the traversal order from the two given strings.

DS-U4-S2-Q09MCQeasy

A binary tree with distinct keys has preorder 1 2 4 8 9 5 3 6 7 and inorder 8 4 9 2 5 1 6 3 7. What is its postorder traversal?

  1. 8 9 4 5 2 7 6 3 1
  2. 9 8 4 5 2 6 7 3 1
  3. 8 9 4 5 2 6 7 3 1
  4. 8 9 5 4 2 6 7 3 1
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Preorder names the root first, inorder splits the remaining keys into the left and right subtrees. Rebuilding bottom-up and printing left, right, root gives the postorder.

Step by step.

  1. Root 1; inorder splits as 8 4 9 2 5 | 1 | 6 3 7.
  2. Left subtree, preorder 2 4 8 9 5, inorder 8 4 9 2 5: root 2, left 4(8, 9), right 5.
  3. Right subtree, preorder 3 6 7, inorder 6 3 7: root 3, left 6, right 7.
  4. Postorder: 8 9 4, 5, 2, then 6, 7, 3, then 1 → 8 9 4 5 2 6 7 3 1.

Answer: 8 9 4 5 2 6 7 3 1

Common trap. Swapping the left and right children of the root, which yields the option ending ... 2 7 6 3 1. Preorder and inorder both carry the left-right distinction, so the split must be done exactly.

Takeaway. The left subtree of the root is exactly the keys before the root in inorder, and their preorder is exactly the matching prefix of preorder.

DS-U4-S2-Q10MCQmedium

A binary tree with distinct keys has preorder A B C D E F G H and inorder C B D A F E H G. How many leaves does the tree have?

  1. 3
  2. 4
  3. 5
  4. 2
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Rebuild the tree from the two traversals, then count nodes with no children. A leaf is exactly a node that has neither a left nor a right child.

Step by step.

  1. Root A; inorder splits as C B D | A | F E H G.
  2. Left subtree (preorder B C D, inorder C B D): root B with children C and D. Both are leaves.
  3. Right subtree (preorder E F G H, inorder F E H G): root E, left child F, right subtree with preorder G H and inorder H G gives root G with left child H and no right child.
  4. Leaves: C, D, F, H → 4 leaves.
  5. Check with the identity: internal nodes with two children are A, B, E (and G has one child), so n2 = 3 and n0 = n2 + 1 = 4. Consistent.

Answer: 4 leaves

Common trap. Counting only the deepest nodes, which misses F and C; or forgetting that G has a single child, so it is not a leaf.

Takeaway. Leaves sit at several depths. Rebuild the shape first and then read off the leaves, or use n0 = n2 + 1 once the two-child nodes are known.

DS-U4-S2-Q11MSQmedium

Which of the following statements are TRUE for binary trees whose keys are all distinct?

  1. If the preorder and the postorder traversals of a full binary tree are both known, the tree is uniquely determined.
  2. The preorder traversal of a binary search tree lists its keys in increasing order.
  3. Two different binary trees can have the same preorder traversal and the same postorder traversal.
  4. The postorder traversal of a binary tree is always the reverse of its preorder traversal.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A traversal pair determines a tree only when it fixes every left/right decision. The obstruction is a node with exactly one child, which the pair cannot tell apart from its mirror.

Step by step.

  1. A is TRUE. In a full tree every node has 0 or 2 children, so the size of each subtree is read directly from postorder, and the split is unambiguous at every level.
  2. B is FALSE. Preorder visits the root first, so for the BST 2(1, 3) it gives 2 1 3. Only the inorder traversal of a BST is sorted.
  3. C is TRUE. Take the chain P → Q → R with all children on the left and the chain with all children on the right. Both give preorder P Q R and postorder R Q P.
  4. D is FALSE. For 2(1, 3), preorder is 2 1 3 and postorder is 1 3 2, whose reverse is 2 3 1, not 2 1 3.

Answer: Options (A) and (C)

Common trap. Assuming that any two traversals always pin a binary tree down. Preorder with inorder, or postorder with inorder, always does; preorder with postorder does not, unless fullness removes the one-child ambiguity.

Takeaway. The pair that always works is {preorder or postorder} together with inorder. Level order plus inorder also works when all keys are distinct.

DS-U4-S2-Q12MCQhard

A binary tree with distinct keys has preorder P Q R S and postorder S R Q P. How many different binary trees have exactly these two traversals?

  1. 4
  2. 6
  3. 16
  4. 8
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Preorder visits a root before its descendants and postorder after them. When the two orders are exact reversals, every node must be an ancestor of the next, so the tree is a chain and only the left/right orientation of each edge is free.

Step by step.

  1. In preorder the next key after P is Q; in postorder the key before P is Q as well, so Q is the only child of P. The same argument repeats for R and S.
  2. So the shape is the chain P → Q → R → S, with 3 edges.
  3. Each edge is independently a left edge or a right edge, and the orientation never changes preorder or postorder.
  4. Count: 2^3 = 8.

Answer: 8 trees

Common trap. Answering 1, on the belief that preorder plus postorder always determine the tree. They determine it only when no node has a single child; here every node except the last has one.

Takeaway. Preorder and postorder together determine a tree uniquely if and only if the tree is full; otherwise the free choices are exactly the one-child nodes, giving 2^k trees for k such nodes.

DS-U4-S2-Q13MCQhard

A binary tree with distinct keys has preorder A B C D E F G and postorder C B E G F D A. Which of the following is its inorder traversal?

  1. C B A E D F G
  2. B C A E D G F
  3. C B A E D G F
  4. C B A D E G F
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. With only preorder and postorder, the subtree sizes must be read from postorder: the postorder of a subtree is a contiguous block ending at that subtree's root, and its length is the size of the subtree.

Step by step.

  1. Root A. In preorder the next key is B, the root of the left subtree. In postorder B sits at index 1, so the left subtree has 2 nodes and its postorder is C B → B with left child C.
  2. The right subtree is the rest: postorder E G F D, preorder D E F G, so its root is D.
  3. Inside it, preorder next is E, at index 0 of the postorder E G F, so E has no children.
  4. The remaining right subtree has postorder G F and preorder F G: root F with left child G.
  5. Inorder (left, root, right): C B, A, E, D, G F → C B A E D G F.

Answer: C B A E D G F

Common trap. Placing G as the right child of F. The split comes from postorder, where G precedes F, which makes it the left child. Guessing from the letters' order in preorder produces option (A).

Takeaway. For preorder + postorder, always measure a subtree by the position of its root in the postorder block; never assume balanced children.

Threaded binary trees · 12
DS-U4-Q14MCQeasy

In an inorder-threaded binary tree, the right pointer of a node that has no right child is replaced by a thread. What does that thread point to?

  1. its parent
  2. its inorder successor
  3. its inorder predecessor
  4. the root of the tree
Show answer & solution

Concept. Threading reuses the NULL pointers of a binary tree to store the inorder neighbours of a node, so that inorder traversal needs no stack.

Step by step.

  1. A node with no right child is the last node visited in its own subtree, so the next node of the inorder walk is its ancestor on the first turn to the right.
  2. That ancestor is the inorder successor, so the right thread points there.
  3. Symmetrically, a NULL left pointer becomes a thread to the inorder predecessor.

Answer: Option (B) — its inorder successor

Common trap. Saying the parent: for a node that is a left child the successor is the parent, but for a right child it is some higher ancestor, so "parent" is not general.

Takeaway. Right thread = successor, left thread = predecessor; that is what makes "find next in inorder" an O(1) step.

DS-U4-Q15NATeasy

A binary tree with 20 nodes is stored as a fully threaded binary tree, so every NULL left or right pointer is replaced by a thread. How many thread pointers does the tree contain?

Show answer & solution

Concept. A binary tree with n nodes has exactly n + 1 NULL child pointers, because it has 2n child fields and n − 1 of them hold edges.

Step by step.

  1. Child fields: 2 × 20 = 40.
  2. Fields holding a real child: 20 − 1 = 19 edges.
  3. NULL fields: 40 − 19 = 21. Full threading turns every one of them into a thread, so there are 21 threads.

Answer: 21

Common trap. Answering 20, one thread per node; the count is per NULL pointer, and a leaf contributes two.

Takeaway. Threads = n + 1 for a fully threaded tree of n nodes, the same count as the NULL pointers it replaces.

DS-U4-Q16MCQmedium

Compared with an ordinary binary tree, what is the main cost of threading?

  1. Each node needs an extra child pointer.
  2. Insertion and deletion must update the threads of neighbouring nodes.
  3. Inorder traversal needs an auxiliary stack.
  4. The tree can no longer be searched in time proportional to its height.
Show answer & solution

Concept. Threading trades update work for traversal work: it stores inorder neighbours in the unused pointers, which removes the stack but makes structural updates more delicate.

Step by step.

  1. Traversal gets cheaper: inorder runs in O(n) time and O(1) extra space, so (C) is false.
  2. No pointer is added; a NULL child pointer is reused, possibly with one tag bit, so (A) is false.
  3. Searching still follows one path of length at most the height, so (D) is false.
  4. When a node is inserted or deleted, the threads that pointed to the old neighbour must be redirected, and that repair work is what threading costs: (B) is true.

Answer: Option (B)

Common trap. Assuming threading needs extra storage per node; the whole point is to reuse the NULL pointers that already exist.

Takeaway. Threading pays off when the tree is built once and traversed many times.

DS-U4-Q17MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about threaded binary trees are TRUE?

  1. In a fully threaded binary tree a NULL left pointer is replaced by a thread to the inorder predecessor.
  2. Threading reduces the number of nodes that must be stored.
  3. Threads must be distinguishable from ordinary child pointers, typically by a one-bit tag per pointer.
  4. An inorder traversal of a fully threaded tree needs O(1) extra space.
Show answer & solution

Concept. A thread is a pointer to an inorder neighbour stored in a child field that would otherwise be NULL; a tag bit tells the two uses of the same field apart.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: the left thread of a node is its inorder predecessor, the right thread its successor.
  2. (B) false: threading changes no node count. It adds no node and removes none; only the meaning of some pointer fields changes.
  3. (C) true: without a tag the traversal cannot tell whether a field holds a child or a thread.
  4. (D) true: the successor is reached by following one thread, so no stack and no recursion is needed.

Answer: Options (A), (C), (D)

Common trap. Accepting (B) because threading "saves space": it saves the O(h) stack of a recursive traversal, not any node.

Takeaway. Threading is a space-for-update trade inside the existing nodes.

DS-U4-Q18MCQhard

A fully threaded binary tree uses a dummy header node. Its left pointer points to the root, its right pointer points to itself, and it acts as the inorder predecessor of the leftmost node and the inorder successor of the rightmost node. For a tree with n ≥ 1 real nodes, how many thread pointers point at the header?

  1. 0
  2. 1
  3. 2
  4. n
Show answer & solution

Concept. With a header the structure has n + 1 nodes and therefore n + 2 threads. Every thread points at an inorder neighbour, and the header is the neighbour of exactly two nodes.

Step by step.

  1. The leftmost node has no left child, so its left thread points to its inorder predecessor, which is the header.
  2. The rightmost node has no right child, so its right thread points to its inorder successor, which is again the header.
  3. Every other thread points at a real node, so exactly 2 threads reach the header.
  4. Check the count: n + 2 threads in all, of which 2 go to the header and n to real nodes.

Answer: Option (C) — 2

Common trap. Answering 1 by thinking only the rightmost node needs the header, or n by counting one header pointer per node.

Takeaway. The header closes the inorder walk into a cycle, so the traversal can stop on returning to it instead of testing for NULL.

DS-U4-Q19NAThard

A binary tree has root 10. Its left child is 5 with children 2 and 7. Its right child is 15 with left child 12 and right child 20, and 12 has a left child 11 and no right child. The tree is converted to a fully threaded binary tree. How many nodes have their right pointer replaced by a thread?

Show answer & solution

Concept. A right pointer becomes a thread exactly for those nodes that have no right child. The left pointers are a separate question.

Step by step.

  1. List the nodes with no right child: 2 (leaf), 7 (leaf), 12 (only a left child), 11 (leaf) and 20 (leaf).
  2. Nodes that do have a right child: 10 (child 15), 5 (child 7) and 15 (child 20). They keep a real right pointer.
  3. So 5 nodes carry a right thread.
  4. For contrast, nodes with no left child are 2, 7, 11 and 20, that is 4, so counting both kinds of thread would wrongly give 9.

Answer: 5

Common trap. Answering 4 by counting leaves only: node 12 is not a leaf, but it still has no right child and therefore still carries a right thread.

Takeaway. Threads are per missing child pointer, not per leaf.

DS-U4-S2-Q14MCQeasy

In an inorder-threaded binary tree, the left pointer of a node that has no left child is replaced by a thread. Which node does that thread point to?

  1. The node's inorder successor
  2. The node's inorder predecessor
  3. The node's parent
  4. The leftmost leaf of the whole tree
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Threads store the traversal step that the missing pointer would have wasted. A missing left pointer means the node has nothing to its left, so the traversal reaches it straight after its predecessor.

Step by step.

  1. In inorder, a node is visited after its entire left subtree. If the left subtree is empty, the visit follows the predecessor immediately.
  2. So the left thread points to the inorder predecessor, and symmetrically the right thread of a node with no right child points to its inorder successor.
  3. For the leftmost node the predecessor does not exist, which is why a fully threaded tree adds a dummy header node as a sentinel.

Answer: Its inorder predecessor

Common trap. Mixing up the two directions and saying the left thread points to the successor. The right thread carries the successor; the left thread carries the predecessor.

Takeaway. Left thread = predecessor, right thread = successor, both in inorder. The header node stands in when the predecessor or successor does not exist.

DS-U4-S2-Q15MSQmedium

A fully threaded binary tree is one in which every NULL child pointer has been replaced by a thread. Which of the following are TRUE?

  1. Such a tree with n nodes contains exactly n + 1 threads.
  2. A binary tree with 13 nodes has 14 NULL child pointers before threading.
  3. Every thread points to the inorder predecessor or the inorder successor of the node that owns it.
  4. The number of threads in such a tree equals the number of its leaves.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Threading reuses exactly the NULL pointers, so the thread count is the NULL-pointer count, n + 1, and each thread stores one inorder neighbour.

Step by step.

  1. A is TRUE. A binary tree with n nodes has 2n child pointers, of which n − 1 are real, leaving n + 1 NULL pointers and therefore n + 1 threads.
  2. B is TRUE. 13 + 1 = 14.
  3. C is TRUE. That is the definition of an inorder thread: the field that would have held a missing child now holds the neighbour in the inorder sequence.
  4. D is FALSE. Threads are counted per missing child pointer, not per leaf. The perfect tree with 7 nodes has 4 leaves but 8 threads.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Common trap. Equating threads with leaves. A leaf contributes two threads only if it is the only node at its position; in general a node with one child contributes one thread, so the total is n + 1 regardless of the leaf count.

Takeaway. Threads = NULL pointers = n + 1. Leaves = n2 + 1. These agree only in special shapes.

DS-U4-S2-Q16MCQmedium

Each node of a threaded binary tree stores two pointer fields, with a one-bit tag beside each of them. Why are the tags necessary?

  1. A thread and a real child pointer occupy the same field, and nothing else distinguishes them.
  2. A thread may point to a node that has already been freed.
  3. Threading doubles the number of nodes in the tree.
  4. The tags record the depth of each node in the tree.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Threading trades a NULL pointer for a traversal link, so the same field must be able to mean two different things. A one-bit tag resolves which meaning is in force.

Step by step.

  1. Without a tag, the traversal cannot tell whether a non-NULL pointer leads to a child (descend) or to an ancestor (follow the thread).
  2. One bit per pointer is enough: 0 for a real child, 1 for a thread.
  3. That is why a threaded node costs two extra bits, and why the memory saving comes from dropping the stack, not from dropping a field.

Answer: The first option

Common trap. Believing threading stores the extra information in a new pointer field. It does not: the field is reused, and only a flag is added.

Takeaway. Threading is a tagged union of child and thread. Any implementation must carry the tag or lose the ability to traverse.

DS-U4-S2-Q17MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about inorder-threaded binary trees are TRUE?

  1. The inorder successor of a node that has a right child is found in O(1) time.
  2. Threading needs one extra bit per pointer field to mark a thread.
  3. In a preorder-threaded tree the preorder successor of a leaf is reached in O(1) time.
  4. Threading changes the number of nodes in the tree.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A thread is a stored successor. It helps only where the successor would otherwise require a descent, so the cost of finding a successor depends on whether the node has a right subtree.

Step by step.

  1. A is FALSE. If the node has a right child, its inorder successor is the leftmost node of that right subtree, reached by descending a spine of length up to h. That is O(h), not O(1).
  2. B is TRUE. One bit per pointer field separates a thread from a real child.
  3. C is TRUE. For a leaf the preorder successor is stored directly as a thread, so it is a single pointer step.
  4. D is FALSE. Threading rewrites NULL pointers and adds tags; the node set is unchanged.

Answer: Options (B) and (C)

Common trap. Assuming threads make every successor step constant time. Only the case where the needed subtree is missing is constant; a real subtree still has to be entered.

Takeaway. Threads remove stack use, not descent. A threaded traversal is O(n) overall but individual successor steps may cost O(h).

DS-U4-S2-Q18MCQhard

A fully inorder-threaded binary tree with n nodes is traversed inorder by following threads and descending left spines, without any stack. What is the total time of this traversal, and why?

  1. Θ(n log n), because every thread step is preceded by a descent.
  2. Θ(n²), because finding a successor may require a descent to a leaf each time.
  3. Θ(h), because the traversal only ever walks the left spine.
  4. Θ(n), because across the whole traversal each edge of the tree is descended at most once.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Individual successor steps can cost O(h), but an amortised argument bounds the whole traversal: the descents only ever move downward along edges that are then never re-descended.

Step by step.

  1. Each of the n nodes is printed exactly once, which is already Ω(n).
  2. Every left-spine descent moves down a tree edge, and once a subtree has been traversed the traversal never descends into it again. A tree has n − 1 edges, so all descents together cost O(n).
  3. Thread steps cost O(1) each and there are O(n) of them.
  4. Total Θ(n), with O(1) amortised per node and O(1) extra space.

Answer: Θ(n)

Common trap. Multiplying the worst-case O(h) successor cost by n and concluding O(nh). That over-counts: the expensive descents happen along disjoint parts of the tree, not at every step.

Takeaway. Amortised analysis is the point of threading: worst-case O(h) per successor, O(n) in total, O(1) space.

DS-U4-S2-Q19NATmedium

A binary tree holds 8 at the root, 3 as its left child and 10 as its right child. Node 3 has children 1 and 6, and node 6 has children 4 and 7. Node 10 has only a right child 14, and node 14 has only a left child 13. Every NULL child pointer is replaced by a thread. How many of the threads replace a missing right child?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A thread that replaces a missing right child is exactly a node with no right child, so the question counts nodes whose right pointer is NULL.

Step by step.

  1. List the nodes: 8, 3, 1, 6, 4, 7, 10, 14, 13 — nine nodes.
  2. Nodes that do have a right child: 3 (right child 6), 6 (right child 7), 8 (right child 10), 10 (right child 14). That is four nodes.
  3. So nine minus four, that is 5 nodes, have no right child: 1, 4, 7, 14 and 13.
  4. Check the total: the tree has 9 + 1 = 10 NULL pointers, 5 missing left children (1, 4, 7, 10, 13) and 5 missing right children.

Answer: 5 right threads

Common trap. Counting leaves, which gives 4. Node 14 is not a leaf, because it has a left child, but it has no right child, so it still owns a right thread.

Takeaway. Count missing pointers per node, not leaves: a node with one child contributes exactly one thread.

Expression trees · 12
DS-U4-Q20MCQeasy

The operators +, −, * have their usual precedence and all are left associative. What is the postfix form of a + b * c − d?

  1. a b + c * d −
  2. a b c * + d −
  3. a b c + * d −
  4. a b c * d + −
Show answer & solution

Concept. Postfix is the postorder traversal of the expression tree: both operands of an operator appear before the operator itself, and the tree already encodes precedence and associativity.

Step by step.

  1. * binds tighter than + and −, so the expression parses as (a + (b * c)) − d.
  2. Innermost b * c becomes b c *.
  3. a + (b c *) becomes a b c * +.
  4. (a b c * +) − d becomes a b c * + d −.

Answer: Option (B) — a b c * + d −

Common trap. Option (A) is (a + b) * c − d, which ignores precedence, and option (C) is a * (b + c) − d.

Takeaway. Convert by tree, not by eye: postfix of a node is postfix(left) postfix(right) operator.

DS-U4-Q21MCQeasy

Which traversal of an expression tree produces the prefix form of the expression?

  1. inorder
  2. postorder
  3. preorder
  4. level order
Show answer & solution

Concept. In an expression tree each internal node is an operator and each leaf an operand, so the three depth-first orders give the three notations.

Step by step.

  1. Preorder visits the operator before its operands, which is exactly prefix notation.
  2. Postorder visits the operator after both operands, which is postfix notation.
  3. Inorder visits left, operator, right, which reproduces infix — without the parentheses that the tree itself encodes.

Answer: Option (C) — preorder

Common trap. Choosing inorder and expecting the original bracketed string; inorder alone is ambiguous for a + b * c and (a + b) * c.

Takeaway. preorder = prefix, postorder = postfix, inorder = infix without brackets.

DS-U4-Q22NATmedium

Evaluate the postfix expression 5 3 + 8 2 − * 4 /. Every intermediate value is exact and the division is exact. Give the value.

Show answer & solution

Concept. Postfix is evaluated with one stack: push every operand, and on an operator pop the right operand first and then the left.

Step by step.

  1. Push 5, push 3; on +: pop 3 and 5, push 5 + 3 = 8. Stack: 8.
  2. Push 8, push 2; on −: pop 2 and 8, push 8 − 2 = 6. Stack: 8, 6.
  3. On *: pop 6 and 8, push 8 × 6 = 48. Stack: 48.
  4. Push 4; on /: pop 4 and 48, push 48 / 4 = 12.

Answer: 12

Common trap. Popping in the wrong order at the subtraction, computing 2 − 8 = −6 and finishing with −12.

Takeaway. The first pop is the right operand; for − and / the order changes the answer.

DS-U4-Q23MSQmedium

Consider an expression tree in which every internal node is a binary operator and every leaf is an operand. Which of the following are TRUE?

  1. Its inorder traversal reproduces the original infix string with all its parentheses.
  2. The number of leaves is one more than the number of internal nodes.
  3. Evaluating the postfix form of the expression needs a stack.
  4. The tree is a full binary tree.
Show answer & solution

Concept. Such a tree is full by construction — every internal node has exactly two children — and the leaf/internal-node identity follows from that.

Step by step.

  1. (D) true: every internal node is a binary operator with two operands as children.
  2. (B) true: in a full binary tree L = I + 1. With 9 operators there are 10 operands, and the token count 2I + 1 agrees.
  3. (C) true: postfix evaluation pushes operands and pops on operators, so it needs a stack of depth up to the tree height.
  4. (A) false: inorder gives the infix order but no parentheses, so (a + b) * c and a + b * c produce the same inorder string a + b * c.

Answer: Options (B), (C), (D)

Common trap. Believing the tree "remembers" the parentheses in its inorder; the shape carries them, the inorder string does not.

Takeaway. Expression trees are full binary trees, so leaves = operators + 1 and tokens = 2 × operators + 1.

DS-U4-Q24MCQmedium

The expression a + b * c − d parses, with the usual precedence and left associativity, as (a + (b * c)) − d. In its expression tree, how many nodes lie in the left subtree of the root?

  1. 5
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Concept. The root of the expression tree is the last operator applied, and its subtrees are the two operands of that operator.

Step by step.

  1. The outermost operator is −, so it is the root.
  2. Its left operand is the subexpression a + (b * c), whose tree is + with children a and *, and * has children b and c.
  3. That is 5 nodes: +, a, *, b, c.
  4. Its right operand is the single leaf d, so the whole tree has 7 nodes.

Answer: Option (A) — 5

Common trap. Answering 3 by counting only the operators and the leaf a, or 4 by forgetting the * node.

Takeaway. Count every operator and every operand; a subtree with k operators holds 2k + 1 nodes.

DS-U4-Q25MSQhard

An expression tree has 9 internal nodes, all of them binary operators, and 10 leaves. Which of the following are TRUE?

  1. Its postfix form has 19 tokens.
  2. Every internal node has exactly two children.
  3. Its prefix form has 18 tokens.
  4. It has exactly one more leaf than internal node.
Show answer & solution

Concept. Prefix, infix and postfix forms of the same expression tree contain the same tokens, merely in different orders: I operators plus L operands.

Step by step.

  1. Token count = I + L = 9 + 10 = 19, so (A) is true and (C), which claims 18, is false.
  2. Every internal node is a binary operator, so it has two operands as children: (B) is true.
  3. L = I + 1 because 10 = 9 + 1, the standard full-tree identity: (D) is true.
  4. Equivalently, a full tree with I internal nodes has 2I + 1 nodes in all, here 19, which matches the token count.

Answer: Options (A), (B), (D)

Common trap. Believing prefix is shorter than postfix because it "starts with the operator"; the token multiset is identical, so both have 2I + 1 tokens.

Takeaway. Any expression tree gives 2I + 1 tokens in each of the three notations.

DS-U4-S2-Q20MCQeasy

The operators +, −, * and / have their usual precedence, with * and / binding tighter than + and −, and all four associate to the left. What is the postfix form of (a + b) * (c − d) / e ?

  1. a b + c d * − e /
  2. a b + c d − * e /
  3. a b c d + − * e /
  4. a b + c d − e / *
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Postfix is the postorder traversal of the expression tree. Parenthesisation fixes the tree, so convert the parse tree, not the string, and division takes the whole product as its left operand.

Step by step.

  1. a + b becomes a b + and c − d becomes c d −.
  2. Their product is (a b +) (c d −) *.
  3. The division by e applies to that product: a b + c d − * e /.
  4. Check the token count: 5 operators and 6 operands, so 11 tokens, which the answer has.

Answer: a b + c d − * e /

Common trap. Writing a b + c d − e / * by reading left to right. The division is the last operation performed, so it must be the last operator emitted.

Takeaway. Postfix of an expression is the postorder of its tree; the last operator printed is always the root, that is, the outermost operation.

DS-U4-S2-Q21MCQeasy

What is the infix form of the postfix expression a b c * + d −, read with the usual precedence and left associativity?

  1. a + b * c − d
  2. a + (b * c − d)
  3. (a + b) * c − d
  4. a * b + c − d
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Convert the postfix string to an expression tree with a stack, then read the tree inorder, inserting parentheses only where precedence would otherwise change the meaning.

Step by step.

  1. Push a, b, c. On *: pop c and b, push (b * c).
  2. On +: pop (b * c) and a, push (a + (b * c)).
  3. Push d. On −: pop d and the sum, push ((a + (b * c)) − d).
  4. Remove the redundant parentheses: a + b * c − d. The * binds tightest, then the + and the − in left-to-right order.

Answer: a + b * c − d

Common trap. Keeping the parentheses as a + (b * c − d), which is a different tree: there the subtraction is inside the right operand of the plus.

Takeaway. Parentheses may be dropped exactly when the tree's structure is already implied by precedence and associativity.

DS-U4-S2-Q22NATeasy

Evaluate the postfix expression 6 2 3 * − 4 5 * +. Every value is an exact integer.

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Postfix evaluation uses a stack of values: push operands, and on an operator pop the right operand first and then the left operand.

Step by step.

  1. Push 6, 2, 3. On *: pop 3 (right) and 2 (left) → 6. Stack: 6, 6.
  2. On −: pop 6 (right) and 6 (left) → 6 − 6 = 0. Stack: 0.
  3. Push 4, 5. On *: 4 × 5 = 20. Stack: 0, 20.
  4. On +: 0 + 20 = 20.

Answer: 20

Common trap. Popping the operands in the wrong order at the −, giving 2 − 3 = −1 or 6 − 6 written as 6 − 0. For non-commutative operators the first popped value is the right operand.

Takeaway. In postfix evaluation, always pop right first, then left. Only + and * hide a mistake; − and / expose it.

DS-U4-S2-Q23MSQmedium

In an expression tree every internal node is a binary operator and every leaf is an operand. Which of the following are TRUE?

  1. A valid postfix expression with k operators has exactly k + 1 operands.
  2. The expression tree of a fully parenthesised binary expression with k operators has 2k + 1 nodes.
  3. An inorder traversal of an expression tree may fail to reproduce the original expression unless parentheses are added.
  4. The expression tree of a + b + c + d, parsed left associatively, has height 2 in edges.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An expression tree is a full binary tree: every internal node has exactly two children. That single fact fixes the node counts, and the inorder traversal is the one that loses structure.

Step by step.

  1. A is TRUE. Each operator consumes two operands and returns one value, so a postfix string with k operators nets k + 1 operands. The stack is never empty and ends with one value.
  2. B is TRUE. With k internal nodes and k + 1 leaves, the tree has 2k + 1 nodes, which is 2n0 − 1 for a full tree.
  3. C is TRUE. Inorder gives a + b * c for the tree of a + (b * c) and of (a + b) * c alike; only parentheses restore the distinction.
  4. D is FALSE. Left associativity gives ((a + b) + c) + d, a left spine of 3 edges, so the height is 3.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Common trap. Answering that inorder reproduces the expression. It reproduces the token sequence but not the grouping, which is exactly why infix needs parentheses and postfix does not.

Takeaway. An expression tree is full, so operators = operands − 1 and nodes = 2 × operators + 1.

DS-U4-S2-Q24MCQeasy

How many nodes does the expression tree of a * (b + c) − d / (e − f) have, where every internal node is one operator and every leaf is one operand?

  1. 10
  2. 12
  3. 11
  4. 9
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In an expression tree every operator becomes one internal node and every operand one leaf, and the tree is full, so the total is 2k + 1 for k operators.

Step by step.

  1. Operators: *, +, −, /, − → 5 operators.
  2. Operands: a, b, c, d, e, f → 6 operands.
  3. Total nodes: 5 + 6 = 11 = 2 × 5 + 1.
  4. Shape check: root −, left *(a, +(b, c)), right /(d, −(e, f)), which uses every token exactly once.

Answer: 11 nodes

Common trap. Counting 10 by taking only the operands and the four binary operators of the top level, or 12 by counting a parenthesis as a node. Parentheses are not nodes; they only fix the shape.

Takeaway. For any fully parenthesised binary expression: nodes = 2k + 1 where k is the operator count.

DS-U4-S2-Q25NAThard

An expression tree is built from the postfix expression a b + c d e + * * f −, with one operator per internal node and one operand per leaf. What is the height of the tree in edges?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Postfix is a postorder listing of the tree, so building it with a stack recovers the shape exactly. The height is then computed bottom-up as 1 + max(height of the two children), with leaves at height 0.

Step by step.

  1. a b + gives node P = +(a, b), height 1.
  2. d e + gives node Q = +(d, e), height 1. Then c and Q with * give R = *(c, Q), height 1 + max(0, 1) = 2.
  3. The next * joins P and R: S = *(P, R), height 1 + max(1, 2) = 3.
  4. Finally f with −: root −(S, f), height 1 + max(3, 0) = 4.

Answer: 4

Common trap. Answering 3 by taking the height of the deepest subexpression only, or 5 by counting nodes on the longest path instead of edges.

Takeaway. Build the tree with a stack, then evaluate height bottom-up; the outermost operator always adds one level to the taller child.

Review the 50 solved questions from Unit 5 — Binary Search Trees & Balanced Trees
BST insertion & deletion · 14
DS-U5-Q01MCQeasy

The keys 50, 30, 70, 20, 40, 60, 80 are inserted into an empty binary search tree in that order. The key 50 is then deleted by replacing it with its inorder successor. Which key is at the root of the resulting tree?

  1. 30
  2. 40
  3. 70
  4. 60
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Deleting a node with two children in a BST means finding a key that can legally take its place: either the inorder predecessor (largest key in the left subtree) or the inorder successor (smallest key in the right subtree).

Step by step.

  1. Inorder traversal of the tree: 20, 30, 40, 50, 60, 70, 80.
  2. The right subtree of 50 is rooted at 70, and its leftmost key is 60, so the successor of 50 is 60.
  3. Copy 60 into the root position and delete 60 from its old place; 60 had no children, so nothing takes its place.
  4. Result: root 60, left subtree 30 with children 20 and 40, right subtree 70 with right child 80. Inorder is still 20, 30, 40, 60, 70, 80, so the BST property holds.

Answer: Option (D), 60.

Common trap. Answering 30, which is the inorder predecessor and also a legal choice — but the question fixes the successor rule. Answering 70 assumes the right child simply moves up, which breaks nothing here but is not the successor rule either.

Takeaway. Both predecessor and successor preserve the BST property; the rule must be read from the question, and only the successor rule gives 60.

DS-U5-Q02NATeasy

The keys 20, 10, 30, 5, 15, 25, 35, 3, 7, 12, 17 are inserted in that order into an empty binary search tree. Taking the root to be at depth 0, what is the sum of the depths of all eleven keys?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Insertion order fixes the shape of a BST completely: each new key descends from the root along the unique path its comparisons allow, so the depth of a key is the number of edges on that path.

Step by step.

  1. 20 is the root, depth 0.
  2. 10 and 30 attach to 20, depth 1 each.
  3. 5, 15, 25, 35 attach one level lower, depth 2 each.
  4. 3, 7, 12, 17 attach one level lower again, depth 3 each.
  5. Sum = 0 + 2(1) + 4(2) + 4(3) = 0 + 2 + 8 + 12 = 22.

Answer: 22.

Common trap. Counting depth in nodes rather than edges, which adds 1 per key and gives 33. Also easy to miscount the four depth-2 nodes as five by counting 10 and 30 twice.

Takeaway. For an insertion-built BST the level sizes are determined by the comparison path, so list the keys level by level before summing anything.

DS-U5-Q03MCQmedium

The keys 45, 25, 65, 15, 35, 55, 75 are inserted in that order into an empty binary search tree. The key 25 is then deleted by replacing it with its inorder successor. Which key becomes the left child of 45?

  1. 35
  2. 15
  3. 40
  4. 30
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. When the successor of the deleted node is a key inside the deleted node's right subtree, the successor is lifted into the vacated position and inherits the deleted node's left subtree unchanged.

Step by step.

  1. The subtree rooted at 25 is: 25 with left child 15 and right child 35.
  2. The leftmost key of the right subtree of 25 is 35 itself, so the successor is 35.
  3. 35 has no children, so it moves up with no further repair: the position of 25 now holds 35, whose left child is 15 and whose right child is empty.
  4. The parent of that position is 45, so the left child of 45 becomes 35.

Answer: Option (A), 35.

Common trap. Answering 15, which keeps the original left subtree as the direct child of 45 and so loses the promotion step; or answering 30, a key that is not in the tree at all.

Takeaway. Draw the two-node subtree of the deleted key before and after the lift; the left subtree travels with the successor, it is not re-parented to the grandparent.

DS-U5-Q04MCQhard

The keys 4, 2, 6, 1, 3, 5, 7 are inserted in that order into an empty binary search tree. The key 2 is then deleted by replacing it with its inorder successor. What is the postorder traversal of the resulting tree?

  1. 1, 3, 5, 7, 6, 4
  2. 1, 3, 7, 5, 6, 4
  3. 3, 1, 5, 7, 6, 4
  4. 1, 3, 5, 7, 4, 6
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Deletion changes the tree first and only then does the traversal read it; the two operations must be kept in order, and postorder visits left subtree, then right subtree, then the node itself.

Step by step.

  1. The inserted tree is complete: root 4, children 2 and 6, grandchildren 1, 3, 5, 7.
  2. The successor of 2 is the leftmost key of its right subtree, namely 3; 3 is a leaf, so it is lifted into the position of 2.
  3. Resulting tree: root 4, left child 3 with left child 1 and no right child, right child 6 with children 5 and 7.
  4. Postorder: left subtree of 4 gives 1, 3; right subtree gives 5, 7, 6; finally the root 4. Total 1, 3, 5, 7, 6, 4.

Answer: Option (A), 1, 3, 5, 7, 6, 4.

Common trap. Traversing the tree as it was before the deletion, which gives 1, 2, 3, 5, 7, 6, 4 and matches no option — so a candidate then guesses. Option (C) reverses the order inside the left subtree, confusing postorder with a mirrored inorder.

Takeaway. After any structural edit, redraw the affected subtree and re-derive the traversal from the drawing instead of editing the old traversal list.

DS-U5-Q05MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about deletion in a binary search tree with distinct keys are TRUE?

  1. Both the inorder predecessor and the inorder successor of a node with two children must themselves be leaves.
  2. Copying the inorder successor into a two-child node and then deleting the successor from the right subtree preserves the BST ordering.
  3. Two different insertion orders of the same set of distinct keys always produce the same tree shape.
  4. Deleting a node with two children can cost Θ(h) key comparisons in the worst case, where h is the height of the tree.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Deletion in a BST rests on one fact: the inorder predecessor is the largest key below the deleted key and the inorder successor is the smallest key above it, so either can occupy the vacated position without breaking any ordering constraint.

Step by step.

  1. Statement (A): the predecessor is the rightmost node of the left subtree, so it has no right child — but it may have a left child. Counterexample: insert 4, 2, 6, 1, 3; the predecessor of 4 is 3, a leaf, but insert 4, 2, 6, 1, 3, 2.5 and the predecessor of 4 is a node with a left child. False.
  2. Statement (B): the successor is larger than every key in the deleted node's left subtree and smaller than every key in its right subtree, which is exactly the interval the position requires. True.
  3. Statement (C): inserting 1, 2, 3 gives a right chain; inserting 2, 1, 3 gives a balanced tree from the same three keys. False.
  4. Statement (D): finding the successor means walking down the left spine of the right subtree, at most h steps, and the recursive descent to the node itself is also at most h. True.

Answer: Options (A) and (D) are FALSE, so the correct set is (B), (D).

Common trap. Accepting (A) because the standard picture of a successor is a leaf; the left-spine walk can end at a node that carries a left subtree.

Takeaway. Prove each claim against the interval argument, not against one picture; the predecessor and successor are the two keys whose values straddle the deleted key.

DS-U5-Q06NATmedium

The keys 20, 10, 30, 5, 15, 25, 35, 2, 7, 12, 17, 22, 27, 32, 37 are inserted in that order into an empty binary search tree. The key 20 is then deleted by replacing it with its inorder successor. Taking the root of the resulting tree to be at depth 0, what is the sum of the depths of all remaining fourteen keys?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A root deletion by successor moves the smallest key of the right subtree to the top, and every key on the spine it was taken from shifts up by one level — so the depths must be recomputed, not patched.

Step by step.

  1. The inserted tree is perfectly balanced: root 20, children 10 and 30, grandchildren 5, 15, 25, 35, and great-grandchildren 2, 7, 12, 17, 22, 27, 32, 37.
  2. The successor of 20 is the leftmost key of the right subtree: 30 → 25 → 22. So 22 becomes the root and is removed from its old position.
  3. Resulting tree: root 22; left child 10 with 5 (2, 7) and 15 (12, 17); right child 30 with 25 (27) and 35 (32, 37).
  4. Depths: 22 at 0; 10 and 30 at 1; 5, 15, 25, 35 at 2; 2, 7, 12, 17, 27, 32, 37 at 3.
  5. Sum = 0 + 2(1) + 4(2) + 7(3) = 0 + 2 + 8 + 21 = 31.

Answer: 31.

Common trap. Adding the deleted key 20 back into the depth sum, or keeping 22 at its old depth 3 and getting 32; both come from patching the old list instead of rebuilding the tree.

Takeaway. After a root deletion, list the keys level by level from the new root. Fourteen keys in a near-complete shape means one key at depth 0, two at depth 1, four at depth 2 and seven at depth 3.

DS-U5-Q07MCQeasy

A binary search tree holds distinct keys and has height h, measured in edges, so a tree with a single node has h = 0. What is the maximum number of key comparisons that a successful search can perform?

  1. h
  2. h + 1
  3. h − 1
  4. 2h
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each comparison in a BST search either finds the key or moves one level down, so the number of comparisons equals the number of nodes on the search path — one more than the number of edges on it.

Step by step.

  1. A successful search for a key ends at the node holding it.
  2. That node sits at depth d for some 0 ≤ d ≤ h, and the search compared the key once at every node from the root down to it.
  3. The path holds d + 1 nodes, so the search made d + 1 comparisons.
  4. The worst case is the deepest node, d = h, giving h + 1 comparisons.

Answer: Option (B), h + 1.

Common trap. Answering h, which counts edges on the path instead of nodes — an off-by-one that also appears in the claim that a one-node tree needs zero comparisons.

Takeaway. Comparisons = nodes on the path = edges + 1. The same off-by-one decides the worst-case time of BST search, O(h) comparisons and O(h + 1) node visits.

DS-U5-Q26MCQeasy

The keys 40, 20, 60, 10, 30, 50, 70 are inserted in that order into an empty binary search tree. How many nodes of the resulting tree have exactly one child?

  1. 2
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 0
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The number of children a node ends up with is fixed by how many later keys fall on each side of it while it is the active search position. A node gets two children exactly when at least one later key is smaller and at least one is larger than it.

Step by step.

  1. 40 becomes the root.
  2. 20 < 40 goes left; 60 > 40 goes right.
  3. 10 < 40 then < 20 becomes the left child of 20; 30 < 40 then > 20 becomes the right child of 20.
  4. 50 < 40? No, 50 > 40 then < 60 becomes the left child of 60; 70 > 40 then > 60 becomes the right child of 60.
  5. The tree is a perfect tree of height 2: root 40, children 20 and 60, and each of those has two children. The three internal nodes each have two children and the four leaves have none, so no node has exactly one child.

Answer: Option (D), 0.

Common trap. Counting 20 or 60 as one-child nodes by looking only at one level of the drawing, or confusing “one child” with “one subtree that contains keys”. Every internal node here has both children present.

Takeaway. Draw the final tree, then count children per node. A balanced insertion order fills both sides of every internal node, so the one-child count is zero.

DS-U5-Q27MCQeasy

The keys 15, 10, 25, 5, 20, 30 are inserted in that order into an empty binary search tree. What is the preorder traversal of the resulting tree?

  1. 15, 10, 25, 5, 20, 30
  2. 15, 10, 5, 25, 20, 30
  3. 15, 10, 5, 20, 25, 30
  4. 15, 25, 20, 30, 10, 5
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Preorder is root, then the whole left subtree, then the whole right subtree. In a binary search tree the shape is decided by the insertion order, and the traversal must be read off that shape, not off the sorted key order.

Step by step.

  1. 15 is the root. 10 < 15 becomes its left child; 25 > 15 becomes its right child.
  2. 5 < 15, < 10 becomes the left child of 10. 20 < 25 and > 15 becomes the left child of 25. 30 > 25 becomes the right child of 25.
  3. Left subtree of the root is 10 with left child 5, so its preorder is 10, 5.
  4. Right subtree of the root is 25 with children 20 and 30, so its preorder is 25, 20, 30.
  5. Preorder: 15, then 10, 5, then 25, 20, 30.

Answer: Option (B), 15, 10, 5, 25, 20, 30.

Common trap. Writing the sorted order 5, 10, 15, 20, 25, 30, which is the inorder traversal and is the same for every insertion order of these keys. Option (A) is the level-order reading of the first three keys only.

Takeaway. Inorder is always sorted in a binary search tree and therefore never distinguishes one insertion order from another; preorder and postorder do.

DS-U5-Q28NATeasy

The keys 8, 4, 12, 2, 6, 10, 14 are inserted in that order into an empty binary search tree. What is the height of the resulting tree, measured in edges, so that a tree with a single node has height 0?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Height measured in edges is the number of edges on the longest root-to-leaf path; a one-node tree has height 0 and each extra level adds one.

Step by step.

  1. 8 is the root.
  2. 4 < 8 goes left of 8; 12 > 8 goes right of 8.
  3. 2 < 4 goes left of 4; 6 > 4 goes right of 4.
  4. 10 < 12 goes left of 12; 14 > 12 goes right of 12.
  5. The tree is perfect with three levels: level 0 holds 8, level 1 holds 4 and 12, level 2 holds 2, 6, 10, 14.
  6. The longest root-to-leaf path has two edges, for example 8 → 4 → 2.

Answer: 2.

Common trap. Reporting 3 by counting the number of levels instead of the number of edges on the path, which is the usual source of an off-by-one in height questions.

Takeaway. Fix the convention before counting: levels equal edges plus one for a root-to-leaf path, so height in edges is always one less than the level count.

DS-U5-Q29MCQmedium

The keys 50, 30, 70, 20, 40, 60, 80, 35 are inserted in that order into an empty binary search tree. The key 40, which has exactly one child, is then deleted by splicing that child into its place. Which key becomes the right child of 30?

  1. 35
  2. 20
  3. 45
  4. 60
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Deletion in a binary search tree has three cases. A node with no children is simply removed, a node with one child is replaced by that child, and only a node with two children needs a predecessor or successor substitution.

Step by step.

  1. Build the tree: 50 is the root, 30 its left child, 70 its right child.
  2. 20 goes left of 30, 40 goes right of 30. 60 goes left of 70, 80 goes right of 70.
  3. 35 < 50, < 30? No: 35 > 30, so it continues to 40, and 35 < 40 makes it the left child of 40.
  4. Node 40 therefore has exactly one child, 35, and no right child.
  5. Splicing replaces 40 by 35: the right pointer of 30 now points at 35, and 35 keeps its own subtree, which is empty.
  6. Inorder after deletion: 20, 30, 35, 50, 60, 70, 80, still sorted, so the tree is a valid binary search tree.

Answer: Option (A), 35.

Common trap. Applying the two-child rule anyway and looking for a successor. Here the successor of 40 is 50, not 35, so that route gives the wrong key; and 45 is not even in the tree.

Takeaway. Count the children of the node being deleted before choosing a rule. One-child deletion is a pointer splice and never needs a successor or predecessor.

DS-U5-Q30MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about searching in a binary search tree with n distinct keys are TRUE?

  1. In the worst case, a search in a binary search tree with n nodes performs Θ(n) comparisons.
  2. A successful search for a key stored at depth d, with the root at depth 0, performs exactly d + 1 comparisons.
  3. Inserting n distinct keys in increasing sorted order builds a tree of height ⌈log2 n⌉.
  4. Every binary search tree with n nodes has height O(log n).
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A binary search tree gives no balance guarantee on its own. Its search cost is the height of the tree, and the height is a property of the insertion order, not of n.

Step by step.

  1. Statement (A): a tree that degenerates into a chain of n nodes has height n − 1, and a search from the root to the deepest node touches every node, so the worst case is Θ(n). True.
  2. Statement (B): the search compares the root once, then one key per edge descended. A node at depth d is reached after d edges, so d + 1 comparisons. True.
  3. Statement (C): increasing order makes every new key larger than all earlier ones, so each insertion hangs off the right of the previous node. The result is a right chain of height n − 1, not ⌈log2 n⌉. False.
  4. Statement (D): the same right-chain example has height Θ(n), so O(log n) fails for general binary search trees. False.

Answer: Options (A) and (B); (C) and (D) are FALSE.

Common trap. Assuming the average-case O(log n) of a randomly built tree is a guarantee. Sorted or nearly sorted input is the standard way to force the linear case, and it is exactly what balanced trees such as AVL trees are designed to prevent.

Takeaway. Separate the cost model from the guarantee: cost equals height plus one comparisons, and the height is O(log n) only when the tree is balanced.

DS-U5-Q31MCQhard

Each of the following sequences is inserted, in the order shown, into an empty binary search tree. For which sequence is the preorder traversal of the resulting tree NOT 50, 30, 20, 40, 70, 60, 80?

  1. 50, 30, 70, 20, 40, 60, 80
  2. 50, 30, 20, 70, 40, 60, 80
  3. 50, 70, 30, 60, 20, 40, 80
  4. 50, 30, 20, 40, 60, 70, 80
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The preorder traversal of a binary search tree determines the tree uniquely, because the first key is the root and the remaining keys split at the first value greater than the root. Once the tree is fixed, any insertion order that places every node before its own descendants rebuilds exactly that tree.

Step by step.
