  1. The target preorder gives root 50; 30 < 50 starts the left block, so the left subtree is 30 with children 20 and 40; the right block is 70 with children 60 and 80.
  2. The constraint on an insertion order is therefore: 50 first, 30 before 20 and 40, and 70 before 60 and 80. Anything else changes the shape.
  3. (A) 50, 30, 70, 20, 40, 60, 80: every ancestor precedes its descendants. The tree is the target one.
  4. (B) 50, 30, 20, 70, 40, 60, 80: 20 is inserted before 40 but 30 precedes both, so the shape is unchanged.
  5. (C) 50, 70, 30, 60, 20, 40, 80: 60 comes after 70, and 30 comes before 20 and 40. Again the target tree.
  6. (D) 50, 30, 20, 40, 60, 70, 80: 60 is inserted before 70. Since 60 > 50, it first lands as the right child of 50; then 70 > 60 becomes the right child of 60, and 80 the right child of 70. The right side is the chain 60–70–80, whose preorder is 50, 30, 20, 40, 60, 70, 80.

Answer: Option (D), 50, 30, 20, 40, 60, 70, 80.

Common trap. Believing the insertion order must be the preorder itself. It need not be: any linear extension of the ancestor relation works, which is why three different orders give the same tree here.

Takeaway. To test an insertion order, check only one thing: is every node inserted before the keys that end up in its subtree? If yes the tree is unchanged; the first violation reshapes the tree.

DS-U5-Q32NATmedium

The keys 50, 30, 70, 20, 40, 60, 80, 35, 45, 65 are inserted in that order into an empty binary search tree. The key 30 is then deleted by replacing it with its inorder successor. How many leaf nodes does the resulting tree have?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Deleting a two-child node by successor replacement copies the smallest key of the right subtree into the deleted position and then removes that successor from where it sat, which is always a node with no left child.

Step by step.

  1. Build the tree: 50 is the root; 30 is its left child with children 20 and 40; 70 is its right child with children 60 and 80.
  2. 35 > 30 goes to the right of 30 and then left of 40; 45 > 40 becomes the right child of 40; 65 < 70 and > 60 becomes the right child of 60.
  3. Node 30 has two children. Its right subtree is rooted at 40, whose leftmost key is 35, so the successor of 30 is 35.
  4. Copy 35 into the position of 30 and delete 35 from under 40. Node 35 was a leaf, so nothing takes its place.
  5. The resulting tree: root 50; left subtree 35 with children 20 and 40, where 40 has only the right child 45; right subtree 70 with children 60 and 80, where 60 has the right child 65.
  6. Leaves are the nodes with no children: 20, 45, 65 and 80, so there are four.

Answer: 4.

Common trap. Counting 35 as a leaf after the move, or leaving 35 in place as well and so reporting five leaves. The successor is relocated, not duplicated, so 40 loses its left child and 35 stops being a leaf.

Takeaway. Successor replacement moves one key and removes one node from the right subtree. Recount the leaves from scratch after the move instead of adjusting the old count.

AVL trees & rotations · 12
DS-U5-Q08MCQeasy

The keys 10, 20, 30 are inserted in that order into an empty AVL tree. Which key ends up at the root after the rebalancing?

  1. 10
  2. 20
  3. 30
  4. 15
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An insertion into an AVL tree can unbalance only the nodes on the insertion path, and the deepest unbalanced node is repaired by a rotation that lifts the middle key of the three involved to the top.

Step by step.

  1. After 10 and 20 the tree is 10 with right child 20; balance factors are 0 and 1 at worst, so no rotation.
  2. Inserting 30 gives 10 → 20 → 30, a right-right chain. At node 10 the right subtree has height 1 while the left is empty, so the balance factor is 2.
  3. Right-right means a single left rotation about 10: 20 rises, 10 becomes its left child and 30 stays its right child.
  4. The root is therefore 20, with children 10 and 30.

Answer: Option (B), 20.

Common trap. Answering 10 by assuming the first key inserted stays at the root, or answering 30 by rotating the wrong way and producing 30 with a left child 20.

Takeaway. In a right-right (or left-left) case the middle key of the three becomes the root; a single rotation is enough whenever the two imbalance directions agree.

DS-U5-Q09MCQmedium

The keys 30, 20, 10, 25, 28 are inserted in that order into an empty AVL tree. What is the preorder traversal of the final tree?

  1. 20, 10, 28, 25, 30
  2. 20, 10, 25, 28, 30
  3. 28, 20, 10, 25, 30
  4. 20, 28, 10, 25, 30
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An AVL insertion performs at most one repair, but that repair may be a double rotation, and a later insertion can trigger a second one; both must be traced in order.

Step by step.

  1. 30, 20, 10: a left-left chain at 30, so a single right rotation gives root 20 with children 10 and 30.
  2. 25: descends 20 → 30 → left, becoming the left child of 30. Balance factors stay within ±1.
  3. 28: descends 20 → 30 → 25 → right, becoming the right child of 25. Now node 30 has a left subtree of height 1 and no right subtree, and node 20 has a right subtree of height 2 against a left subtree of height 0 — a balance factor of 2 at 20.
  4. The right child 30 leans left, so this is the right-left case: first a right rotation about 30 lifts 25, then a left rotation about 20 lifts 25 again to the root of that side.
  5. Final tree: root 20, left child 10, right child 28 with children 25 and 30. Preorder is 20, 10, 28, 25, 30.

Answer: Option (A), 20, 10, 28, 25, 30.

Common trap. Stopping after the first rotation at 30 and leaving 25 as the right child of 20, which gives option (B) and an unbalanced tree.

Takeaway. A right-left case needs the inner key (25 here) to rise twice; skipping the first rotation is the standard error, and the resulting tree visibly violates the balance condition.

DS-U5-Q10MCQhard

Each of the following sequences is inserted, in the order shown, into an empty AVL tree. For which sequence does the whole insertion perform exactly one double rotation (LR or RL) and no single rotation?

  1. 10, 20, 30
  2. 20, 10, 30
  3. 30, 10, 20
  4. 30, 20, 10
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The rotation a repair needs is decided by the direction of the imbalance and the direction of the taller child: agreeing directions need one single rotation, disagreeing directions need a double rotation.

Step by step.

  1. 10, 20, 30: a right-right chain at 10, so one single left rotation gives root 20. Not a double rotation.
  2. 20, 10, 30: a balanced tree from the start, no rotation at all.
  3. 30, 10, 20: 30 gets left child 10, then 20 becomes the right child of 10. At 30 the left subtree has height 1 and the right is empty, and the left child 10 leans right — a left-right case. A left rotation about 10 followed by a right rotation about 30 gives root 20 with children 10 and 30. Exactly one double rotation, no single rotation.
  4. 30, 20, 10: a left-left chain at 30, so one single right rotation gives root 20.

Answer: Option (C), 30, 10, 20.

Common trap. Choosing 10, 20, 30 because it is the textbook first example of rebalancing — but it is the single-rotation case. The double rotation needs the two imbalance directions to disagree, which only a zig-zag insertion produces.

Takeaway. Three keys always produce root = the median, but the cost differs: sorted triples cost one single rotation, a zig-zag triple costs one double rotation, and only the zig-zag shows LR or RL.

DS-U5-Q11MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about AVL trees are TRUE?

  1. Every AVL tree is a binary search tree.
  2. For every node, the heights of its left and right subtrees differ by at most 1.
  3. An AVL tree with n nodes has height O(log n).
  4. A single insertion into an AVL tree may require more than two rotations.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An AVL tree is a BST plus a balance invariant, and the invariant is what converts the O(h) bound of a plain BST into an O(log n) bound.

Step by step.

  1. Statement (A): the AVL condition is imposed on top of the BST ordering, which the rotations are designed to preserve, so every AVL tree is a BST. True.
  2. Statement (B): this is the defining balance condition. True.
  3. Statement (C): the minimum number of nodes in an AVL tree of height h grows like φh, so h = O(log n) with base φ ≈ 1.618. True.
  4. Statement (D): an insertion unbalances only nodes on the insertion path, and the deepest unbalanced node is repaired by one single rotation or one double rotation; after that repair every node on the path is balanced again, so at most two elementary rotations occur. False.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C); (D) is FALSE.

Common trap. Believing that every node on the insertion path needs its own rotation. Only the deepest unbalanced node is repaired, and the repair fixes the ancestors as well.

Takeaway. One insertion costs at most one single or one double rotation; it is deletion that can need O(log n) rotations, which is the usual place this pair of facts gets mixed up.

DS-U5-Q12NATeasy

What is the minimum number of nodes in an AVL tree of height 4, where the height of a tree is the number of edges on the longest root-to-leaf path?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The sparsest AVL tree of a given height is built by making the two subtrees as unbalanced as the rule allows: heights h − 1 and h − 2. That recurrence gives the minimum node count N(h) = N(h−1) + N(h−2) + 1.

Step by step.

  1. Base cases: N(0) = 1 (a single node) and N(1) = 2 (a root with one child).
  2. N(2) = N(1) + N(0) + 1 = 2 + 1 + 1 = 4.
  3. N(3) = N(2) + N(1) + 1 = 4 + 2 + 1 = 7.
  4. N(4) = N(3) + N(2) + 1 = 7 + 4 + 1 = 12.

Answer: 12.

Common trap. Using the complete-tree count 2h+1 − 1 = 31, which is the maximum, not the minimum; or counting height in levels, which would answer 7.

Takeaway. The sequence 1, 2, 4, 7, 12, 20, 33 is the AVL minimum-node sequence; it is what makes the height logarithmic and it is worth memorising to height 5.

DS-U5-Q13MCQmedium

The keys 50, 40, 60, 30, 45, 55, 70, 20 are inserted in that order into an empty AVL tree. How many rotations, single or double, does the whole insertion sequence perform?

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. 0
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A rotation is performed only when a node's balance factor reaches ±2. A tree can be lopsided and still perfectly legal, so the balance factor must actually be computed at every step rather than assumed from the shape.

Step by step.

  1. 50, 40, 60, 30, 45, 55, 70: a complete tree of height 2, every balance factor within ±1, no rotation.
  2. Inserting 20 descends 50 → 40 → 30 and becomes the left child of 30.
  3. Check the path bottom-up. Node 30: left subtree height 0, right subtree empty, factor −1. Node 40: left subtree (30 with child 20) has height 1, right subtree (45) has height 0, factor −1. Node 50: left subtree height 2, right subtree (60 with 55 and 70) height 1, factor −1.
  4. Every factor is within ±1, so no rotation is triggered anywhere on the path.
  5. Final tree: root 50, left 40 with 30 (20) and 45, right 60 with 55 and 70. Preorder 50, 40, 30, 20, 45, 60, 55, 70.

Answer: Option (D), 0 rotations.

Common trap. Assuming that eight keys cannot possibly stay balanced, or counting 20 as a left-left case at 40 — 40's right subtree 45 is not empty, so the two sides differ by only one.

Takeaway. Compute balance factors bottom-up along the insertion path; an imbalance needs a difference of 2, and one short right subtree is often enough to keep a chain legal.

DS-U5-Q33MCQeasy

In an AVL tree the balance factor of a node is defined as the height of its left subtree minus the height of its right subtree. A node has balance factor −1. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. Its right subtree is exactly one level taller than its left subtree.
  2. Its left subtree is exactly one level taller than its right subtree.
  3. Its two subtrees have equal height.
  4. The node breaks the AVL condition and must be rotated.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The balance factor encodes the difference of the two subtree heights, and its sign carries the direction. A negative value means the right side is heavier, a positive value means the left side is heavier, and zero means the two sides are level.

Step by step.

  1. Balance factor = height(left) − height(right) = −1.
  2. Rearranging, height(left) = height(right) − 1, so the left subtree is one level shorter, that is the right subtree is one level taller.
  3. The AVL condition allows balance factors of −1, 0 and +1, so −1 is legal and no rotation is triggered.

Answer: Option (A).

Common trap. Reversing the sign convention and calling −1 a left-heavy node. Always read the definition given in the question: left minus right makes −1 a right-heavy node.

Takeaway. Write the balance-factor definition at the top of any AVL question. Every rotation choice then follows mechanically from the sign at the unbalanced node and the sign at its taller child.

DS-U5-Q34MCQmedium

The keys 15, 10, 20, 5, 12, 25, 30 are inserted in that order into an empty AVL tree. What is the preorder traversal of the final tree?

  1. 15, 10, 5, 12, 25, 20, 30
  2. 15, 10, 5, 12, 20, 25, 30
  3. 25, 15, 10, 5, 12, 20, 30
  4. 15, 10, 12, 5, 25, 20, 30
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An AVL insertion repairs only the deepest unbalanced node on the insertion path, using one single or one double rotation, and that repair restores the balance of every ancestor as well.

Step by step.

  1. 15, 10, 20, 5, 12: the tree is 15 with left child 10 (children 5 and 12) and right child 20. Every balance factor is within ±1.
  2. Insert 25: it becomes the right child of 20. Heights at 20 are left 0 and right 1, factor −1; at 15 they are left 2 and right 2, factor 0. Still balanced.
  3. Insert 30: it descends 15 → 20 → 25 and becomes the right child of 25.
  4. At node 20 the left subtree is empty and the right subtree has height 2, so the factor is −2. Its taller child 25 has factor −1, the same direction, so a single left rotation at 20 is enough.
  5. After the rotation 25 takes the place of 20, with left child 20 and right child 30. Node 15 now has left height 2 and right height 2, factor 0.
  6. Final tree: 15 with left child 10 (children 5, 12) and right child 25 (children 20, 30). Preorder: 15, 10, 5, 12, 25, 20, 30.

Answer: Option (A), 15, 10, 5, 12, 25, 20, 30.

Common trap. Reporting the plain binary search tree order 15, 10, 5, 12, 20, 25, 30 by forgetting the rotation entirely, or rotating at 15 instead of at the deepest unbalanced node 20.

Takeaway. Rotate at the lowest node whose factor reaches ±2. Rotating higher up would not fix the actual violation, and the repair would have to be undone later.

DS-U5-Q35NATeasy

What is the maximum number of nodes in an AVL tree of height 3, where the height is the number of edges on the longest root-to-leaf path?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. To pack as many nodes as possible into a fixed height, fill every level completely. A complete binary tree of height h satisfies the AVL condition, because at every node the two subtree heights differ by at most one.

Step by step.

  1. Height 0 holds at most 1 node.
  2. Height 1 holds at most 1 + 2 = 3 nodes.
  3. Height 2 holds at most 1 + 2 + 4 = 7 nodes.
  4. Height 3 holds at most 1 + 2 + 4 + 8 = 15 nodes.
  5. Equivalently, 2h+1 − 1 with h = 3 gives 16 − 1 = 15.
  6. The complete tree of height 3 is itself AVL, so the bound is attained and not merely an upper limit.

Answer: 15.

Common trap. Answering 7 by treating the height as the number of levels, or answering 12 by quoting the minimum-node sequence instead of the maximum.

Takeaway. Keep the two AVL bounds apart: at most 2h+1 − 1 nodes, at least N(h) = N(h−1) + N(h−2) + 1 nodes, where the minimum sequence is 1, 2, 4, 7, 12, 20.

DS-U5-Q36MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about AVL trees are TRUE?

  1. The balance factor of every node is −1, 0 or 1.
  2. Every complete binary tree satisfies the AVL balance condition.
  3. A rotation preserves the inorder sequence of the keys in the tree.
  4. An AVL tree with 20 nodes cannot have height 5, where height is measured in edges.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An AVL tree is a binary search tree plus a height invariant, and the rotations used to restore that invariant are chosen so that the ordering of keys never changes.

Step by step.

  1. (A) The invariant is exactly |height(left) − height(right)| ≤ 1 at every node, so the factor lies in {−1, 0, 1}. True.
  2. (B) In a complete binary tree every level except the last is full, so at any node the two subtree heights differ by at most one. True.
  3. (C) A rotation only rewires parent and child pointers inside a subtree whose keys keep their relative order, so the inorder listing is unchanged. True.
  4. (D) The sparsest AVL tree of height h satisfies N(h) = N(h−1) + N(h−2) + 1 with N(0) = 1, N(1) = 2, giving 4, 7, 12, 20 for h = 2, 3, 4, 5. Exactly 20 nodes is enough for height 5, so such a tree exists. False.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C); (D) is FALSE.

Common trap. Treating the minimum-node sequence 1, 2, 4, 7, 12, 20 as an exclusive bound and concluding that height 5 needs more than 20 nodes. The value 20 is attainable, not exceeded.

Takeaway. For any “can an AVL tree of n nodes have height h” question, compare n with N(h): the height is possible exactly when N(h) ≤ n ≤ 2h+1 − 1.

DS-U5-Q37MCQhard

The keys 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7 are inserted in that order into an empty AVL tree. Which key is at the root of the final tree?

  1. 3
  2. 5
  3. 4
  4. 7
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Inserting keys in increasing order into an AVL tree triggers repeated single left rotations, and the tree that results is the complete tree of the same size, not a chain.

Step by step.

  1. Insert 1, 2, 3: 3 creates a right-right chain at 1, so one left rotation makes 2 the root with children 1 and 3.
  2. Insert 4: it becomes the right child of 3. Balance factors stay within ±1.
  3. Insert 5: it makes 3’s right subtree a chain 4–5, so a left rotation at 3 lifts 4 above 3 and 5. The root 2 now has left height 1 and right height 2.
  4. Insert 6: the right subtree becomes 4 with children 3 and 5, and 5 gains the right child 6. The factor at 2 reaches −2, so a left rotation at 2 lifts 4 to the root: 4 with left child 2 (children 1 and 3) and right child 5 (right child 6).
  5. Insert 7: it makes 6’s right subtree a chain, so a left rotation at 5 lifts 6 above 5 and 7.
  6. The root stays 4, and the tree is 4 with left child 2 (children 1, 3) and right child 6 (children 5, 7), the complete tree on seven keys.

Answer: Option (C), 4.

Common trap. Guessing the middle key 4 because it is the median. That happens to be right here, but the median argument is not the reason and it fails for other sizes; also easy to answer 5 by forgetting the final rotation after inserting 6.

Takeaway. Sorted insertion into an AVL tree builds a complete tree level by level. For seven keys the complete tree has root 4, and four elementary rotations are performed in total.

DS-U5-Q38NAThard

The keys 100, 50, 150, 25, 75, 60, 90, 55, 58 are inserted in that order into an empty AVL tree. Counting a single rotation as one elementary rotation and a double rotation as two, how many elementary rotations does the whole insertion sequence perform?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A rotation is performed only when some node reaches a balance factor of ±2. A single rotation repairs an outside case (LL or RR), a double rotation repairs an inside case (LR or RL).

Step by step.

  1. 100, 50, 150, 25, 75: the tree is 100 with left child 50 (children 25 and 75) and right child 150. All factors are within ±1.
  2. 60 becomes the left child of 75, and 90 the right child of 75. At 50 the left height is 1 and the right height is 2, factor −1; no rotation.
  3. 55 becomes the left child of 60. At 60 the left height is 1 and the right 0, factor +1; at 50 the left height is 1 and the right height is 3, factor −2.
  4. At node 50 the taller child 75 has factor +1, the opposite direction, so this is an RL case: a right rotation at 75 followed by a left rotation at 50, two elementary rotations. Node 60 now sits above 50 and 75.
  5. 58 descends 100 → 50 → 60 → 55 and becomes the right child of 55. At 55 the factor is −1, at 60 the left height is 2 against 0 on the right, factor +2.
  6. At node 60 the taller child 55 has factor −1, the opposite direction, so this is an LR case: a left rotation at 55 then a right rotation at 60, two more elementary rotations. Node 58 sits above 55 and 60.
  7. Total: 2 + 2 = 4 elementary rotations. The final root is 75, with preorder 75, 50, 25, 58, 55, 60, 100, 90, 150.

Answer: 4.

Common trap. Counting each double rotation as one event and answering 2, or counting the whole insertion as needing a rotation at every ancestor and answering far too many. Also easy to rotate at 100 rather than at the deepest unbalanced node 50 or 60.

Takeaway. Walk the insertion path bottom-up and stop at the first node with factor ±2. Compare its sign with the sign of its taller child: same sign means one rotation, opposite sign means two.

B-trees & B+ trees · 12
DS-U5-Q14MCQeasy

In a B-tree of order 5, where the order is the maximum number of children a node may have, what is the maximum number of keys a single node can hold?

  1. 5
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 2
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A B-tree node with c children separates them with c − 1 keys, so the child bound and the key bound differ by exactly one.

Step by step.

  1. The order fixes the maximum number of children: 5.
  2. Between and around those 5 children sit 5 − 1 = 4 separator keys.
  3. So a node holds at most 4 keys, and the same node, when it holds 4 keys, is full.

Answer: Option (C), 4.

Common trap. Answering 5, which is the child bound rather than the key bound. The minimum side is just as easily confused: at least ⌈5/2⌉ − 1 = 2 keys for a non-root node.

Takeaway. Memorise the pair together: order m means at most m children, at most m − 1 keys, and at least ⌈m/2⌉ − 1 keys for every node except the root.

DS-U5-Q15MCQmedium

A B+ tree is stored on 4096-byte disk blocks. Each key occupies 8 bytes and each pointer 6 bytes, and an internal node stores n pointers together with n − 1 keys. What is the largest n for which an internal node still fits in one block?

  1. 256
  2. 292
  3. 294
  4. 293
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The fan-out of an internal node is a packing question: pointers and keys share the block, so the constraint is 6n + 8(n − 1) ≤ block size, and n must be an integer.

Step by step.

  1. Write the space used by an internal node with n pointers: 6n + 8(n − 1) = 14n − 8 bytes.
  2. Require 14n − 8 ≤ 4096, so 14n ≤ 4104 and n ≤ 293.14.
  3. Check n = 293: 6(293) + 8(292) = 1758 + 2336 = 4094 bytes, which fits.
  4. Check n = 294: 6(294) + 8(293) = 1764 + 2344 = 4108 bytes, which overflows the block by 12 bytes.

Answer: Option (D), 293.

Common trap. Dividing 4096 by the 14 bytes per pointer-key pair and rounding to the nearest integer, giving 294; the −8 byte term makes the exact bound one lower, and the rounded-up answer overflows the block.

Takeaway. Always check the candidate and its successor numerically; with a subtraction term in the inequality, rounding to nearest is wrong and the floor of the exact quotient is right.

DS-U5-Q16MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about B+ trees are TRUE?

  1. Every record pointer is stored at the leaf level.
  2. The leaf nodes are linked in key order, so a range query need not return to the root for each key.
  3. A key that appears in an internal node is stored again in a leaf.
  4. Internal nodes store record pointers as well as keys.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A B+ tree separates the index from the data: internal nodes hold only separator keys and child pointers, and every record lives in a leaf, which is what makes the height uniform and range scans cheap.

Step by step.

  1. Statement (A): the leaves are the only level that carries record pointers. True.
  2. Statement (B): the leaves form a linked list in key order, so a scan follows the links instead of re-descending. True.
  3. Statement (C): a separator key is a copy of a key that also exists in a leaf, which is why a search must always descend to the leaf. True.
  4. Statement (D): internal nodes hold keys and child pointers only; the record pointers are in the leaves. False.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C); (D) is FALSE.

Common trap. Assuming that finding a key in an internal node ends the search, as it does in a B-tree. In a B+ tree the internal copy is only a signpost, so the search continues to a leaf.

Takeaway. The B-tree answers point queries in fewer levels; the B+ tree trades that for a uniform leaf level and a linked leaf chain, which is why file systems and database indexes use it.

DS-U5-Q17MSQhard

Consider a B-tree of order m, where m is the maximum number of children a node may have and every node except the root holds at least ⌈m/2⌉ − 1 keys. Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. Every internal node other than the root has at least ⌈m/2⌉ children.
  2. All leaves lie at the same level.
  3. For a fixed number of keys n, the height of the tree grows linearly in n.
  4. When the tree has more than one node, the root may hold as few as one key.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The B-tree invariants are an occupancy floor, an occupancy ceiling and a level condition; together they bound the height by a logarithm of the key count.

Step by step.

  1. Statement (A): a node with at least ⌈m/2⌉ − 1 keys has at least one more child than keys, so at least ⌈m/2⌉ children. True.
  2. Statement (B): every leaf is at the same depth by the definition of a B-tree, which is what keeps every search the same length. True.
  3. Statement (C): the minimum fan-out is 2 at the root and ⌈m/2⌉ below it, so the number of keys is at least exponential in the height and the height is O(log⌈m/2⌉ n), not linear. False.
  4. Statement (D): the root is exempt from the occupancy floor and may hold a single key with two children. True.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D); (C) is FALSE.

Common trap. Applying the occupancy floor to the root and concluding that the root needs ⌈m/2⌉ − 1 keys, which would forbid the one-key root that every growing tree passes through.

Takeaway. The floor applies to every node except the root, the ceiling applies to all nodes, and the equal-leaf-depth rule is what makes the height bound O(log n) instead of O(n).

DS-U5-Q18MCQmedium

In a B-tree of order 5, that is with at most 5 children per node, what is the minimum number of children the root can have when the tree contains more than one node?

  1. 2
  2. 1
  3. 3
  4. 4
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The root is the one node exempt from the occupancy floor, but a root with a single child would be a redundant level: the tree is only allowed to grow a level when the root splits into two.

Step by step.

  1. A non-root internal node of order 5 needs at least ⌈5/2⌉ − 1 = 2 keys, hence at least 3 children.
  2. The root needs at least 1 key, hence at least 2 children, whenever it is not a leaf.
  3. A root with exactly 1 child would mean every search passes through it without a comparison that could discriminate, so it is not permitted as a stable state.
  4. The minimum is therefore 2, and this is exactly what a root split produces.

Answer: Option (A), 2.

Common trap. Answering 3 by applying the non-root floor to the root, or 1 by taking "at least one key" literally and forgetting that a one-child root is collapsed.

Takeaway. The root holds between 1 and m − 1 keys and between 2 and m children; the non-root floor ⌈m/2⌉ − 1 keys applies only below the root.

DS-U5-Q19NAThard

A B-tree of order 5 holds at most 5 children and at most 4 keys per node. What is the maximum total number of keys in such a tree of height 2, where the height counts edges from the root and all leaves are at the same level?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The maximum key count of a B-tree of a given height is obtained by filling every node to the ceiling: 4 keys per node and the full 5-way branching at every internal level.

Step by step.

  1. Height 2 means three levels: the root at level 1, its children at level 2, and the leaves at level 3.
  2. Level 1: the root holds at most 4 keys.
  3. Level 2: the root has at most 5 children, each holding at most 4 keys, so 5 × 4 = 20 keys.
  4. Level 3: each of those 5 nodes has at most 5 children, giving 5 × 5 = 25 nodes, each with at most 4 keys, so 25 × 4 = 100 keys.
  5. Total 4 + 20 + 100 = 124.

Answer: 124.

Common trap. Reading height 2 as two levels and answering 24; or using 5 keys per node instead of 4, which gives 155.

Takeaway. Keys at level i of a full B-tree of order m number mi−1(m − 1) counting from the root, so the total is (m − 1)(1 + m + … + mh) = mh+1 − 1, here 53 − 1 = 124.

DS-U5-Q39MCQeasy

In a B+ tree of order 3, where the order is the maximum number of children an internal node may have, what is the maximum number of keys an internal node can hold?

  1. 3
  2. 2
  3. 4
  4. 1
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An internal node with k children separates them with k − 1 keys, because each key is a boundary between two neighbouring child pointers. The maximum key count is therefore one less than the maximum child count.

Step by step.

  1. Order m = 3 means at most 3 children per node.
  2. Children and keys are paired as pointer, key, pointer, key, pointer, so keys = children − 1.
  3. Maximum keys = 3 − 1 = 2.
  4. Minimum keys for a non-root node is ⌈m/2⌉ − 1 = 2 − 1 = 1, so a legal internal node holds 1 or 2 keys.

Answer: Option (B), 2.

Common trap. Reading the order as the key capacity and answering 3. The order is always the child capacity, so the key capacity is m − 1.

Takeaway. Convert the order to a key range once and reuse it: a node of order m holds between ⌈m/2⌉ − 1 and m − 1 keys, the root being allowed as few as one.

DS-U5-Q40MCQmedium

A B-tree of order 4 has height 3, measured in edges from the root, and every node other than the root holds at least ⌈m/2⌉ − 1 keys. What is the minimum number of keys such a tree can contain?

  1. 7
  2. 12
  3. 15
  4. 8
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The minimum key count is obtained by making every node as empty as the rules allow, which minimises both the number of nodes per level and the keys inside each node.

Step by step.

  1. Order m = 4 gives a minimum of ⌈4/2⌉ = 2 children and therefore ⌈4/2⌉ − 1 = 1 key for every non-root node.
  2. The root must have at least 2 children when the tree has more than one node, so it holds 1 key.
  3. Level 1: 2 nodes, 1 key each, 2 keys.
  4. Level 2: each level-1 node has 2 children, so 4 nodes, 1 key each, 4 keys.
  5. Level 3: 8 nodes, 1 key each, 8 keys.
  6. Total = 1 + 2 + 4 + 8 = 15 keys.

Answer: Option (C), 15.

Common trap. Using 2 keys per node because the maximum is 3, which gives 45, or forgetting the root’s single key and answering 14.

Takeaway. Minimum keys at height h in a B-tree of order m is 1 + 2·(⌈m/2⌉) + 2·(⌈m/2⌉)2 + …, with each term weighted by the minimum keys per node; here every weight is 1, so the sum is just the node count.

DS-U5-Q41MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about B-trees are TRUE?

  1. All leaves of a B-tree lie at the same level.
  2. A B-tree of order m stores at most m − 1 keys in a node.
  3. When a node overflows, its median key moves up into the parent node.
  4. A B-tree may store the same key in more than one node.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A B-tree is a balanced multiway search tree: every root-to-leaf path has the same length, every node holds a bounded number of sorted keys, and overflow is handled by splitting rather than by growing one branch.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Equal leaf depth is part of the definition, which is what keeps every search the same length. True.
  2. (B) Order m bounds the children at m and the keys at m − 1, since k children need k − 1 separators. True.
  3. (C) A split promotes the median of the overflowing node to the parent and divides the rest into two nodes. True.
  4. (D) A key appears exactly once in a B-tree; duplication of separator keys is a B+ tree property, not a B-tree one. False.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C); (D) is FALSE.

Common trap. Mixing the B-tree and B+ tree definitions. In a B-tree each key is stored once and may carry a record pointer at any node; in a B+ tree keys are repeated, with all records at the leaves.

Takeaway. Three facts cover most B-tree theory questions: equal leaf depth, keys between ⌈m/2⌉ − 1 and m − 1 per non-root node, and split by promoting the median.

DS-U5-Q42MSQhard

Which of the following statements about B+ trees are TRUE?

  1. In a B+ tree, a successful search for a key always ends at a leaf.
  2. A root-to-leaf path in a B+ tree of height h, measured in edges, visits at most h + 1 nodes.
  3. Deleting a key from a B+ tree may leave every internal node unchanged.
  4. A B+ tree stores each key exactly once in the whole tree.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a B+ tree the internal nodes form an index that only routes the search; every record, and therefore every key that carries data, lives in the leaf level, and the leaves are chained in key order.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Internal nodes hold separator keys that merely direct the descent; the matching record is always found in a leaf. True.
  2. (B) A path of h edges passes through h + 1 nodes counting the root and the leaf. True.
  3. (C) If the leaf still has enough keys after the removal and the deleted key is not a separator in an ancestor, no internal node changes at all. True.
  4. (D) A key that acts as a separator in an internal node is stored again in the leaf that holds its record, so keys can appear more than once. False.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C); (D) is FALSE.

Common trap. Assuming a deletion must ripple up to the root. Only an underfull leaf, or the removal of a separator key, forces a change above the leaf level.

Takeaway. Remember the division of labour: internal nodes route, leaves store and are linked. Point queries cost the height, range queries cost the height plus the leaves scanned.

DS-U5-Q43MCQmedium

A B-tree of order 5 holds more than one node and has height 1, that is a root plus one level of leaves. Every node other than the root holds at least ⌈m/2⌉ − 1 keys. What is the minimum number of keys the tree can contain?

  1. 3
  2. 5
  3. 4
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The occupancy floor is what makes a B-tree dense: a node may not be arbitrarily empty, so a given height forces a minimum number of keys.

Step by step.

  1. Order m = 5, so every non-root node holds at least ⌈5/2⌉ − 1 = 3 − 1 = 2 keys and has at least 3 children.
  2. A root with more than one node in the tree must have at least 2 children, and 2 children need 1 key.
  3. Height 1 means exactly two levels, so there are two leaves, each holding its minimum of 2 keys.
  4. Total = 1 + 2 + 2 = 5 keys.

Answer: Option (B), 5.

Common trap. Giving each leaf only one key, which would answer 3. A leaf of order 5 must hold at least 2 keys, so one-key leaves are not legal here.

Takeaway. Always compute the floor ⌈m/2⌉ − 1 first. The floor applies to every non-root node, leaves included, and it is what makes the minimum-height calculation meaningful.

DS-U5-Q44MCQmedium

The keys 10, 20, 30, 40, 50 are inserted in that order into an empty B-tree of order 3, that is at most 3 children and at most 2 keys per node. Which keys are stored in the root of the final tree?

  1. 30 only
  2. 20 only
  3. 30 and 40
  4. 20 and 40
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A node that would exceed m − 1 keys splits: its median key rises into the parent and the remaining keys form two nodes. When the root splits, the tree grows one level.

Step by step.

  1. Insert 10 and 20: the root holds [10, 20], which is the maximum for order 3.
  2. Insert 30: the root would hold three keys, so it splits. The median 20 goes up into a new root, and [10] and [30] become its two children.
  3. Insert 40: it descends past 20 to the right leaf, which becomes [30, 40].
  4. Insert 50: that leaf would hold [30, 40, 50], so it splits. Its median 40 rises to the root, which becomes [20, 40], and the leaf becomes [30] and [50].
  5. Final tree: root [20, 40] with the three leaves [10], [30] and [50].

Answer: Option (D), 20 and 40.

Common trap. Promoting the largest key 50 instead of the median, or stopping after the first split and reporting 20 alone.

Takeaway. Splitting always promotes the median, never the maximum or the minimum, and a split can cascade upwards one level at a time.

Trie basics · 12
DS-U5-Q20MCQeasy

The strings cat, car and dog are stored in a standard trie with one node per distinct prefix character and one root node. Counting the root, how many nodes does the trie have?

  1. 7
  2. 9
  3. 8
  4. 10
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A trie merges the prefixes of its keys: one node per distinct prefix, so shared prefixes cost nothing extra and the node count is the number of distinct prefixes including the empty one.

Step by step.

  1. The root represents the empty prefix: 1 node.
  2. cat contributes c, a, t. car reuses c and a and adds only r. So the c-branch holds 4 nodes after the root.
  3. dog contributes d, o, g, none of them shared: 3 more nodes.
  4. Total 1 + 4 + 3 = 8.

Answer: Option (C), 8.

Common trap. Counting one node per character of every key, which gives 9 and ignores that car shares c and a with cat.

Takeaway. The node count is the number of distinct prefixes, not the total length of the keys; a trie saves exactly the length of every shared prefix.

DS-U5-Q21MCQeasy

A trie stores n keys whose maximum length is L characters, and each child lookup takes O(1) time. What is the worst-case time to search for a key of length L?

  1. O(1)
  2. O(log n)
  3. O(L)
  4. O(n)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A trie search is a walk down one root-to-node path with one step per character, so its cost is set by the length of the key, not by how many keys are stored.

Step by step.

  1. The search starts at the root and reads the key character by character.
  2. At each step it performs one child lookup, which is O(1) under the stated assumption.
  3. A key of length L uses exactly L steps, whatever n is.
  4. So the cost is O(L), which is O(1) only when the alphabet and key length are bounded.

Answer: Option (C), O(L).

Common trap. Answering O(log n) by analogy with balanced search trees, or O(1) by analogy with hashing. A trie is neither: it pays per character, and a long key is slow even in a small trie.

Takeaway. This is the trie's headline property and also its cost: comparisons per key drop to zero, but the work becomes proportional to key length.

DS-U5-Q22MCQhard

The eight strings bear, bell, bid, bull, buy, sell, stock and stop are stored in a standard trie with one node per distinct prefix character and one root node. Counting the root, how many nodes does the trie have?

  1. 21
  2. 22
  3. 24
  4. 19
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each key adds exactly the characters that extend the longest prefix it shares with the keys already inserted, so the node count is one plus the sum over keys of that extension length.

Step by step.

  1. bear: b, e, a, r are all new → 4 nodes.
  2. bell: b and e exist; l, l are new → 2 nodes.
  3. bid: b exists; i, d are new → 2 nodes.
  4. bull: b exists; u, l, l are new → 3 nodes.
  5. buy: b and u exist; y is new → 1 node.
  6. sell: s, e, l, l are all new → 4 nodes. Note e here is a different node from the e under b.
  7. stock: s exists; t, o, c, k are new → 4 nodes.
  8. stop: s, t, o exist; p is new → 1 node.
  9. Total 1 + 4 + 2 + 2 + 3 + 1 + 4 + 4 + 1 = 22.

Answer: Option (B), 22.

Common trap. Sharing the e of bell with the e of sell, which gives 21; a trie branches at the root by first character, so those two e's are distinct nodes. Counting only the 21 characters added and forgetting the root gives 21 as well.

Takeaway. Add the characters key by key and only merge a prefix with a key already inserted; count the root separately and last.

DS-U5-Q23MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about standard tries are TRUE?

  1. The time to look up a key of length L does not depend on how many keys the trie holds.
  2. A trie holding n keys always uses O(n) nodes, whatever the lengths of the keys.
  3. Keys that share a prefix share the nodes on the path for that prefix.
  4. Storing a key that is a proper prefix of another key requires an explicit end-of-key marker.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A trie indexes characters, not keys: the path encodes the key and the node count is driven by the total number of distinct prefixes, which is why its cost model differs from every comparison-based structure.

Step by step.

  1. Statement (A): a lookup walks L nodes, one per character, and never inspects the other keys. True.
  2. Statement (B): the node count is bounded by the total number of characters over all keys, which is unbounded in n alone. Counterexample: two keys of length 1000 need about 2000 nodes. False.
  3. Statement (C): that sharing is the definition of the structure. True.
  4. Statement (D): without a marker, the node ending "cat" cannot be distinguished from an internal node on the way to "cater". True.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D); (B) is FALSE.

Common trap. Assuming the node count is linear in the number of keys. It is linear in the total length of the keys, which is the real space cost of a trie and the reason compressed tries exist.

Takeaway. A trie trades space for time: O(L) lookups at O(total characters) nodes. A compressed (radix) trie merges unary chains to bring the space back down.

DS-U5-Q24MCQeasy

The children of every node of a trie are visited in alphabetical order. Which traversal then lists all stored keys in lexicographic order?

  1. Postorder depth-first
  2. Preorder depth-first
  3. Breadth-first
  4. Reverse inorder
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Lexicographic order is the order in which a depth-first walk that visits children alphabetically reaches the nodes, so the keys are emitted as soon as their node is entered, before any sibling branch is explored.

Step by step.

  1. In a preorder walk a node is reported before its children, so a key is reported the moment its last character is reached.
  2. Visiting the alphabetically smaller child first means every key with a smaller next character is reported before any key with a larger one.
  3. That is exactly the lexicographic rule, and it also places a prefix key before all keys that extend it.
  4. A postorder walk would report a key only after all longer keys that extend it, reversing the required order for prefixes; breadth-first mixes lengths.

Answer: Option (B), preorder depth-first.

Common trap. Choosing postorder, which is the traversal that would order "cater" before "cat" — the opposite of lexicographic order. Breadth-first is tempting because it looks ordered, but it sorts by length.

Takeaway. Lexicographic order on a trie is a preorder DFS with sorted children; this is the mechanism behind autocomplete and the reason tries are used for ordered dictionaries.

DS-U5-Q25NATmedium

The five strings do, dog, done, cat and car are stored in a standard trie with one node per distinct prefix character and one root node. Counting the root, how many nodes does the trie have?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The node count of a trie is one plus the number of distinct non-empty prefixes of its keys; keys that are prefixes of other keys add no nodes of their own.

Step by step.

  1. Start at the root: 1 node.
  2. do contributes d, o → 2 nodes.
  3. dog extends do by g → 1 node.
  4. done shares do and extends by n, e → 2 nodes. Note that do, dog and done all branch from the same o.
  5. cat contributes c, a, t → 3 nodes, since c and a are new.
  6. car shares c and a and adds only r → 1 node.
  7. Total 1 + 2 + 1 + 2 + 3 + 1 = 10.

Answer: 10.

Common trap. Counting one node per character of every key, which gives 12 by double-counting the shared d, o and the shared c, a; or forgetting that do, dog and done share the node for o.

Takeaway. List the distinct prefixes rather than the characters: root, d, do, dog, don, done, c, ca, cat, car gives ten nodes, which is also the safest way to check the count by hand.

DS-U5-Q45MCQeasy

The single string banana is stored in a standard trie with one node per distinct prefix character and one root node. Counting the root, how many nodes does the trie have?

  1. 6
  2. 8
  3. 12
  4. 7
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A standard trie stores one node per distinct prefix of the stored keys, plus a root that represents the empty prefix. A single key of length L therefore contributes exactly L nodes.

Step by step.

  1. Prefixes of banana: the empty prefix, b, ba, ban, bana, banan, banana.
  2. That is 6 non-empty prefixes, so 6 nodes below the root.
  3. Adding the root gives 6 + 1 = 7 nodes.

Answer: Option (D), 7.

Common trap. Counting the repeated letters as one node because the alphabet is small, which gives 4 or 5, or forgetting the root and answering 6.

Takeaway. Nodes correspond to prefixes, not to distinct characters. Repetition inside one key still costs one node per position.

DS-U5-Q46MCQmedium

A standard trie is implemented with one array of 26 child pointers per node, each pointer taking 4 bytes, plus a 1-byte end-of-key flag. How many bytes does a single trie node occupy?

  1. 104
  2. 105
  3. 130
  4. 108
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The array-of-children representation trades memory for O(1) child lookup: every node reserves a slot for every letter of the alphabet whether or not that letter occurs.

Step by step.

  1. Children: 26 slots × 4 bytes = 104 bytes.
  2. End-of-key flag: 1 byte.
  3. Total per node: 104 + 1 = 105 bytes.
  4. Compare this with the n·L + 1 node bound: a trie holding 500 keys of length 8 can reach 4001 nodes, which at 105 bytes each is about 420 KB, most of it empty child slots.

Answer: Option (B), 105 bytes.

Common trap. Answering 104 by forgetting the end-of-key flag, which is what lets a key end at an internal node; or assuming each node stores only the characters actually present, which is the compressed, not the array-based, representation.

Takeaway. The array representation makes lookups O(1) per character at the cost of Θ(σ) space per node. Terminating a key needs an explicit flag because keys can end at internal nodes.

DS-U5-Q47MCQhard

The seven strings ant, ante, anti, bat, batch, ban and band are stored in a standard trie with one node per distinct prefix character and one root node. Counting the root, how many nodes does the trie have?

  1. 12
  2. 14
  3. 13
  4. 16
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The node count equals the number of distinct prefixes of the stored keys, counting the empty prefix as the root. Shared prefixes are counted once, which is exactly where the saving over 7 × 5 comes from.

Step by step.

  1. Under the root, the first characters are a and b, so two nodes at level 1.
  2. Branch a: a → n → t gives ant. From that t the children e and i give ante and anti. So a contributes a, n, t, e, i = 5 nodes.
  3. Branch b: b → a. From that a, one child t and one child n. The t branch adds c and h for batch, so b, a, t, c, h = 5 nodes; the n branch adds d for band, so n and d = 2 more.
  4. Total below the root: 5 + 5 + 2 = 12 nodes.
  5. Adding the root: 13 nodes.

Answer: Option (C), 13.

Common trap. Counting 7 × 4 characters and forgetting that ant, ante and anti share a, n, t; or forgetting that ban is a proper prefix of band, which is why the node for ban must be marked as an end of key rather than being a leaf.

Takeaway. Build the trie character by character and count nodes as you create them. A key that is a proper prefix of another ends at an internal node, which needs an explicit end marker.

DS-U5-Q48MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about standard tries are TRUE?

  1. Inserting a key of length L takes O(L) time when each child lookup is O(1).
  2. A trie holding n keys of length at most L has at most n·L + 1 nodes, counting the root.
  3. Two keys that differ in their first character share no node below the root.
  4. In a standard trie, keys are stored only at leaves and never at internal nodes.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A trie moves the work from key comparisons to character steps: the path spelling a key is the key, so cost is measured per character and sharing happens along common prefixes.

Step by step.

  1. (A) The insertion follows one child pointer per character and creates a node only where the path is missing, so it costs O(L). True.
  2. (B) Each of the n keys creates at most L new nodes, and the root is one more, so n·L + 1 is a valid upper bound. True.
  3. (C) The root’s children are indexed by first character, so keys with different first characters leave through different children and never meet again. True.
  4. (D) A key that is a proper prefix of another ends at an internal node, which is marked as an end of key; keys are therefore not confined to leaves. False.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C); (D) is FALSE.

Common trap. Carrying over the B+ tree rule that all records live at the leaves. A trie has no such rule, which is why it needs an end-of-key flag.

Takeaway. Trie cost is O(L) per operation, independent of n, and its size is bounded by the total number of characters stored plus the root.

DS-U5-Q49NATeasy

The three strings in, inn and int are stored in a standard trie with one node per distinct prefix character and one root node. Counting the root, how many nodes does the trie have?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each distinct prefix of the stored keys gets one node, and the empty prefix is the root.

Step by step.

  1. The first character is i for all three keys, so one node for i.
  2. The second character is n for all three keys, so one node for in.
  3. From in, the keys continue with n for inn and t for int, so two more nodes.
  4. Nodes below the root: i, in, inn, int, which is 4.
  5. Adding the root gives 5.

Answer: 5.

Common trap. Counting 2 + 3 + 3 = 8 characters without merging the shared prefixes, or forgetting that in is itself a stored key and so needs an end marker at the node for in.

Takeaway. Count distinct prefixes, not characters. Overlapping keys shrink the trie quickly, which is why tries suit dictionaries with long shared stems.

DS-U5-Q50MCQeasy

Which structure answers a prefix query, listing every stored key that begins with a given string, in time proportional to the length of the prefix plus the number of keys reported, in the worst case?

  1. A hash table with chaining
  2. A balanced binary search tree
  3. A trie
  4. A sorted array with binary search
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A prefix query needs the structure to group keys by their leading characters. Only a structure that is organised along characters can walk to the prefix once and then enumerate the subtree beneath it.

Step by step.

  1. A hash table scatters keys by their full value, so keys sharing a prefix land in unrelated buckets and every key must be examined. It gives no bound of the required form.
  2. A balanced binary search tree can find one key starting with the prefix in O(log n) and then scan successors, but the cost includes the log n descent for the first key and it stores characters only implicitly through key comparisons.
  3. A trie walks the prefix in O(L) steps and then traverses the subtree whose nodes are exactly the matching keys, so the cost is O(L + number of keys reported).
  4. A sorted array needs a binary search plus a contiguous scan, and insertion into it is expensive, so it is not the intended answer for the query cost alone.

Answer: Option (C), a trie.

Common trap. Choosing the hash table because lookups are O(1). That bound is for an exact key match and collapses for prefix queries, which need ordering by character.

Takeaway. Match the structure to the query: exact match favours hashing, ordered and range queries favour balanced trees, and prefix queries favour tries.
