Heap order & min-max heaps · 6
DS-U6-Q01MCQeasy

A max-heap of n distinct keys is stored in an array A[1..n] with 1-based indexing, the children of A[i] being A[2i] and A[2i+1]. Which one of the following is guaranteed for every valid index?

  1. A[i] ≥ A[j] for every pair of indices with i < j
  2. The array is sorted in non-increasing order, so A[i] ≥ A[i+1] for every 1 ≤ i < n
  3. A[i] ≥ A[2i] and A[i] ≥ A[2i+1] whenever those children exist
  4. A[i] ≥ A[i+1] for every 1 ≤ i < n
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A max-heap is a complete binary tree in which every parent is at least as large as each of its children. Written in level order that is exactly the local constraint A[i] ≥ A[2i] and A[i] ≥ A[2i+1].

Step by step.

  1. The heap property is local: it relates a node only to its two children.
  2. It says nothing about siblings. For A = [9, 7, 8, 5, 6, 4, 3] the property holds, yet A[2] = 7 < A[3] = 8.
  3. It also says nothing about the array as a whole, so the array need not be sorted: in that same example A[2] = 7 < A[3] = 8 while the maximum 9 sits at A[1].
  4. Only the parent–child inequality survives for every index.

Answer: Option (C) — a node is at least as large as each of its children.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) The example above breaks it: 2 < 3 but A[2] = 7 < A[3] = 8.
  • (B) That is the sorted-array property, which is far stronger than heap order.
  • (D) The same example breaks it as well, since A[2] < A[3].

Common misconception. Treating a heap as a sorted array. A heap is only partially ordered, and that is precisely what makes it cheap to maintain.

Takeaway. A heap guarantees parent ≥ child and nothing else; the root is the maximum, the rest of the array is arbitrary.

DS-U6-Q02MCQeasy

A max-heap of 100 distinct keys is stored in A[1..100]. Which statement about the position of the smallest key is correct?

  1. It is always stored at A[100].
  2. It is stored at some index in the range 51–100, and each index in that range is possible.
  3. It must be stored at A[2] or A[3].
  4. It may be stored at any index from 1 to 100.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a 1-indexed array of n nodes, node i is a leaf exactly when 2i > n, that is when i > ⌊n/2⌋. Nodes 1..⌊n/2⌋ are internal.

Step by step.

  1. For n = 100, ⌊100/2⌋ = 50, so nodes 51–100 are exactly the leaves.
  2. An internal node has at least one child, and the heap property forces that child to be no larger than the parent. An internal node therefore always has a key smaller than itself somewhere below it, so it can never be the global minimum.
  3. The minimum must therefore be a leaf, that is at some index in 51–100.
  4. Every leaf position is attainable: place the smallest key at the leaf you want and fill the rest so that each parent dominates its subtree.

Answer: Option (B) — the smallest key is a leaf, at some index in 51–100.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) A[100] is a leaf, but the minimum may sit at any of the 50 leaves.
  • (C) A[2] and A[3] are internal for n = 100, so neither can hold the minimum; they are where the second largest key may sit.
  • (D) No internal node can hold the minimum.

Common misconception. Mirroring the maximum and expecting the minimum at a fixed cell. The maximum is pinned to the root by the heap property; the minimum is only known to be somewhere on the bottom level.

Takeaway. In a max-heap the maximum is A[1] and the minimum lies among the leaves ⌊n/2⌋+1 to n.

DS-U6-Q03MCQeasy

Which one of the following arrays of seven distinct keys, read as A[1..7], is a valid max-heap?

  1. 9, 8, 7, 6, 9, 4, 3
  2. 8, 9, 7, 6, 5, 4, 3
  3. 9, 6, 8, 7, 5, 4, 3
  4. 9, 7, 8, 5, 6, 4, 3
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. For A[1..7] the internal nodes are 1, 2 and 3, with children (2,3), (4,5), (6,7). Three inequalities decide everything: A[1] ≥ A[2], A[3]; A[2] ≥ A[4], A[5]; A[3] ≥ A[6], A[7].

Step by step.

  1. Check 9, 8, 7, 6, 9, 4, 3: A[2] = 8 but its child A[5] = 9, so the parent is smaller than a child. Invalid.
  2. Check 8, 9, 7, 6, 5, 4, 3: the root 8 is smaller than A[2] = 9. Invalid.
  3. Check 9, 6, 8, 7, 5, 4, 3: A[2] = 6 but its child A[4] = 7. Invalid.
  4. Check 9, 7, 8, 5, 6, 4, 3: 9 ≥ 7 and 9 ≥ 8; 7 ≥ 5 and 7 ≥ 6; 8 ≥ 4 and 8 ≥ 3. All six inequalities hold, so this is a max-heap.

Answer: Option (D) — 9, 7, 8, 5, 6, 4, 3 is the only valid max-heap.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) has a child larger than its parent: 9 sits below 8.
  • (B) has a root smaller than its left child.
  • (C) has 7 below 6 at index 4 below index 2.

Common misconception. Scanning the array from left to right and checking only consecutive pairs. Heap order is between a node and the nodes at double and double-plus-one, never between neighbours.

Takeaway. Test A[i] ≥ A[2i] and A[i] ≥ A[2i+1] for the internal nodes only; that is the whole definition.

DS-U6-Q04NATeasy

A min-heap stores n = 1000 keys in A[1..1000]. How many of its nodes have exactly one child?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Node i has exactly one child when 2i ≤ n but 2i + 1 > n, that is when 2i = n exactly.

Step by step.

  1. Node i has two children when 2i + 1 ≤ n, one child when 2i = n, and none when 2i > n.
  2. So a node with exactly one child exists only when n is even, and then only the single index i = n/2 qualifies.
  3. Here n = 1000 is even, so i = 500 has the left child A[1000] and no right child.
  4. Every other internal node (1 to 499) has two children, and nodes 501 to 1000 are leaves.

Answer: 1 node — the node at index 500.

Common misconception. Assuming a complete tree must have either 0 or 2 children everywhere. Only the last internal node can be short of a right child, and only when n is even.

Takeaway. In an n-node heap exactly one node has one child when n is even (index n/2), and none when n is odd.

DS-U6-Q05MSQmedium

Let H be a binary min-heap holding n ≥ 2 distinct keys in A[1..n]. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The second smallest key is stored at A[2] or A[3].
  2. Deleting the minimum and restoring the heap property takes O(log n) time.
  3. Deciding whether an arbitrary key k occurs in H takes O(log n) time in the worst case.
  4. Along every path from the root to a leaf the keys are non-decreasing.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Everything below the root is at least as large as the root, and the heap property applies along every parent–child edge, so it holds along every root-to-leaf path. There is no ordering between siblings, which is what limits search.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Every key other than the root is in one of the two subtrees of the root, so the smallest of the remaining keys is the smaller of A[2] and A[3]. True.
  2. (B) DELETE-MIN moves the last key to the root, shrinks the heap and sifts down one root-to-leaf path, which has length at most the height ⌊log₂ n⌋. True.
  3. (C) A heap offers no way to choose a subtree: both children may contain k or neither, so in the worst case every node is examined, which is Θ(n). False.
  4. (D) Each edge satisfies parent ≤ child, so the keys along any root-to-leaf path are non-decreasing. True.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D) are correct.

Why option (C) is not selected.

  • The heap order does not constrain the relative order of the two subtrees of a node, so a search for k cannot prune either side. Searching a heap is Θ(n) in the worst case, which is why a heap is not a search structure.

Common misconception. Confusing “logarithmic depth” with “logarithmic search”. A binary search tree prunes one subtree; a heap cannot.

Takeaway. A heap gives O(1) access to the extremum, O(log n) insertion and deletion, and Θ(n) arbitrary search.

DS-U6-Q06MCQhard

A min-max heap alternates min levels and max levels, the root being on level 0, a min level. Which one of the following is its defining invariant?

  1. Every node on a min level is ≤ every key in its own subtree, and every node on a max level is ≥ every key in its own subtree.
  2. Every node on a min level is ≤ its two children only, and every node on a max level is ≥ its two children only.
  3. The keys obey the ordinary heap order along every root-to-leaf path, exactly as in a binary min-heap.
  4. Insertion compares a new key only with its parent, exactly as in an ordinary binary heap.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A min-max heap keeps two orders at once. On a min level the node is the smallest key of its subtree; on a max level it is the largest key of its subtree. The guarantee is against the whole subtree, not merely against the immediate children.

Step by step.

  1. Levels alternate: level 0 min, level 1 max, level 2 min, and so on.
  2. The min-level guarantee is what makes the root the global minimum, and the max-level guarantee is what makes the maximum one of the two children of the root.
  3. Verifying against the grandchildren is enough in practice, because a child of a min-level node is on a max level and is already ≥ its own children.
  4. That is why insertion and deletion compare a new key with its parent and its grandparent, and the cost stays O(log n).

Answer: Option (A) — a min-level node dominates its whole subtree from below, a max-level node from above.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) Weakening the invariant to the two children only would allow a grandchild smaller than a min-level node, and the root would no longer be the minimum.
  • (C) That is the ordinary binary min-heap, which cannot give O(1) access to the maximum.
  • (D) A min-max heap must compare with the grandparent as well as the parent, which is exactly what the two-level order requires.

Common misconception. Assuming a min-max heap is just a min-heap that also happens to know the maximum. The subtree guarantee is the whole point: it bounds the maximum without a full scan.

Takeaway. Min-max heaps give O(1) access to both the minimum and the maximum and O(log n) insert, delete-min and delete-max.

Heapify & bottom-up build · 7
DS-U6-Q07MCQeasy

BUILD-MAX-HEAP is the bottom-up procedure that calls MAX-HEAPIFY on i = ⌊n/2⌋, ⌊n/2⌋ − 1, …, 1 for an array of n keys. Its tight running time on an arbitrary array is:

  1. Θ(n log n)
  2. Θ(log n)
  3. Θ(n²)
  4. Θ(n)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Bottom-up heap construction is linear, not n log n. Most nodes sit near the bottom of the tree and therefore sift down only a short distance.

Step by step.

  1. A node of height h can descend at most h levels, so its MAX-HEAPIFY costs O(h).
  2. There are at most ⌈n/2^(h+1)⌉ nodes of height h.
  3. Total work is Σₕ ⌈n/2^(h+1)⌉·O(h) = O(n·Σₕ h/2^h), and Σₕ h/2^h = 2, a constant.
  4. So the total is O(n), and since every key must be read at least once it is Θ(n).

Answer: Option (D) — Θ(n).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Θ(n log n) is the cost of inserting the keys one at a time, not of the bottom-up build.
  • (B) Θ(log n) is the cost of a single MAX-HEAPIFY call.
  • (C) No comparison-based heap build is quadratic.

Common misconception. Multiplying “about n/2 calls” by “O(log n) per call” and concluding n log n. The calls are not all deep: the deepest are the fewest.

Takeaway. Bottom-up build-heap is Θ(n); repeated insertion is Θ(n log n) in the worst case.

DS-U6-Q08NATmedium

BUILD-MAX-HEAP is run on A[1..7] = [10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 60, 70], calling MAX-HEAPIFY on i = 3, 2, 1 in that order. A call exchanges A[i] with its larger child whenever that child is larger, and then continues from that child. How many exchanges does the complete build perform?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. MAX-HEAPIFY on a node either stops immediately or performs exactly one exchange per level it descends. Counting exchanges therefore means counting the total number of levels descended by all three calls.

Step by step.

  1. MAX-HEAPIFY(3): A[3] = 30, children A[6] = 60 and A[7] = 70. The larger child is 70, so 30 and 70 are exchanged (1). Index 7 is a leaf, so the call ends. Array: 10, 20, 70, 40, 50, 60, 30.
  2. MAX-HEAPIFY(2): A[2] = 20, children A[4] = 40 and A[5] = 50. Exchange 20 with 50 (2). Index 5 is a leaf. Array: 10, 50, 70, 40, 20, 60, 30.
  3. MAX-HEAPIFY(1): A[1] = 10, children A[2] = 50 and A[3] = 70. Exchange 10 with 70 (3). At index 3 the children are A[6] = 60 and A[7] = 30; exchange 10 with 60 (4). Index 6 is a leaf.
  4. Total exchanges 1 + 1 + 2 = 4, and the final array is [70, 50, 60, 40, 20, 10, 30], which is a valid max-heap.

Answer: 4 exchanges.

Common misconception. Stopping after the first exchange at a node instead of continuing down the path, or forgetting that the key sifted to index 3 must be sifted again from there.

Takeaway. One exchange per level descended; the count is the sum of the descent depths, not the number of calls.

DS-U6-Q09MCQmedium

MAX-HEAPIFY(A, 2) is called on A = [16, 4, 10, 14, 7, 9, 3, 2, 8, 1]. Which array results?

  1. [16, 14, 10, 8, 7, 9, 3, 2, 4, 1]
  2. [16, 14, 10, 4, 7, 9, 3, 2, 8, 1]
  3. [16, 8, 10, 14, 7, 9, 3, 2, 4, 1]
  4. [16, 4, 10, 14, 8, 9, 3, 2, 7, 1]
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. MAX-HEAPIFY lets a key sink: at each step it is exchanged with the larger of its two children, and the process continues from that child until the key dominates both children or reaches a leaf.

Step by step.

  1. At i = 2 the key is 4, with children A[4] = 14 and A[5] = 7. The larger child is 14, so exchange: [16, 14, 10, 4, 7, 9, 3, 2, 8, 1].
  2. The key 4 is now at i = 4, with children A[8] = 2 and A[9] = 8. The larger child is 8, so exchange: [16, 14, 10, 8, 7, 9, 3, 2, 4, 1].
  3. The key 4 is now at i = 9, whose children A[18] and A[19] do not exist, so the call ends.
  4. Two exchanges were performed, and the subtree rooted at 2 is now a valid max-heap.

Answer: Option (A) — [16, 14, 10, 8, 7, 9, 3, 2, 4, 1].

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) stops after the first exchange and leaves 4 above 8, so the heap property still fails at index 4.
  • (C) exchanges with 8 first, but 8 is a grandchild and is never compared at index 2.
  • (D) moves 8 into the array while leaving 4 in place at index 2, which is not what sifting does.

Common misconception. Choosing the smaller child, or stopping as soon as the first exchange is made. MAX-HEAPIFY always picks the larger child, and it recurses.

Takeaway. A sift-down performs one exchange per level and must run to a leaf or to a point where the key already dominates its children.

DS-U6-Q10MSQmedium

Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. BUILD-MAX-HEAP runs in Θ(n) time, whereas inserting the n keys one at a time costs Ω(n log n) in the worst case.
  2. BUILD-MAX-HEAP makes exactly ⌊n/2⌋ calls to MAX-HEAPIFY.
  3. After BUILD-MAX-HEAP the largest key is at A[1] and the second largest is at A[2] or A[3].
  4. Building by successive insertion and building bottom-up always leave exactly the same array.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The bottom-up build calls MAX-HEAPIFY once on every internal node, in decreasing index order, so that each subtree is already a heap when its parent is processed.

Step by step.

  1. (A) The bottom-up build is Θ(n) by the height-sum argument. Successive insertion can force every new key to bubble up to the root, which is Ω(log n) per key and Ω(n log n) overall — attained, for instance, by inserting keys in increasing order into a max-heap. True.
  2. (B) Internal nodes are exactly 1..⌊n/2⌋, and each is passed to MAX-HEAPIFY once. True.
  3. (C) The heap property forces the root to be the maximum. Every other key lies in one of the two subtrees of the root, so the largest of them is the larger of A[2] and A[3]. True.
  4. (D) The two procedures are both correct but not equivalent: on [1, 2, 3, 4] bottom-up gives [4, 2, 3, 1] while inserting 1, 2, 3, 4 in turn gives [4, 3, 2, 1]. False.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C) are correct.

Why option (D) is not selected.

  • A heap is not unique for a given multiset of keys. The two construction orders can leave different valid heaps, so neither can be claimed to reproduce the other.

Common misconception. Believing the O(n log n) cost of insertion forces the build to be n log n, or assuming that a correct build must be unique.

Takeaway. Bottom-up build is linear and calls MAX-HEAPIFY once per internal node; the resulting heap is valid but not unique.

DS-U6-Q11NAThard

BUILD-MIN-HEAP is run on A[1..8] = [8, 7, 6, 5, 4, 3, 2, 1], calling MIN-HEAPIFY on i = 4, 3, 2, 1 in that order. A call exchanges A[i] with its smaller child whenever that child is smaller, and then continues from that child. How many exchanges does the complete build perform?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The descending array is the worst shape for a bottom-up build: nearly every internal node has to sift its key all the way to a leaf. Count one exchange per level descended.

Step by step.

  1. MIN-HEAPIFY(4): A[4] = 5, single child A[8] = 1. Exchange (1). Array [8, 7, 6, 1, 4, 3, 2, 5].
  2. MIN-HEAPIFY(3): A[3] = 6, children 3 and 2; smaller is 2 at index 7. Exchange (2). Index 7 is a leaf. Array [8, 7, 2, 1, 4, 3, 6, 5].
  3. MIN-HEAPIFY(2): A[2] = 7, children 1 and 4; smaller is 1 at index 4. Exchange (3). At index 4 the single child is A[8] = 5, smaller than 7, so exchange again (4). Array [8, 1, 2, 5, 4, 3, 6, 7].
  4. MIN-HEAPIFY(1): A[1] = 8, children 1 and 2; smaller is 1 at index 2. Exchange (5). At index 2 the children are 5 and 4; smaller is 4 at index 5, so exchange (6). Index 5 has no children. Final array [1, 4, 2, 5, 8, 3, 6, 7].

Answer: 6 exchanges.

Common misconception. Assuming a descending array needs no work because it already looks “reverse ordered”. In level order it is a heap with every parent larger than its children, which for a min-heap is the maximum amount of sifting.

Takeaway. Total exchanges = sum of descent depths. A reverse-sorted array maximises it; the total stays O(n) even so.

DS-U6-Q12MCQhard

In the linear-time analysis of BUILD-MAX-HEAP the work is summed over node heights as Σₕ₊₀ ⌈n/2^(h+1)⌉·O(h) = O(n·Σₕ h/2^h) = O(n). Which statement explains why this bound is linear rather than Θ(n log n)?

  1. Only O(log n) nodes are internal, so only O(log n) calls to MAX-HEAPIFY are ever made.
  2. Half of the nodes are leaves and do no work, a quarter descend at most one level, an eighth at most two, and so on, so Σₕ h/2^h converges to the constant 2.
  3. Each call to MAX-HEAPIFY costs O(1) because the array is a complete binary tree.
  4. MAX-HEAPIFY recurses at most twice at every node.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The cost of a build is dominated by where the nodes sit, not by how many there are. Nodes are numerous exactly where their height is small.

Step by step.

  1. A complete binary tree on n nodes has ⌈n/2⌋ leaves, each of height 0 and cost 0.
  2. It has ⌈n/4⌉ nodes of height 1 costing 1 each, ⌈n/8⌉ of height 2 costing 2 each, and in general ⌈n/2^(h+1)⌉ nodes of height h costing h each.
  3. Total work is n·Σₕ h/2^(h+1), and the series Σₕ h/2^h converges to 2, so the total is at most 2n.
  4. The few deep nodes cost log n each, but there are only a constant number of them, so their contribution is O(log n) — negligible against Θ(n).

Answer: Option (B) — the height distribution makes the sum converge to a constant.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) There are ⌊n/2⌋ internal nodes, not O(log n).
  • (C) A single MAX-HEAPIFY can cost O(log n); the bound is not O(1) per call.
  • (D) MAX-HEAPIFY makes one recursive call, not two, and its depth is bounded by the height.

Common misconception. Summing the maximum cost of each call instead of weighting by how many calls have that cost. Multiplying “n/2 calls” by “log n each” ignores that only a handful of calls are deep.

Takeaway. Cost is bounded by the number of nodes at each height times that height, and that weighted sum is linear.

DS-U6-Q13MCQmedium

A max-heap holds 500 keys. DELETE-MAX replaces the root by the last key, shrinks the heap by one and calls MAX-HEAPIFY on the root. What is the largest number of exchanges a single DELETE-MAX can perform on this heap?

  1. 7
  2. 9
  3. 8
  4. 499
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The key moved to the root sifts down one root-to-leaf path, so the number of exchanges is bounded by the height of the tree, which is ⌊log₂ n⌋ for n nodes.

Step by step.

  1. For n = 500, 2⁸ = 256 ≤ 500 < 512 = 2⁹, so the height is 8: levels 0 to 8.
  2. After the deletion the heap holds 499 keys, and 2⁸ = 256 ≤ 499 < 512, so the height is still 8.
  3. The moved key can therefore descend at most 8 edges, performing 8 exchanges.
  4. Eight exchanges are attainable: take distinct keys and make the moved key smaller than every key on one root-to-leaf path of length 8.

Answer: Option (C) — 8 exchanges.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 7 is the height of a heap with at most 255 keys.
  • (B) 9 would need at least 512 keys.
  • (D) 499 is the number of keys, not the number of levels on a path.

Common misconception. Reading “n = 500” as a height of 500, or forgetting that the heap shrinks by one before the sift-down starts.

Takeaway. DELETE-MAX costs Θ(log n): one move plus at most ⌊log₂ n⌋ exchanges.

Heap-sort · 6
DS-U6-Q14MCQeasy

HEAPSORT is implemented in place: it builds a max-heap and then repeatedly swaps A[1] with the last unsorted slot. Its worst-case running time and its auxiliary space are:

  1. Θ(n log n) time and Θ(n) extra space
  2. Θ(n²) time and Θ(1) extra space
  3. Θ(n log n) time and Θ(1) extra space
  4. Θ(n) time and Θ(n) extra space
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. HEAPSORT is the only common sorting algorithm that is simultaneously Θ(n log n) in the worst case and Θ(1) in extra space.

Step by step.

  1. The build costs Θ(n).
  2. There are n − 1 extraction steps; each swaps the root into place and sifts the new root down, costing O(log n).
  3. Total Θ(n) + (n − 1)·O(log n) = Θ(n log n) in the best, average and worst case alike.
  4. Nothing beyond a few index variables is allocated, so the extra space is Θ(1).

Answer: Option (C) — Θ(n log n) time, Θ(1) extra space.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Θ(n) extra space describes merge sort, not heapsort.
  • (B) Θ(n²) describes quicksort's worst case or insertion sort, not heapsort.
  • (D) No comparison sort of n arbitrary keys can run in Θ(n) time.

Common misconception. Assuming an in-place algorithm must be quadratic, as quicksort's worst case is. Heapsort gets both guarantees at once, which is why it is used when memory is tight and the input may be adversarial.

Takeaway. Heapsort: Θ(n log n) worst case, Θ(1) space, not stable.

DS-U6-Q15NATmedium

HEAPSORT begins by running BUILD-MAX-HEAP on A[1..9] = [5, 13, 2, 25, 7, 17, 20, 8, 4]. Immediately after the build and before any extraction, what key is stored at A[3]?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The build calls MAX-HEAPIFY on i = 4, 3, 2, 1 in that order. Only after all four calls is the array a max-heap, and the question asks about that moment.

Step by step.

  1. MAX-HEAPIFY(4): A[4] = 25 with child A[8] = 8. 25 already dominates, so no exchange.
  2. MAX-HEAPIFY(3): A[3] = 2 with children A[6] = 17 and A[7] = 20. Exchange 2 with 20: array becomes [5, 13, 20, 25, 7, 17, 2, 8, 4].
  3. MAX-HEAPIFY(2): A[2] = 13 with children 25 and 7. Exchange with 25: [5, 25, 20, 13, 7, 17, 2, 8, 4].
  4. MAX-HEAPIFY(1): A[1] = 5 with children 25 and 20. Exchange with 25 (index 2). At index 2 the children are 13 and 7, so exchange 5 with 13. Final array [25, 13, 20, 8, 7, 17, 2, 5, 4], in which A[3] = 20.

Answer: 20.

Common misconception. Reading A[3] from the input array (where it is 2), or stopping the build early and assuming the largest key at the root is all that matters.

Takeaway. The build must be traced to the end; the value at any interior index depends on every sift-down that passed through it.

DS-U6-Q16MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about HEAPSORT are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. It is not a stable sorting algorithm.
  2. It sorts in place, using Θ(1) auxiliary space beyond the array itself.
  3. Building a max-heap and swapping the root into the last unsorted slot produces ascending order.
  4. Its worst-case running time is asymptotically worse than merge sort's worst case.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A max-heap keeps the largest remaining key at the root. Moving that root to the end of the unsorted prefix, then restoring the heap on the prefix, fills the array from the back with ever smaller keys.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Sift-down moves keys over long distances, so equal keys are freely reordered. False for stability, so the statement that it is unstable is true.
  2. (B) The only extra storage is a constant number of index variables. True.
  3. (C) The largest key goes to position n, the next largest to n − 1, and so on, so the final array is non-decreasing. True.
  4. (D) Merge sort's worst case is Θ(n log n) and so is heapsort's; neither is asymptotically worse. False.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C) are correct.

Why option (D) is not selected.

  • Both algorithms are Θ(n log n) in the worst case. Heapsort is slower in practice because of cache behaviour and a larger constant factor, but that is not an asymptotic difference.

Common misconception. Confusing “in place” with “stable”, or thinking heapsort has a quadratic worst case like quicksort.

Takeaway. Heapsort: Θ(n log n) worst case, Θ(1) space, unstable, and ascending output from a max-heap.

DS-U6-Q17MCQeasy

HEAPSORT is rewritten to build a min-heap and, at every step, to swap A[1] with the last unsorted slot of the same array. The array it finally produces is:

  1. ascending
  2. identical to the input array
  3. ascending only if all keys are distinct
  4. descending
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In place, the extracted extremum is parked at the back of the shrinking unsorted prefix. A min-heap extracts the smallest key first, so the smallest keys are written into the last slots first.

Step by step.

  1. Step 1 puts the minimum at position n.
  2. Step 2 puts the second smallest at position n − 1.
  3. Continuing, the smallest key ends at the last index and the largest at index 1, which is descending order.
  4. To obtain ascending order in place, the heap must be a max-heap; a min-heap gives ascending order only when it is extracted into a separate output array.

Answer: Option (D) — descending.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Ascending order needs a max-heap when the extraction target is the array's tail.
  • (B) The array is a heap at every stage and is sorted at the end, so it cannot equal an arbitrary input.
  • (C) Distinctness is irrelevant; the direction is set by which extremum the heap exposes.

Common misconception. Assuming the heap type does not matter because both heaps “sort”. What matters is where the extracted key is written: into the array's tail, or into a separate output.

Takeaway. In place, a max-heap yields ascending order and a min-heap yields descending order; out of place, the reverse.

DS-U6-Q18MCQmedium

HEAPSORT and merge sort are both Θ(n log n) in the worst case, yet heapsort is usually the slower of the two on real hardware. The main reason is:

  1. Sift-down jumps between positions far apart in the array, so heapsort suffers many more cache misses.
  2. Heapsort is not in place and must allocate an auxiliary array.
  3. Heapsort's worst case is Θ(n²).
  4. Heapsort cannot handle arrays that contain duplicate keys.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Asymptotic bounds count comparisons and exchanges, not memory accesses. On a real machine the constant factor is dominated by whether those accesses hit the cache.

Step by step.

  1. The children of index i are at 2i and 2i + 1, so a sift-down from the root touches positions n/2 apart in a single step.
  2. Each step of the descent therefore lands in a different cache line, and the first pass over a large array is essentially a random-access walk.
  3. Merge sort, by contrast, reads and writes contiguous runs, which the prefetcher handles well; quicksort's partitioning is also mostly sequential.
  4. That locality penalty, not the comparison count, is why heapsort loses in practice while remaining the better guarantee.

Answer: Option (A) — poor locality of the sift-down accesses.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) Heapsort is the classic in-place Θ(n log n) sort; merge sort is the one that needs auxiliary space.
  • (C) Heapsort's worst case is Θ(n log n).
  • (D) Duplicate keys are handled correctly; heapsort is merely unstable.

Common misconception. Reading “same asymptotic bound” as “same speed”. Constants and memory behaviour decide the practical ordering.

Takeaway. Heapsort's advantage is the worst-case guarantee and Θ(1) space; its disadvantage is cache behaviour.

DS-U6-Q19MCQhard

In the standard in-place HEAPSORT, MAX-HEAPIFY is called once for each internal node during the build, and once after each of the n − 1 extraction swaps. What is the total number of MAX-HEAPIFY calls made by one complete run on n keys?

  1. n − 1
  2. n log n
  3. ⌊n/2⌋ + n
  4. ⌊n/2⌋ + n − 1
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Count the two phases separately. The build touches every internal node once; the extraction phase touches the root once per extraction.

Step by step.

  1. Phase 1, the build: MAX-HEAPIFY is invoked on indices ⌊n/2⌋, …, 1, which is ⌊n/2⌋ calls.
  2. Phase 2, extraction: for k = n down to 2, the root is swapped with position k and MAX-HEAPIFY is called on the root of the shrunken heap. That is n − 1 calls.
  3. The final extraction leaves a single element, which needs no call.
  4. Total ⌊n/2⌋ + (n − 1) calls.

Answer: Option (D) — ⌊n/2⌋ + n − 1 calls.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) n − 1 counts only the extraction phase.
  • (B) n log n is a bound on the work, not on the number of calls.
  • (C) ⌊n/2⌋ + n counts one extraction too many: the last one leaves a single key and needs no sift-down.

Common misconception. Counting the calls as if the build called MAX-HEAPIFY on all n nodes. Leaves are already valid one-element heaps and are never passed to MAX-HEAPIFY.

Takeaway. Build: ⌊n/2⌋ calls. Extraction: n − 1 calls. The total is ⌊n/2⌋ + n − 1.

Priority queues & their applications · 6
DS-U6-Q20MCQeasy

A priority queue on n items must support INSERT and EXTRACT-MIN. Which representation gives the best worst-case bound for both operations together?

  1. An unsorted array
  2. A sorted array
  3. A binary heap
  4. A singly linked list kept in insertion order
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The right structure is the one that keeps both operations logarithmic. Insertion into a heap costs O(log n) and extraction costs O(log n).

Step by step.

  1. Unsorted array: INSERT is O(1) at the end, but EXTRACT-MIN needs a full scan, Θ(n).
  2. Sorted array: EXTRACT-MIN is O(1) from one end, but INSERT must shift elements, Θ(n).
  3. Binary heap: INSERT bubbles a key up one root-to-leaf path, O(log n); EXTRACT-MIN moves the last key to the root and sifts down, O(log n).
  4. Linked list in insertion order: EXTRACT-MIN is Θ(n).

Answer: Option (C) — a binary heap.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (D) are linear in extraction.
  • (B) is linear in insertion.
  • Each of the three loses one of the two operations; only the heap keeps both logarithmic.

Common misconception. Optimising one operation in isolation. A priority queue is judged on the pair, and the workload usually mixes both.

Takeaway. Binary heap: O(log n) insert and O(log n) extract-min, with O(1) peek.

DS-U6-Q21MSQeasy

Which of the following are standard uses of a priority queue? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Huffman's algorithm, repeatedly merging the two least frequent symbols.
  2. Prim's algorithm, repeatedly taking the cheapest edge leaving the current tree.
  3. Binary search for a key in a sorted array.
  4. Dijkstra's algorithm on a graph with non-negative edge weights.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A priority queue is the right tool whenever the algorithm repeatedly needs the current extremum of a changing set.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Huffman's algorithm extracts the two smallest weights, merges them and inserts the sum: two extract-mins and one insert per merge. True.
  2. (B) Prim's algorithm needs the minimum-weight crossing edge, which is exactly a decrease-key and extract-min on the frontier. True.
  3. (C) Binary search indexes the middle of a sorted array directly; it needs no ordering structure at all. False.
  4. (D) Dijkstra's algorithm repeatedly extracts the unsettled vertex of least tentative distance, which is a priority-queue operation. True.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D) are correct.

Why option (C) is not selected.

  • Binary search relies on the array already being sorted and on random access by index. It never asks for a minimum of a dynamic set, so a priority queue would only add overhead.

Common misconception. Equating “needs ordering” with “needs a priority queue”. A priority queue is for a set whose minimum changes as elements arrive and leave.

Takeaway. Greedy algorithms that repeatedly take the best available item — Huffman, Prim, Dijkstra, A* — are the natural home of the priority queue.

DS-U6-Q22MCQmedium

Dijkstra's algorithm is implemented with a binary heap and without a decrease-key operation: when a distance improves, a duplicate entry is pushed instead. Its running time is:

  1. O((V + E) log V)
  2. O(V²)
  3. O(V·E)
  4. O(V log E)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. With a binary heap the algorithm performs at most one insertion per edge relaxation and one extraction per heap entry, and each costs O(log V).

Step by step.

  1. Each of the E edges can cause at most one successful relaxation, so at most E entries are pushed in addition to the V initial ones.
  2. The heap therefore holds O(V + E) entries, and each push or pop costs O(log(V + E)) = O(log V) because E ≤ V² and log V² = 2 log V.
  3. Total O((V + E) log V).
  4. With decrease-key the same bound holds with fewer entries; lazy deletion only changes the constant.

Answer: Option (A) — O((V + E) log V).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) O(V²) is the bound for the unsorted-array or adjacency-matrix implementation.
  • (C) O(V·E) is the bound for Bellman-Ford, which tolerates negative edges.
  • (D) O(V log E) does not account for the E relaxations.

Common misconception. Thinking that skipping decrease-key makes the algorithm asymptotically worse. It only adds duplicate entries, and each is charged to the edge that created it.

Takeaway. Dijkstra with a binary heap is O((V + E) log V), with or without decrease-key.

DS-U6-Q23NATeasy

Huffman coding is applied to six symbols whose frequencies are 45, 13, 12, 16, 9 and 5. What is the total number of bits in the encoded file, that is the sum over symbols of frequency × code length?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The total length of a Huffman-coded file equals the weighted external path length of the Huffman tree: the sum, over every merge, of the weight of the merged node.

Step by step.

  1. Start with the six leaves 45, 16, 13, 12, 9, 5.
  2. Merge 9 and 5 into 14 (weight 14). Remaining: 45, 16, 14, 13, 12.
  3. Merge 13 and 12 into 25. Remaining: 45, 25, 16, 14.
  4. Merge 16 and 14 into 30. Remaining: 45, 30, 25.
  5. Merge 30 and 25 into 55. Remaining: 55, 45.
  6. Merge 55 and 45 into 100. The total is 14 + 25 + 30 + 55 + 100 = 224 bits, an average of 2.24 bits per symbol against 3 bits for a fixed-length code.

Answer: 224 bits.

Common misconception. Summing the code lengths of the six symbols instead of weighting each by its frequency, or forgetting that the total equals the sum of the merge weights.

Takeaway. Total Huffman cost = sum of all merge weights = weighted external path length.

DS-U6-Q24MSQmedium

A stream of n numbers must be processed so that the 100 largest can be reported, using a heap of fixed capacity. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A min-heap of capacity 100 is the right structure: its root is the smallest of the current 100 largest and is the key to evict.
  2. A max-heap of capacity 100 works equally well.
  3. Each arriving element costs O(log 100) time.
  4. The total cost is O(n log 100), which is O(n) when the capacity is fixed.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. To keep the k largest elements you must be able to identify, and discard, the smallest of the k you are holding. That requires the root to be the minimum, hence a min-heap.

Step by step.

  1. (A) With a min-heap of capacity 100 the root is the weakest of the current top 100, so comparing each new element with the root and replacing it when the new element is larger keeps exactly the top 100. True.
  2. (B) A max-heap of capacity 100 exposes the largest of the stored elements, which is the last thing you would ever evict. False.
  3. (C) One comparison against the root, and at most one replacement followed by a sift-down of depth log₂ 100 ≈ 7. True.
  4. (D) n elements at O(log k) each gives O(n log k), and with k fixed at 100 this is O(n). True.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D) are correct.

Why option (B) is not selected.

  • The heap must expose the element you want to throw away, not the one you want to keep. Keeping the k largest therefore needs a min-heap of size k, just as keeping the k smallest needs a max-heap.

Common misconception. Reaching for a max-heap because the task says “largest”. The heap is chosen by which element must be evictable.

Takeaway. Top-k largest: min-heap of size k, O(n log k) time and O(k) space.

DS-U6-Q25MCQhard

M sorted lists holding n elements in total are merged into one sorted list using a heap that holds one current element from each list. The running time is:

  1. O(n·M)
  2. O(n log M)
  3. O(n log n)
  4. O(M log n)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. At any moment the next output element is the smallest of the M current heads, so a heap of size M answers each step in O(log M).

Step by step.

  1. Initialise the heap with the first element of each non-empty list: O(M) time.
  2. Repeat n times: extract the minimum, append it to the output, and insert the next element of the list it came from.
  3. Each of the n steps costs one extract-min and at most one insert, each O(log M).
  4. Total O(n log M), which for M = 2 collapses to the linear merge of merge sort.

Answer: Option (B) — O(n log M).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) O(n·M) is the cost of scanning all M heads at every step.
  • (C) O(n log n) would be right only if every element had to be compared with every other, which the heap avoids; it is also worse than O(n log M) whenever M ≪ n.
  • (D) O(M log n) has the roles of n and M interchanged.

Common misconception. Sorting the concatenation instead of merging. Re-sorting costs O(n log n), which ignores that the M lists are already sorted.

Takeaway. k-way merge with a heap is O(n log k), the engine behind external sorting and multiway merge.

Unit 7 has no banked questions yet.

What this test contains

25 questions on Unit 6 (Heaps & Priority Queues), Set 2 of 2. All four subtopics are covered: heap order and min-max heaps — which indices are leaves, where the second-largest and the second-smallest keys can lie, the last index with two children, counting the distinct max-heaps on six keys, and the subtree guarantees of a min-max heap on each level; heapify and the bottom-up build — the last internal node, the single subtree MAX-HEAPIFY recurses into, two sift-down traces on given arrays, the exchange count of a build on an ascending array, and the statements that make the build linear; heap-sort — its instability, the number of extraction exchanges, two extraction traces, the Θ(n log n) accounting, and how many inputs need no exchange at all; and priority queues — the linear cost of searching a heap by value, constant-time access to the minimum, two Huffman runs, and the Θ trade-off between arrays, heaps, Fibonacci heaps and balanced trees. Composition: 15 MCQ, 5 MSQ, 5 NAT; difficulty 10 easy, 10 medium, 5 hard; 60 minutes, 35 marks. MCQ carries 1 mark, MSQ and NAT carry 2 marks, there is no negative marking, and an MSQ scores only when the exact set of correct options is selected. This is a practice marking scheme, not the official GATE scheme.

Concept map used to build the questions

  • Shape first. A heap is a complete binary tree in level order, so the tree is fixed by n alone: children at 2i and 2i+1, parent at ⌊i/2⌋, internal nodes 1..⌊n/2⌋, leaves ⌊n/2⌋+1..n, and exactly one one-child node when n is even. Most index questions are settled before any key is compared.
  • Order, not sorting. The heap invariant relates a node only to its descendants, so the root is the extreme and the second extreme is a child of the root — but neighbours are unrelated and a value search is linear. Counting distinct heaps, n!/∏size(v), makes the same point quantitatively.
  • Two levels of order. A min-max heap alternates min and max levels; the guarantee is always about the subtree, so the maximum is not confined to the root’s children, the second minimum sits at a child or grandchild, and insertion compares with the grandparent as well as the parent.
  • One path at a time. MAX-HEAPIFY recurses into exactly the subtree that received the displaced key, so it costs O(log n); the bottom-up build repairs nodes in decreasing index order, so both subtrees are already heaps and the height-weighted sum ∑ h·n/2^(h+1) gives O(n).
  • Extraction is a swap plus a sift. HEAPSORT parks the root at the end of the array and shrinks the heap; the number of exchanges, the array after one extraction and the instability of the sort all follow from that single move.
  • Choosing the structure. A binary heap gives O(1) peek and O(log n) insert, extract and decrease-key; a sorted array trades the other way; a Fibonacci heap buys amortized constant decrease-key; Huffman and top-k are the two standard applications where the heap is the whole algorithm.

Source categories

Each question carries a data-source tag. STANDARD_TEXTBOOK marks questions built on the standard treatment of heaps and priority queues (Cormen–Leiserson–Rivest–Stein, Horowitz–Sahni–Rajasekaran, Weiss), written in original wording. ORIGINAL_SYNTHESIS marks independently designed GATE-style practice, including every instance whose numbers must be computed rather than recalled. GATE_PYQ_VARIATION would mark a question built from a verified previous-year question; this set contains none, because no heaps or priority-queue previous-year question has been verified against an official paper and answer key for this run. No question on this paper is presented as an official GATE question.

Provenance: no question here is presented as an official GATE question unless its year, paper and official answer key are recorded in the Question Bank tab. All other items are practice questions written for this series.
